02
How to Read This Document
A layered map rather than a conventional lesson-by-lesson note set
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 2
DOCUMENT MODEL
JAVASCRIPT FUNDAMENTALS
|
+-- SESSION 1 -> values, names, types and input/output
|
+-- SESSION 2 -> operations, comparisons and decisions
|
+-- SESSION 3 -> reusable math tools, data expansion and branching
Cross-session idea:
data -> operator -> condition -> action
Design principle
Each major concept is isolated into its own visual block. ASCII trees are used for
structure; short prose is used only to explain what the tree means.
Source boundary
This document summarizes the concepts actually present across Sessions 1-3. Topics shown
in the sample reference but not taught in the supplied sessions, such as Symbol, hoisting
and the Temporal Dead Zone, are intentionally not added as session content.

04
Session 1 - Variables
How JavaScript introduces named state
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 4
SESSION 1 ASCII MAP
SESSION 1
|
+-- VARIABLES
|   +-- var
|   +-- let
|   +-- const
|
+-- VARIABLE LIFECYCLE
|   +-- declaration
|   +-- initialization
|   +-- declaration + initialization
|   +-- redeclaration
|   +-- reassignment
|
+-- CORE RULES
    +-- var -> redeclaration allowed; reassignment allowed
    +-- let -> reassignment allowed; same-scope redeclaration not allowed
    +-- const -> initialize at declaration; reassignment not allowed
Mental model
Session 1 begins with state: a program needs named values, then needs clear rules for how
those values are created and changed.


05
Session 1 - Data Types
Classifying the values a program works with
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 5
SESSION 1 ASCII MAP
DATA TYPES
|
+-- PRIMITIVE
|   +-- String -> textual data
|   +-- Number -> integer / floating-point value
|   +-- Boolean -> true / false
|   +-- Undefined -> declared, no assigned value
|   +-- Null -> intentional absence of value
|   +-- BigInt -> very large integers with n suffix
|
+-- NON-PRIMITIVE / REFERENCE
|   +-- Array -> ordered collection
    +-- Object -> key/value properties
OBSERVATION
+-- arrays and objects are the structured values introduced in Session 1
Important distinction
The session teaches a useful beginner classification: primitive values versus
object/reference types. It does not attempt to reduce all JavaScript memory behavior to
one slogan.
Key examples
String -> "Hello" | Number -> 56 or 12.43 | Boolean -> true | BigInt -> 123n | Array -> [1,2,3]
| Object -> { id: 4 }


06
Session 1 - Input and Output Pipeline
Turning user input into a typed result
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 6
SESSION 1 ASCII FLOW
USER INPUT / OUTPUT FLOW
|
+-- prompt("Enter value")
|     |
|     +-- browser input is normally text
|
+-- Number(prompt(...))
|     |
|     +-- converts numeric text into a number
|
+-- arithmetic / operator work
|
+-- console.log(...)
|     |
|     +-- display the result
|
+-- typeof value
      |
      +-- inspect the resulting type
Canonical example
Two values are collected with prompt(), converted with Number(), added, and checked with
typeof. This creates the first bridge between input, type conversion and computation.
Session 1 outcome
By the end of the session, the student can create values, classify values, take simple
input, convert it, perform basic arithmetic and inspect results.


07
Session 2 - Operator Architecture
How values become expressions and results
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 7
SESSION 2 ASCII MAP
SESSION 2: OPERATORS & LOGIC
|
+-- ARITHMETIC
|   +-- binary: + - * / % **
|   +-- unary mutation: ++ --
|
+-- ASSIGNMENT
|   +-- = += -= *= /=
|
+-- COMPARISON
|   +-- equality: == ===
|   +-- inequality: != !==
|   +-- relational: > < >= <=
|
+-- LOGICAL
|   +-- AND: &&
|   +-- OR: ||
|   +-- NOT: !
|   +-- short-circuit evaluation
|
+-- TERNARY
    +-- condition ? trueResult : falseResult
Core idea
Session 2 is about transforming values with operators and then using the results of those
expressions to reason about program state.


08
Session 2 - Arithmetic and Assignment
Mutating numeric state with concise expressions
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 8
SESSION 2 ASCII MAP
ARITHMETIC + ASSIGNMENT
|
+-- a + b -> addition
+-- a - b -> subtraction
+-- a * b -> multiplication
+-- a / b -> division
+-- a % b -> remainder
+-- a ** b -> exponentiation
|
+-- ++x / --x -> change first, then use
+-- x++ / x-- -> use first, then change
|
+-- x += y -> x = x + y
+-- x -= y -> x = x - y
+-- x *= y -> x = x * y
+-- x /= y -> x = x / y
Trace mindset
Pre/post operators require tracking two things separately: the value produced by the
expression and the variable value after the operator executes.
Example
let x = 3; console.log(++x, x++); -> first expression produces 4 and leaves x at 4; second
produces 4 and leaves x at 5.


09
Session 2 - Comparison and Logic
Turning values into Boolean decisions
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 9
SESSION 2 ASCII MAP
COMPARISON -> LOGICAL DECISION
|
+-- equality
|   +-- 22 == "22" -> true (loose comparison)
|   +-- 22 === "22" -> false (strict comparison)
|
+-- relational
|   +-- > < >= <=
|
+-- inequality
|   +-- != / !==
|
+-- logical combination
    +-- A && B -> both conditions
    +-- A || B -> at least one condition
    +-- !A -> reversed Boolean result
DECISION RESULT
+-- true
+-- false
Type note
The source explicitly contrasts loose equality with strict equality and shows how logical
operators combine Boolean conditions.
Short-circuit idea
For &&, a false result can make the final result known before the right side is needed.
For ||, a true result can make the final result known early.


10
Session 2 - Ternary and Expression Thinking
Condensing a two-way decision into an expression
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 10
SESSION 2 ASCII MAP
TERNARY OPERATOR
|
+-- condition
|
+-- ? trueResult
|
+-- : falseResult
MENTAL MODEL
condition ? valueIfTrue : valueIfFalse
EXAMPLE
age >= 18 ? "You can Vote" : "Not eligible"
RELATION TO SESSION 3
+-- Session 2 -> ternary expresses a compact two-way decision
+-- Session 3 -> if / else if / else handles multi-branch control flow
Expression mindset
A ternary expression evaluates to a value. That makes it useful when a program needs to
choose one of two values directly.
Bridge forward
Session 3 builds on this decision-making idea with full conditional control flow and
validation branches.


11
Session 3 - Math Object
Reusable built-in mathematical utilities
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 11
SESSION 3 ASCII MAP
SESSION 3: MATH OBJECT
|
+-- Math.random()
|   +-- pseudo-random floating-point value in [0,1)
|
+-- Math.floor()
|   +-- rounds downward
|   +-- used with random() for 0..9 integers
|
+-- Math.sqrt(x)
|   +-- square root
|
+-- Math.pow(base, exponent)
|   +-- power calculation
|   +-- source notes equivalence with base ** exponent
|
+-- Math.min(...)
|   +-- smallest argument
|
+-- Math.max(...)
    +-- largest argument
Mental model
Math is presented as a built-in toolbox. It does not create values by itself; it provides
reusable methods that operate on numeric inputs.


12
Session 3 - Spread Operator
Expanding an array into individual arguments
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 12
SESSION 3 ASCII MAP
SPREAD WITH FUNCTIONS
|
+-- array
|   +-- [10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4]
|
+-- Math.max(array)   -> not the same as many arguments
|
+-- Math.max(...array)
    |
    +-- 10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4
        become individual arguments
CONCEPT
...array -> expand iterable elements into the function call
Why this matters
Session 1 introduced arrays as ordered collections. Session 3 reuses that array knowledge
and connects it to function argument requirements through the spread operator.
Cross-session connection
Array -> spread -> Math.max / Math.min -> numeric result. This is one of the clearest
examples of a later session building directly on an earlier data type.


13
Session 3 - Conditional Control Flow
From single decisions to multi-branch program flow
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 13
SESSION 3 ASCII MAP
CONDITIONAL CONTROL FLOW
|
+-- if (condition)
|      |
|      +-- run branch when condition is true
|
+-- else if (nextCondition)
|      |
|      +-- test another branch
|
+-- else
       |
       +-- fallback branch
VALIDATION PATTERN
+-- reject invalid input first
+-- evaluate valid range / rule
+-- select output branch
Control-flow idea
The ternary operator in Session 2 handles a compact two-way choice. Session 3 introduces
a full branching structure for multiple conditions, validation and workflow rules.


14
Sessions 1-3 - Control Flow Evolution
How decision-making becomes more expressive across the sessions
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 14
CROSS-SESSION ASCII MAP
CONTROL FLOW EVOLUTION
|
+-- SESSION 1
|   +-- values + input + output
|       |
|       +-- create the data that decisions will use
|
+-- SESSION 2
|   +-- comparison -> Boolean result
|   +-- logical operators -> combine conditions
|   +-- ternary -> compact two-way decision
|
+-- SESSION 3
    +-- if / else if / else
    +-- validation rules
    +-- nested branches
    +-- workflow simulation
Key progression
The sessions move from representing information, to manipulating information, to making
program decisions from that information.
Practical formula
value -> operator -> condition -> branch -> output

16
Sessions 1-3 - Concept Dependencies
Which earlier concepts are reused by later concepts
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 16
REUSE MAP
DEPENDENCY MAP
|
Session 1: ARRAY
|
+-- Session 3: spread (...array)
    |
    +-- Math.max(...array)
    +-- Math.min(...array)
Session 1: NUMBER + %
|
+-- Session 2: arithmetic / comparison
|
+-- Session 3: divisibility practice
Session 2: && / || / === / %

+-- Session 3: validation, access and grading
Session 2: ternary
|
+-- Session 3: ticket type / compact choices
Pattern to notice
Later exercises are not isolated tricks. They combine earlier concepts: numbers + %,
comparisons + &&, arrays + spread, and conditions + logical expressions.

Cross-Session Logic Patterns
Reusable problem-solving templates from Sessions 1-3
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 18
PROBLEM-SOLVING ASCII MAP
REUSABLE LOGIC TEMPLATES
|
+-- RANGE CHECK
|   value >= min && value <= max
|
+-- MULTI-CONDITION
|   conditionA && conditionB
|
+-- ALTERNATIVE CONDITION
|   conditionA || conditionB
|
+-- DIVISIBILITY
|   value % divisor === 0
|
+-- SIGN CHECK
|   value > 0 / value < 0 / otherwise zero
|
+-- CATEGORY CHAIN
|   if -> else if -> else
|
+-- WORKFLOW GATE
|   validate -> authenticate -> authorize -> act
|
+-- ARRAY EXTREMA
    Math.max(...array) / Math.min(...array)
Session connection
These templates are not separate topics: each one combines concepts from more than one
session. They are the bridge from syntax practice to applied programming logic.

20
Quick Revision - Session 1
The essentials to recall before moving into operators
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 20
SESSION 1 CHEAT SHEET
SESSION 1 QUICK MAP
var   -> redeclare + reassign
let   -> reassign
const -> initialize immediately; no reassignment
declaration -> create the variable name
initialization -> give the first value
redeclaration -> declare the same name again
reassignment -> change the current value
Primitive: String / Number / Boolean / Undefined / Null / BigInt
Structured: Array / Object
prompt() -> input text
Number() -> numeric conversion
console.log() -> output
typeof -> inspect type
Session 1 in one sentence
Create values, understand their types, store them with variables, accept input, convert
it when necessary, and inspect or print the result.


21
Quick Revision - Session 2
The operator toolkit
JavaScript Fundamentals | Sessions 01-03
Page 21
SESSION 2 CHEAT SHEET
SESSION 2 QUICK MAP
Arithmetic: + - * / % **
Mutation: ++ --
Assignment: = += -= *= /= 
Comparison: == === != !== > < >= <=
Logical: && || !
Ternary: condition ? a : b
Pre -> change first, then use
Post -> use first, then change
==  -> loose equality with possible coercion
=== -> strict equality without type conversion
&& -> all required
|| -> at least one
!  -> reverse Boolean result
Session 2 in one sentence
Turn values into expressions, transform numbers, compare values, combine conditions, and
choose between outcomes.

Math.min()    -> minimum argument
Math.max()    -> maximum argument

Session 3 in one sentence
Use built-in math utilities, expand array values when a function expects separate

Three-session foundation
Session 1 teaches what the data is. Session 2 teaches what can be done with the data.
Session 3 teaches how reusable tools and control flow can turn those operations into
program behavior.

03
 Session Roadmap
from symbols to problem solving
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 3
Return to Index
BIG PICTURE
Session 1 taught us how to store values. Session 2 is about what we can DO with
those values: calculate, compare, combine conditions and choose a result.
01
Operators
symbols / keywords that perform work
02
Operands
the values being worked on
03
Expressions
code that evaluates to a value
04
Comparisons
turn values into true/false checks
05
Logic
combine conditions
06
Ternary
pick one of two values quickly
07
Practice
turn the rules into small programs
MY RULE FOR THIS SESSION
Study trick: every time an operator appears, ask two questions: "What value
comes in?" and "What value comes out?" That habit makes ++, == and && much
easier to trace.


04
 Operator, Operand, Expression
three words to keep separate
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 4
Return to Index
TINY EXAMPLE
10 + 5
Operator
The symbol that tells
JavaScript what to do.
+ means add.
Operands
The values that the operator
works on.
10 and 5 are the operands.
Expression
A piece of code that
evaluates to a value.
10 + 5 becomes 15.
REMEMBER
An expression can be as small as `5 + 2`, or bigger, like `age >= 18 ? "adult" :
"minor"`. The important part is that JavaScript can evaluate it and obtain a result.


05
 Arithmetic Operators
the operators you use for calculations
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 5
Return to Index
Operator
Name
What it does
Example
+
addition
adds values
8 + 3 -> 11
-
subtraction
subtracts
8 - 3 -> 5
*
multiplication
multiplies
8 * 3 -> 24
/
division
divides
8 / 4 -> 2
%
remainder
gives the remainder
8 % 3 -> 2
**
power
raises a number to a power
2 ** 3 -> 8
++
increment
adds 1
x++
--
decrement
subtracts 1
x--
THREE EASY TRAPS
`/` can produce a decimal. Example: `5 / 2` gives `2.5`. `**` is power, so `3 ** 2` means 3
raised to 2 = 9. `%` is not a percentage operator - it is remainder.


06
 Arithmetic in Action
write the result into a variable, then print it
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 6
Return to Index
BEST BEGINNER EXAMPLE
let price = 120;
let quantity = 3;
let total = price * quantity;
console.log("Total:", total);
HOW TO READ THE CODE
Step 1 -> `price` stores 120. Step 2 -> `quantity` stores 3. Step 3 -> `price * quantity`
calculates 360. Step 4 -> `total` stores 360. Step 5 -> `console.log()` prints it.
QUICK PRACTICE
let a = 2;
let b = 4;
console.log(a + b);
console.log(a - b);
console.log(a * b);
console.log(a / b);
console.log(a ** b);
CHECK YOURSELF
Expected values: 6, -2, 8, 0.5 and 16. Do not memorize the numbers - learn what
each symbol asks JavaScript to calculate.


07
 Remainder Operator (%)
the quickest path to divisibility checks
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 7
Return to Index
START HERE
console.log(10 % 3);
// 1
10 / 3
quotient = 3     remainder = 1
WHY % IS USEFUL
When checking even/odd, divide by 2. If the remainder is 0, the number is even. If
the remainder is 1 (for whole numbers), it is odd.
EVEN / ODD CHECK
let num = 42;
const result = num % 2 === 0;
console.log(result); // true
let other = 21;
console.log(other % 2 === 0); // false
PATTERN TO REMEMBER
The pattern is: `value % divisor === 0`. If the comparison is true, the value is exactly
divisible by that divisor.


08
 Operator Precedence
when more than one operator appears
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 8
Return to Index
FIRST EXAMPLE
let result = 2 + 3 * 4;
console.log(result); // 14
WHAT HAPPENED?
JavaScript does not simply read every operator from left to right. Some operators
are applied before others. Multiplication happens before addition here, so `3 * 4`
becomes 12, then 2 + 12 becomes 14.
USE PARENTHESES WHEN YOU WANT A DIFFERENT GROUPING
let result = (2 + 3) * 4;
console.log(result); // 20
NOTEBOOK RULE
Parentheses are your clarity tool. Even when JavaScript already knows the
precedence, parentheses can make your intention obvious to another programmer.
Small beginner priority list
-
 parentheses `( )`
-
 power `**`
-
 multiplication / division / remainder `* / %`
-
 addition / subtraction `+ -`
-
 comparisons such as `>`, `<`, `===`
-
 logical `&&`, then `||`
-
 ternary `? :`


09
 Increment and Decrement
same symbol, different timing
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 9
Return to Index
CORE IDEA
`++` adds 1. `--` subtracts 1. The important question is not only "what changes?" but
also "when is the new value used?"
Form
What changes?
What value does the expression produce?
++x
x increases first
new value
x++
x increases after
old value
--x
x decreases first
new value
x--
x decreases after
old value
NORMAL USE: JUST CHANGE THE VARIABLE
let score = 10;
score++;
console.log(score);
// 11
GOOD HABIT
Most beginner code is easier to read when you use `score++` or `score += 1` on its
own line. The pre/post difference matters when the expression itself is used to
produce a value.


10
 Pre-Increment (++x)
change first, use second
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 10
Return to Index
TRACE THIS SLOWLY
let x = 2;
console.log(++x);
console.log(x);
Start: x = 2
 1. ++x -> x becomes 3
2. expression value -> 3
3. x stays 3 after the statement
ONE-LINE MEMORY
Think of `++x` as: "increase x, then hand me the updated value."
STORED RESULT EXAMPLE
let points = 9;
let shown = ++points;
console.log(shown);  // 10
console.log(points); // 10


11
 Post-Increment (x++)
use the old value, then change the variable
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 11
Return to Index
TRACE THIS SLOWLY
let x = 4;
console.log(x++);
console.log(x);
Start: x = 4
 1. expression value -> 4
2. then x becomes 5
3. next console.log(x) prints 5
ONE-LINE MEMORY
Think of `x++` as: "give me the current x, then increase x."
STORED RESULT EXAMPLE
let count = 7;
const before = count++;
console.log(before); // 7
console.log(count);  // 8


12
 Pre vs Post - Side by Side
same update, different returned value
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 12
Return to Index
Code
Step 1
Step 2
Value produced
++x
x = x + 1
use x
new x
x++
use x
x = x + 1
old x
--x
x = x - 1
use x
new x
x--
use x
x = x - 1
old x
COMPARE THE OUTPUTS
let n = 5;
console.log(++n); // 6
console.log(n);   // 6
let m = 5;
console.log(m++); // 5
console.log(m);   // 6
IMPORTANT
If `++x` or `x++` is on its own line, both increase x by 1. The difference only becomes
visible when JavaScript needs the value produced by the expression.


13
 Execution Tracing
make a tiny table instead of guessing
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 13
Return to Index
EXAMPLE TO TRACE
let x = 3;
console.log(++x, x++);
console.log(x);
Step
Expression
x before
value produced
x after
1
++x
3
4
4
2
x++
4
4
5
RESULT
The output is `4 4` on the first line and `5` on the second. When a pre/post operator
confuses you, write the variable state before and after.
Tracing recipe
-
 write the starting value
-
 read one operator from left to right
-
 write the value produced
-
 update the variable
-
 move to the next operator


14
 Compound ++ Expression
slow down - one operator at a time
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 14
Return to Index
SOURCE-INSPIRED CHALLENGE
let x = 5;
let y = 5;
const result = x++ + y++ + ++y + y++;
console.log(result);
console.log(y);
Part
value used
variable after
x++
5
x = 6
y++
5
y = 6
++y
7
y = 7
y++
7
y = 8
HOW TO SOLVE IT
Add the values that were USED by each expression: 5 + 5 + 7 + 7 = 24. The final y is
8. Do not try to calculate the whole thing in one jump.
READABLE-CODE NOTE
This kind of dense expression is useful for learning how the operator works, but
clear production code is usually easier to maintain when the updates are split into
separate lines.


15
 Decrement Operators
the same timing rule, but subtract 1
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 15
Return to Index
PRE AND POST DECREMENT TOGETHER
let value = 5;
console.log(--value); // 4
console.log(value--); // 4
console.log(value);    // 3
READ THE ORDER
`--value` changes first, so the expression gives 4. `value--` gives the current 4 first,
then changes the variable to 3.
Form
Meaning
--x
x = x - 1, then use x
x--
use x, then x = x - 1
MIRROR RULE
Increment and decrement are mirror images: `++` adds one, `--` subtracts one. The
pre/post timing rule is identical.


16
 Assignment Operators
calculate and save the new value
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 16
Return to Index
Operator
Same as
Simple meaning
=
x = y
put a value into x
+=
x = x + y
add, then store
-=
x = x - y
subtract, then store
*=
x = x * y
multiply, then store
/=
x = x / y
divide, then store
%=
x = x % y
remainder, then store
**=
x = x ** y
power, then store
SHORT FORM VS LONG FORM
let total = 30;
let extra = 40;
total += extra;
console.log(total); // 70
// same idea written longhand
total = total + extra;
REMEMBER
The shortcut is not a brand-new operation. It is a compact way of saying: calculate
with the current value and assign the result back to the same variable.

READING METHOD
Shortcut thinking: first look at the left variable, then look at the right value, then
perform the operation. Finally store the result back in the left variable.


18
 Comparison Operators
comparisons usually answer with true or false
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 18
Return to Index
Operator
Meaning
Example
==
loose equality
5 == "5" -> true
===
strict equality
5 === "5" -> false
!=
loose inequality
5 != "5" -> false
!==
strict inequality
5 !== "5" -> true

less than
10 < 3 -> false

less or equal
10 <= 10 -> true
WHY COMPARISONS MATTER
Comparison operators are the bridge to conditions. Example: `marks >= 40` is a
question. JavaScript evaluates it and gets either true or false.
SMALL CONDITION
let marks = 72;
const passed = marks >= 40;
console.log(passed); // true


19
 Loose vs Strict Equality
the famous == vs === question
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 19
Return to Index
SAME VISIBLE CHARACTERS, DIFFERENT TYPES
console.log(22 == "22");
console.log(22 === "22");
Expression
Result
Why?
22 == "22"
true
loose comparison may convert types
22 === "22"
false
strict comparison keeps the type difference
MENTAL PICTURE
A number `22` and a string `"22"` are not the same type. Strict equality asks for both
a matching value and a matching type.
WHY STRICT EQUALITY IS EASIER TO REASON ABOUT
let userInput = "18";
let age = 18;
console.log(userInput == age);  // true
console.log(userInput === age); // false
STUDY NOTE
For beginner code, using `===` makes the type check visible instead of hiding a
conversion inside the comparison.


20
 Inequality Operators
the opposite side of equality
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 20
Return to Index
SAME PAIR, NOW CHECKING "DIFFERENT"
console.log(10 != "10");
console.log(10 !== "10");
Operator
Think of it as
!=
not equal after loose comparison
!==
not equal under strict comparison
EXAMPLE EXPLAINED
`10 != "10"` is false because loose equality can consider them equal after conversion.
`10 !== "10"` is true because the types differ.
QUICK TEST
const entered = "10";
const expected = 10;
console.log(entered !== expected); // true


21
 Relational Operators
greater, smaller, or equal in one direction
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 21
Return to Index
SAME PAIR AS THE LESSON
let a = 22;
let b = "22";
console.log(a > b);
console.log(a < b);
console.log(a >= b);
console.log(a <= b);
Expression
Result
Plain-language reason
22 > "22"
false
22 is not greater than 22
22 < "22"
false
22 is not smaller than 22
22 >= "22"
true
they compare as equal in value
22 <= "22"
true
they compare as equal in value
IMPORTANT
When different types are compared with `<`, `>`, `<=` or `>=`, JavaScript may convert
values before comparing them. Learn the result AND the rule that produced it.


22
 Logical Operators
combine conditions into one decision
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 22
Return to Index
Operator
Name
Beginner rule
&&
AND
both sides must be true
||
OR
at least one side must be true
!
NOT
flip the Boolean result
REAL-WORLD ACCESS EXAMPLE
let hasPassword = true;
let isVerified = false;
console.log(hasPassword && isVerified); // false
console.log(hasPassword || isVerified); // true
console.log(!isVerified); // true
SIMPLE PICTURE
AND is like a gate with two locks: both conditions must open it. OR is like two
different entry routes: one working route is enough. NOT changes true to false, and
false to true.


23
 Logical Truth Tables
the fastest way to memorize boolean combinations
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes

Return to Index

A
B

true
true
true
false
false
false

false

OR
A
B
A || B
true
true
true
true
false
true
false
true
true
false
false
false
NOT
A
!A
true
false
false
true
ONE SENTENCE
Memory line: AND needs all true. OR needs at least one true. NOT simply flips
the Boolean value.


24
 Short-Circuit Evaluation
sometimes JavaScript stops early
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 24
Return to Index
WHAT "SHORT-CIRCUIT" MEANS
Logical operators are evaluated from left to right. If the answer is already known,
JavaScript may not evaluate the remaining side.
WATCH THE RIGHT-HAND SIDE
false && console.log("this never runs");
true || console.log("this never runs");
TWO MEMORY PATTERNS
`false && anything` is already false, so there is no reason to evaluate `anything`. `true
|| anything` is already true, so JavaScript can stop there too.
PROOF BY SIDE EFFECT
function check(){
    console.log("check ran");
    return true;
}
console.log(true || check());
WHY YOU SHOULD CARE
The function call on the right is skipped because the left side of `||` is already truthy.
This is useful for understanding both performance and side effects.


25
 Truthy, Falsy and Returned Values
a small but important upgrade to the basic AND/OR rule
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 25
Return to Index
THE DEEPER RULE
In simple Boolean examples, `&&` and `||` look like they return true or false. In real
JavaScript, they can return one of their actual operands. What decides the path is
truthiness.
Value
Falsy?
false
yes
0
yes
""
yes
null
yes
undefined
yes
NaN
yes
42
no
"hello"
no
[]
no
{}
no
LOGICAL OPERATORS CAN SELECT VALUES
console.log("" || "Guest");
// "Guest"
console.log(5 && 8);
// 8
console.log(0 && 8);
// 0
EXAM-SAFE EXPLANATION
For the session-level rule, learn the Boolean cases first. Then remember this
advanced fact: `&&`, `||` and `??` are value-selection operators when used with
non-Boolean operands.


26
 Ternary Operator
a compact way to choose between two values
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 26
Return to Index
BEST BEGINNER EXAMPLE
const age = 21;
const status = age >= 18 ? "Adult" : "Minor";
console.log(status);
condition
 ? 
if true
 : 
if false
HOW TO READ IT
Read it as: "If the condition is true, use the value after `?`. Otherwise, use the value
after `:`." The whole ternary expression produces one value, which can be stored in a
variable.
ANOTHER PRACTICAL EXAMPLE
const marks = 76;
const result = marks >= 40 ? "Pass" : "Fail";
console.log(result); // Pass


27
 Ternary vs if / else
same decision, different shape
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 27
Return to Index
TERNARY
const result = age >= 18 ? "Adult" : "Minor";
IF / ELSE
let result;
if (age >= 18) {
    result = "Adult";
} else {
    result = "Minor";
}
READABLE-CODE RULE
Use a ternary when the decision is short and you simply need one of two values.
Use a normal `if / else` block when the logic has multiple steps or becomes harder
to read.
DO NOT OVERUSE IT
Avoid turning one line into a puzzle by nesting many ternaries inside each other.
Shorter code is not automatically clearer code.


28
 Problem Solving: Positive or Negative
comparison + ternary
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 28
Return to Index
SOLUTION
const number = -10;
const sign = number >= 0 ? "Positive" : "Negative";
console.log(sign);
EXPLAIN THE CODE
`number >= 0` is false for -10, so JavaScript chooses the value after `:`. The final
value is `"Negative"`.
SLIGHTLY BETTER REUSABLE VERSION
function getSign(number){
    return number >= 0 ? "Positive" : "Negative";
}
console.log(getSign(25)); // Positive
console.log(getSign(-3)); // Negative
PATTERN
The important problem-solving pattern is more general than this example: build a
Boolean question first, then decide what text or value should be used when the
answer is true or false.


29
 Problem Solving: Largest Number
comparison + ternary, one decision
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 29
Return to Index
BETTER OUTPUT: RETURN THE ACTUAL NUMBER
const a = 10;
const b = 20;
const larger = a > b ? a : b;
console.log(larger); // 20
READ THE LOGIC
The condition `a > b` is false because 10 is not greater than 20. So the ternary gives
us `b`, which is 20.
REALISTIC EXAMPLE
const first = 45;
const second = 32;
const largest = first > second ? first : second;
console.log("Largest:", largest);
WHY THIS VERSION IS USEFUL
A useful improvement over the source-style example is to return the number itself
rather than only printing which variable is larger. That makes the result reusable
later.


30
 Problem Solving: Even or Odd
modulus + strict equality + ternary
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 30
Return to Index
CLEAN SOLUTION
const number = 21;
const type = number % 2 === 0 ? "Even" : "Odd";
console.log(type);
21 % 2
-> 1
-> 1 === 0 is false
-> Odd
THREE OPERATORS WORKING TOGETHER
The `%` operator finds the remainder. The `=== 0` part turns that remainder into a
Boolean check. The ternary turns the Boolean check into a readable result.
TINY PRACTICE LOOP
for (const n of [12, 17, 40, 55]) {
    const kind = n % 2 === 0 ? "Even" : "Odd";
    console.log(n, kind);
}


31
 Problem Solving: Access Check
logical AND in a real rule
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 31
Return to Index
TWO LOCKS, ONE GATE
const hasPassword = true;
const isVerified = true;
const canEnter = hasPassword && isVerified;
console.log(canEnter); // true
EXPLAIN IT
Because both conditions are true, AND produces true. If either condition becomes
false, the final Boolean result becomes false.
OR EXAMPLE
const isAdmin = false;
const isOwner = true;
const canManage = isAdmin || isOwner;
console.log(canManage); // true
PROBLEM-SOLVING PATTERN
This is how logical operators connect simple checks into a larger rule: one
condition describes one fact; `&&` or `||` describes how those facts must work
together.


32
 Common Mistakes
the things that usually cause wrong answers
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 32
Return to Index
1. Confusing `=` with `===`
`=` assigns. `===` compares.
2. Forgetting that `x++` produces the old value
The variable changes after the expression produces its value.
3. Reading `%` as "percent"
In JavaScript it is the remainder operator.
4. Treating `&&` and `||` as always returning Boolean
With non-Boolean values, they can return an operand.
5. Writing a long ternary puzzle
If it becomes hard to read, use `if / else`.
6. Doing mental math on a compound ++ expression
Trace one operator at a time.
7. Ignoring parentheses
Use them when grouping should be obvious.

35
 Answer Key A
solutions with the reason, not just the output
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 35
Return to Index
1. 18 + 7
The expression evaluates to 25.
2. 17 % 5
2, because 15 goes into the division and 2 remains.
3. 5 then 5?
For `x = 4`, x++ prints 4 and then x becomes 5. The second print is 5.
4. score = score + 10
That is the long form of `score += 10`.
5. == vs ===
`==` may coerce types; `===` compares without converting the operands.
6. true
10 and "10" differ in type, so strict inequality is true.
7. true
The relational comparison can coerce the string "12" to a numeric value for
comparison.
8. number % 2 === 0
A zero remainder means the number is divisible by 2.
9. hasPassword && isVerified
Both facts must be true.
10. age >= 18 ? "Adult" : "Minor"
The condition selects the correct text value.


36
 Answer Key B
the useful reasoning behind the harder set
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 36
Return to Index
11. true
`&&` is evaluated before `||`: true && false -> false; false || true -> true.
12. "Guest"
The empty string is falsy, so OR moves to the right-hand value.
13. 14
Multiplication happens before addition: 3 * 4 = 12, then 2 + 12 = 14.
14. total *= 2
That is the compound-assignment form.
15. a < b ? a : b
If a is smaller, use a; otherwise use b.
16. marks >= 40 ? "Pass" : "Fail"
The comparison creates the decision.
17. 6, 6
Starting at 5: ++x makes 6 and produces 6; x++ then produces 6 and moves x to 7
after the expression.
18. name || "Guest"
When name is falsy, the fallback string is selected.
19. The call may be skipped
If the left side already determines the result, the right side is not evaluated.
20. Example below
Use all four pieces in one small program.


37
 Interview Revision
short answers to say confidently
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 37
Return to Index
What is an operator?
A symbol or keyword that performs an operation on one or more operands.
What is an operand?
A value that an operator works with.
What is an expression?
A piece of code that JavaScript evaluates to produce a value.
What does % do?
It returns the remainder after division.
++x vs x++?
`++x` increments first and produces the new value. `x++` produces the old value first,
then increments.
+= vs +?
`+` calculates a sum. `+=` calculates a sum and stores the new value back in the left
variable.
== vs ===?
Loose equality may coerce types. Strict equality compares without type conversion.
&& vs ||?
AND needs all conditions to be true; OR needs at least one. Both can short-circuit.
What is short-circuiting?
Stopping the evaluation when the final logical result is already known.
What is a ternary?
A three-part expression: condition ? trueValue : falseValue.


38
 One-Page Quick Revision
last-minute scan before a quiz or viva
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 38
Return to Index
ARITHMETIC
+ - * / % **
++ --
ASSIGNMENT
= += -= *= /= %= **=
COMPARISON
== === != !==
> < >= <=
LOGICAL
&& || !
AND / OR / NOT
TERNARY
condition ?
valueIfTrue : valueIfFalse
TRACE
pre -> change first
post -> use first
TEN-SECOND SCAN
Fast memory: `=` assigns. `===` compares. `%` finds remainder. `++x` changes first.
`x++` uses first. `&&` needs all true. `||` needs one true. `!` flips. `?:` chooses one value.
EXAM HABIT
Before answering an output question, write the variable state. Before choosing an
operator, say what you want the program to do in plain English.

console.log("=== Operators Practice ===");
const price = 120;
const quantity = 3;
const total = price * quantity;
console.log("Total:", total);
let score = 10;
console.log("Before:", score);
console.log("Post:", score++);
console.log("After:", score);
console.log("Pre:", ++score);
let points = 30;
points += 20;
points -= 5;
console.log("Points:", points);

console.log(typedAge == 18);
console.log(typedAge === 18);

WHAT THIS PROGRAM DEMONSTRATES
This final example combines the session ideas into one readable file: arithmetic,
pre/post operators, compound assignment, strict/loose equality, AND, modulus and
ternary. The variables are named by meaning instead of single letters, so the
program is easier to read later.

03 Session roadmap
Math tools -> spread values -> make decisions -> solve practical problems.
1. MATH OBJECT
A ready-made toolbox for common calculations.
2. SPREAD (...)
Open an array and place its items one-by-one where JavaScript expects separate values.
3. CONDITIONALS
Ask a true/false question and run the branch that matches.
The reference lesson follows this same sequence and then turns the ideas into validation, grading, access,
range and simulation problems.
Study habit
Before writing code, say the rule in plain English. Example: 'The person needs age 18+ AND a valid ID.' Then
translate each part into a condition.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 4
RETURN TO INDEX
04 Math Object
A built-in toolbox for mathematical constants and static methods.
Think of Math like a calculator shelf that already exists inside JavaScript. You call the methods directly.
Common forms
M a t h . r a n d o m ( ) ; 
M a t h . f l o o r ( 8 . 9 ) ; 
M a t h . s q r t ( 8 1 ) ; 
M a t h . p o w ( 2 ,  5 ) ; 
M a t h . m i n ( 4 ,  9 ,  1 ) ; 
M a t h . m a x ( 4 ,  9 ,  1 ) ; 
Remember
Math is not something you create with new. Its methods are called directly on Math.
MDN confirms Math is a namespace-style built-in object whose properties and methods are static.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 5
RETURN TO INDEX
05 Math.random()
Returns a pseudo-random floating-point value from 0 inclusive to 1 exclusive.
c o n s t  v a l u e  =  M a t h . r a n d o m ( ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( v a l u e ) ; 
Memory rule
0 <= value < 1
You may see 0.14, 0.73 or 0.999... The exact value changes between runs.
Security note
Math.random() is not for cryptographic security. Use a Web Crypto API when security-grade randomness is
needed.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 6
RETURN TO INDEX
06 Random integer patterns
Turn Math.random() into the ranges you actually need.
/ /  0 . . 9 
c o n s t  d i g i t  =  M a t h . f l o o r ( M a t h . r a n d o m ( )  *  1 0 ) ; 
/ /  1 . . 6  ( d i c e ) 
c o n s t  d i c e  =  M a t h . f l o o r ( M a t h . r a n d o m ( )  *  6 )  +  1 ; 
/ /  1 . . 1 0 0 
c o n s t  s c o r e  =  M a t h . f l o o r ( M a t h . r a n d o m ( )  *  1 0 0 )  +  1 ; 
1..6 step-by-step
random() -> 0 <= x < 1
x * 6 -> 0 <= x < 6
floor -> 0,1,2,3,4,5
+1 -> 1,2,3,4,5,6

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 7
RETURN TO INDEX
07 Math.floor()
Always rounds down toward the lower integer.
M a t h . f l o o r ( 4 . 9 ) ;    / /  4 
M a t h . f l o o r ( 4 . 1 ) ;    / /  4 
M a t h . f l o o r ( - 4 . 1 ) ;   / /  - 5 
Easy trap
Do not think 'floor = remove decimals'. For negative values, floor can move farther from zero.
Formally, Math.floor(x) returns the greatest integer that is less than or equal to x.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 8
RETURN TO INDEX
08 Math.sqrt()
Find the positive square root.
c o n s t  r o o t  =  M a t h . s q r t ( 1 4 4 ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( r o o t ) ;  / /  1 2 
Think it like this
What number multiplied by itself gives 144? 12 × 12 = 144, so the answer is 12.
Real use
/ /  D i s t a n c e  b e t w e e n  t w o  p o i n t s 
c o n s t  d  =  M a t h . s q r t ( ( x 2 - x 1 ) * * 2  +  ( y 2 - y 1 ) * * 2 ) ; 

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 9
RETURN TO INDEX
09 Math.pow()
Raise a base to an exponent.
M a t h . p o w ( 2 ,  5 ) ;  / /  3 2 
/ /  m o d e r n  e q u i v a l e n t 
2  * *  5 ;           / /  3 2 
Read Math.pow(2, 5) as '2 to the power 5'.
Interview tip
Math.pow(base, exponent) and ** both express exponentiation; ** is the shorter modern form.

JavaScript - Session 3 Page 10
RETURN TO INDEX
10 Math.min()
Return the smallest value from the arguments.
c o n s t  s m a l l e s t  =  M a t h . m i n ( 1 8 ,  4 ,  2 9 ,  1 1 ,  7 ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( s m a l l e s t ) ;  / /  4 
Important
Math.min() receives individual numeric arguments. An array needs to be expanded first.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 11
RETURN TO INDEX
11 Math.max()
Return the largest value from the arguments.
c o n s t  l a r g e s t  =  M a t h . m a x ( 1 8 ,  4 ,  2 9 ,  1 1 ,  7 ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( l a r g e s t ) ;  / /  2 9 
When the numbers already live inside an array, spread connects naturally with Math.max().

12 Math quick table
Fast recall before solving questions.
Method Meaning Example
Math.random() 0 <= value < 1 Math.random()
Math.floor(x) round down Math.floor(4.9) -> 4
Math.sqrt(x) square root Math.sqrt(81) -> 9
Math.pow(a,b) a raised to b 2 ** 5 -> 32
Math.min(...) smallest Math.min(8,2,5) -> 2
Math.max(...) largest Math.max(8,2,5) -> 8
Memory line
random -> floor -> sqrt -> pow -> min/max

13 Spread operator (...)
Expand an iterable such as an array into individual items.
c o n s t  m a r k s  =  [ 7 2 ,  8 8 ,  9 1 ] ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( . . . m a r k s ) ; 
/ /  7 2  8 8  9 1 
Mental model
Read ...marks as: 'take the items inside marks and put them here one-by-one.'
MDN: spread syntax can expand iterable values in function calls and array literals; object spread has its own behavior.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 14
RETURN TO INDEX
14 Spread in function calls
Why spread helps when a function expects separate arguments.
f u n c t i o n  t o t a l ( a ,  b ,  c )  { 
    r e t u r n  a  +  b  +  c ; 
} 
c o n s t  v a l u e s  =  [ 1 0 ,  2 0 ,  3 0 ] ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( t o t a l ( . . . v a l u e s ) ) ;  / /  6 0 
Without spread, total(values) passes one array. With spread, JavaScript effectively receives total(10, 20,
30).

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 15
RETURN TO INDEX
15 Spread + Math.max/min
The key Session 3 connection.
c o n s t  n u m b e r s  =  [ 1 0 ,  4 4 ,  2 ,  7 ,  3 1 ,  5 ] ; 
c o n s t  h i g h e s t  =  M a t h . m a x ( . . . n u m b e r s ) ; 
c o n s t  l o w e s t  =  M a t h . m i n ( . . . n u m b e r s ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( h i g h e s t ) ;  / /  4 4 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( l o w e s t ) ;   / /  2 
Why it works
Math.max(10, 44, 2, ...) is valid. Math.max(numbers) is not the same call. Spread turns the array into separate
arguments.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 16
RETURN TO INDEX
16 Spread in arrays + copying
A useful extension: combine arrays and make shallow copies.
c o n s t  f i r s t  =  [ 1 ,  2 ,  3 ] ; 
c o n s t  c o m b i n e d  =  [ 0 ,  . . . f i r s t ,  4 ] ; 
/ /  [ 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 ] 
c o n s t  c o p y  =  [ . . . f i r s t ] ; 
c o p y . p u s h ( 9 9 ) ; 
Shallow copy
The new array is separate at the outer level. Nested objects/arrays are still shared references.

17 Conditional statements
A condition is a question that becomes true or false.
i f  ( c o n d i t i o n )  { 
    / /  t r u e  b r a n c h 
}  e l s e  i f  ( a n o t h e r C o n d i t i o n )  { 
    / /  n e x t  b r a n c h 
}  e l s e  { 
    / /  f a l l b a c k  b r a n c h 
} 
Flow
Check condition -> choose one branch -> run it -> continue.
The reference lesson defines conditional statements as control-flow constructs that choose different blocks
based on a boolean condition.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 18
RETURN TO INDEX
18 if statement
Run a block only when the condition is true.
c o n s t  a g e  =  2 1 ; 
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A d u l t " ) ; 
} 
Read the question
age >= 18 asks: 'Is age at least 18?' Since 21 satisfies the condition, the block runs.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 19
RETURN TO INDEX
19 if / else
Two possible paths.
c o n s t  a g e  =  1 6 ; 
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " C a n  v o t e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " C a n n o t  v o t e " ) ; 
} 
Exactly one branch runs. If the condition is false, control jumps to else.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 20
RETURN TO INDEX
20 else if chains
Use multiple branches for ranges or categories.
c o n s t  m a r k s  =  7 8 ; 
i f  ( m a r k s  > =  9 0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A + " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  7 5 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  4 0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " P a s s " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " F a i l " ) ; 
} 
Order matters
JavaScript checks from top to bottom and stops at the first matching branch. Put stronger/higher rules first.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 21
RETURN TO INDEX
21 Nested conditions
An if can contain another if.
c o n s t  a g e  =  2 0 ; 
c o n s t  h a s I D  =  t r u e ; 
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  { 
    i f  ( h a s I D )  { 
        c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A c c e s s  g r a n t e d " ) ; 
    } 
} 
Cleaner when the rule is simple
If both checks must pass, combine them with && to make the rule easier to read.
Cleaner version
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8  & &  h a s I D )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A c c e s s  g r a n t e d " ) ; 
} 

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 22
RETURN TO INDEX
22 Branch order
The first true branch wins.
c o n s t  t e m p  =  4 0 ; 
i f  ( t e m p  >  3 5 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " H o t " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( t e m p  > =  1 5 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N o r m a l " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " C o l d " ) ; 
} 
At 40, the first condition is true, so JavaScript never evaluates the later branches for execution.
Remember
Branch order is part of the logic, not just formatting.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 23
RETURN TO INDEX
23 Ternary operator
A compact two-way conditional that produces a value.
c o n s t  a g e  =  1 9 ; 
c o n s t  s t a t u s  =  a g e  > =  1 8 
    ?  " A d u l t " 
    :  " M i n o r " ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( s t a t u s ) ;  / /  A d u l t 
Pattern
condition ? valueWhenTrue : valueWhenFalse
It is called ternary because it has three operands: the condition, the true result and the false result.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 24
RETURN TO INDEX
24 if / else vs ternary
Same decision idea, different style.
Style Best fit
if / else Run blocks, perform several steps, or handle multiple branches.
ternary Choose one value/message from two alternatives.
/ /  i f  /  e l s e 
l e t  r e s u l t ; 
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  r e s u l t  =  " E l i g i b l e " ; 
e l s e  r e s u l t  =  " N o t  e l i g i b l e " ; 
/ /  t e r n a r y 
c o n s t  r e s u l t 2  =  a g e  > =  1 8  ?  " E l i g i b l e "  :  " N o t  e l i g i b l e " ; 
Practical rule
Keep ternaries short. If the expression becomes hard to scan, use if/else.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 25
RETURN TO INDEX
25 Common decision patterns
Turn plain-English rules into code.
/ /  E v e n 
n  %  2  = = =  0 
/ /  I n s i d e  a  r a n g e 
n  > =  1 0  & &  n  < =  5 0 
/ /  B O T H  r e q u i r e m e n t s 
a g e  > =  1 8  & &  h a s I D 
/ /  E I T H E R  r e q u i r e m e n t 
i s A d m i n  | |  i s O w n e r 
/ /  P o s i t i v e  /  n e g a t i v e  /  z e r o 
n  >  0 
n  <  0 
e l s e  - >  z e r o 
Best habit
Write the English rule first. Then convert each part into a comparison. Finally connect the parts with && or ||.

26 Q3 - Voting + validation
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  a g e  =  2 8 ; 
i f  ( a g e  <  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A g e  m u s t  b e  p o s i t i v e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " E l i g i b l e  t o  v o t e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N o t  e l i g i b l e  t o  v o t e " ) ; 
} 
How it works
Validation comes first, so invalid values are rejected before eligibility is decided.
Expected result
Eligible to vote

27 Q4 - Larger of two
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  a  =  2 3 ; 
c o n s t  b  =  3 4 ; 
c o n s t  l a r g e r  =  a  >  b  ?  a  :  b ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( l a r g e r ) ; 
How it works
The ternary selects a only when a is bigger. Otherwise b is used.
Expected result
34

Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.

c o n s t  c  =  5 6 ; 
c o n s t  l a r g e s t  =  M a t h . m a x ( a ,  b ,  c ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( l a r g e s t ) ; 
How it works
Math.max compares the supplied arguments and returns the highest one.
Expected result
56

29 Q6 - Divisible by 3 and 5
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  n  =  1 5 ; 
i f  ( n  %  3  = = =  0  & &  n  %  5  = = =  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " B o t h " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( n  %  3  = = =  0  | |  n  %  5  = = =  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " O n l y  o n e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N e i t h e r " ) ; 
} 
How it works
Remainder 0 means divisible. && checks both rules; || checks whether at least one rule is true.
Expected result
Both

30 Q7 - Divisible by 5 and 10
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  n  =  1 5 ; 
i f  ( n  %  5  = = =  0  & &  n  %  1 0  = = =  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " B o t h " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( n  %  5  = = =  0  | |  n  %  1 0  = = =  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " O n l y  o n e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N e i t h e r " ) ; 
} 
How it works
15 is divisible by 5, but not by 10, so the 'Only one' branch runs.
Expected result
Only one

31 Q8 - Student grading
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  m a r k s  =  9 9 ; 
l e t  g r a d e  =  " " ; 
i f  ( m a r k s  > =  0  & &  m a r k s  < =  1 0 0 )  { 
    i f  ( m a r k s  > =  9 0 )  g r a d e  =  " A + " ; 
    e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  7 0 )  g r a d e  =  " A " ; 
    e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  4 0 )  g r a d e  =  " B " ; 
    e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  3 0 )  g r a d e  =  " C " ; 
    e l s e  g r a d e  =  " F a i l " ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    g r a d e  =  " I n v a l i d  m a r k s " ; 
} 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( g r a d e ) ; 
How it works
First validate the allowed range. Then the nested grade ladder chooses the first matching band.
Expected result
A+

32 Q9 - Age category
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.

} 
How it works

Teenager

33 Q10 - Range check
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  n  =  3 4 ; 
i f  ( n  > =  1 0  & &  n  < =  5 0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " I n s i d e  r a n g e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " O u t s i d e  r a n g e " ) ; 
} 
How it works
The number must satisfy both boundary conditions because the range is inclusive.
Expected result
Inside range

34 Q11 - Username + password
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  u s e r n a m e  =  " a d m i n _ u s e r " ; 
c o n s t  p a s s w o r d  =  " S e c u r e P a s s w o r d 1 2 3 " ; 
i f  ( 
    u s e r n a m e  = = =  " a d m i n _ u s e r "  & & 
    p a s s w o r d  = = =  " S e c u r e P a s s w o r d 1 2 3 " 
)  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " L o g i n  a c c e p t e d " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " L o g i n  r e j e c t e d " ) ; 
} 
How it works
Use strict equality for both fields and && because both credentials must match.
Expected result
Login accepted

37 Q14 - Negative or odd

    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N e i t h e r " ) ; 
} 
How it works

41 Q18 - Passed all subjects
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  m a t h  =  4 5 ; 
c o n s t  s c i e n c e  =  3 8 ; 
c o n s t  e n g l i s h  =  5 0 ; 
c o n s t  p a s s  =  3 5 ; 
i f  ( m a t h  > =  p a s s  & & 
    s c i e n c e  > =  p a s s  & & 
    e n g l i s h  > =  p a s s )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " P a s s e d  a l l  s u b j e c t s " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " F a i l e d  o n e  o r  m o r e  s u b j e c t s " ) ; 
} 
How it works
All three comparisons must succeed. One failed subject is enough to enter else.
Expected result
Passed all subjects

How it works

46 Challenge questions
Predict first; then verify by running the code.
1) What integers can Math.floor(Math.random() * 10) produce?
Answer
0 through 9
2) What reaches Math.max(...[8, 2, 17, 5]) and what is the result?
Answer
8, 2, 17, 5 are individual arguments; result = 17
3) Marks = 72 in the grading ladder. Which grade matches?
Answer
A
4) age = 20 and hasID = false. What happens with age >= 18 && hasID?
Answer
Access denied
5) temp = 40. Which branch wins?
Answer
Hot

49 Interview revision
Short answers for a viva or interview.
What is the Math object?
A built-in namespace object that provides mathematical constants and static methods.
What does Math.random() return?
A pseudo-random floating-point number from 0 inclusive to 1 exclusive.
What does Math.floor() do?
It returns the greatest integer less than or equal to the input - in simple words, it rounds down.
What does spread do?
It expands iterable values, such as array items, into argument positions or array elements.
Why use ...array with Math.max()?
Because Math.max expects separate numeric arguments, while an array is one value.
What is a conditional statement?
A control-flow structure that chooses which block should run based on a condition.
What is ternary?
A three-part expression: condition ? trueValue : falseValue.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 50
RETURN TO INDEX
50 Quick revision sheet
One-page cheat sheet.
Concept Quick memory
Math.random() 0 <= value < 1
Math.floor(x) round down
Math.sqrt(x) square root
Math.pow(a,b) a raised to b
Math.min(...) smallest argument
Math.max(...) largest argument
Spread (...) expand iterable items
if / else choose a branch
else if try another condition
ternary condition ? true : false
&& both must be true
|| at least one must be true
% === 0 common divisibility check
n >= min && n <= max inclusive range
Memory chain
Math -> Spread -> Conditions -> Practice

54 Common mistakes + final checklist
Use this before moving to Session 4.
- Math.random() never returns exactly 1. - Math.floor(-4.1) is -5. - Math.max(numbers) is not the same as
Math.max(...numbers). - Spread expands; rest collects. - Avoid deeply nested ternaries. - else-if branches
are checked top to bottom. - BOTH -> think &&. EITHER -> think ||. - Validate input before applying
business rules. - Write the rule in plain English before writing the condition.
Final self-test
Can you explain Math.random(), Math.floor(), Math.sqrt(), Math.pow(), Math.min(), Math.max(), spread,
if/else-if/else, ternary, &&, || and range checks without looking?

Session 4 Roadmap
How the ideas connect
STRING VALUES
↓
inspect → search → extract → replace → test
↓
NUMBER VALUES
↓
check validity → convert → compare parsing
↓
DOM
↓

Core idea

converted, and the DOM lets JavaScript read data from HTML.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

04
String Methods Overview
A practical toolkit for working with text
String methods help you inspect, search, extract, transform and format text. Most
methods return a new value instead of rewriting the original string.
Group Methods
Inspect length, charAt, at
Search indexOf, lastIndexOf, includes, search, match
Extract slice, substring
Transform toLowerCase, toUpperCase, replace, replaceAll
Combine concat
Test startsWith, endsWith, isWellFormed
Format trim, repeat, padStart, padEnd
Remember
Think: original text → method call → returned result. Store the returned result when you need it later.
let str = "I am learning Javascript learning";
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

05
length
String methods
Gives the size of the string. Spaces count too.
Example
const text = "JavaScript is fun";
console.log(text.length);
What happens
You get a number. For ordinary English text this behaves like a character count.
Remember
length is a property, not a function. Write text.length, not text.length().
str.length
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

06
toLowerCase() / toUpperCase()
String methods
Makes a lowercase or uppercase version.
Example
const text = "JavaScript";
console.log(text.toLowerCase());
console.log(text.toUpperCase());
What happens
javascript
JAVASCRIPT
Remember
The original string stays unchanged.
str.toLowerCase();
str.toUpperCase();
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

07
split()
String methods
Turns one string into an array using a separator.
Example
const line = "red,green,blue";
console.log(line.split(","));
What happens
["red", "green", "blue"]
Remember
With no separator, the whole string becomes one array item.
Common pitfall
split("") works at UTF-16 code-unit level, so it is not always the same as user-perceived characters.
str.split(separator)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

08
indexOf() / lastIndexOf()
String methods
Finds the first or last matching position.
Example
const text = "banana";
console.log(text.indexOf("a"));
console.log(text.lastIndexOf("a"));
What happens
1
5
Remember
If the value is not found, the result is -1.
Common pitfall
Do not use if (text.indexOf("a")) to test existence because index 0 is a valid match. Use !== -1.
str.indexOf(value);
str.lastIndexOf(value);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

09
slice()
String methods
Extracts part of a string. The end index is excluded.
Example
const text = "JavaScript";
console.log(text.slice(0, 4));
console.log(text.slice(-3));
What happens
Java
ipt
Remember
Negative indexes count from the end.
Common pitfall
slice(0, 4) gives indexes 0,1,2,3. The 4 is not included.
str.slice(start, end)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

10
match()
String methods
Finds matches using a regular expression.
Example
const text = "learn learn";
console.log(text.match(/learn/g));
What happens
["learn", "learn"]
Remember
The g flag means global matching. No match can produce null.
str.match(pattern)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

11
concat()
String methods
Joins strings and returns the combined string.
Example
const first = "Hello";
console.log(first.concat(" JavaScript"));
What happens
Hello JavaScript
Remember
For simple joining, the + operator is also common.
str.concat(value1, value2, ...)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

12
replace()
String methods
Replaces the first matching occurrence when the search value is a string.
Example
const text = "hi hi";
console.log(text.replace("hi", "hello"));
What happens
hello hi
Remember
Use replaceAll() or a global pattern when every occurrence should change.
str.replace(searchValue, newValue)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

13
replaceAll()
String methods
Replaces every matching string occurrence.
Example
const text = "hi hi";
console.log(text.replaceAll("hi", "hello"));
What happens
hello hello
Remember
replace() and replaceAll() answer different questions: first occurrence vs all occurrences.
str.replaceAll(searchValue, newValue)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

14
startsWith() / endsWith()
String methods
Checks the beginning or ending of a string.
Example
const file = "notes.pdf";
console.log(file.startsWith("notes"));
console.log(file.endsWith(".pdf"));
What happens
true
true
Remember
Both return true or false and are case-sensitive.
str.startsWith(value);
str.endsWith(value);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

15
charAt()
String methods
Reads a character at a zero-based index.
Example
const word = "Code";
console.log(word.charAt(0));
console.log(word.charAt(3));
What happens
C
e
Remember
Modern alternatives include word[0] and word.at(0). Out-of-range charAt() returns an empty string.
str.charAt(index)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

16
isWellFormed()
String methods
Checks whether a string has well-formed Unicode code units.
Example
const text = "Hello";
console.log(text.isWellFormed());
What happens
true
Remember
It is more specialized than the other beginner methods, but it appears in this session.
str.isWellFormed()
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

17
Related String Methods I
Useful methods that complete the same family
Method Simple meaning Example
includes() Does the text contain this value? text.includes("JS")
search() Find the first regex match index text.search(/JS/)
substring() Extract using two non-negative indexes text.substring(0,4)
When to use which
Use includes() for a clear yes/no check, search() when a regular expression is useful, and substring()
when you specifically want substring-style index rules.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

18
Related String Methods II
Whitespace, indexing and character information
Method Use Example
trim() Remove whitespace from both ends input.trim()
trimStart() Remove leading whitespace input.trimStart()
trimEnd() Remove trailing whitespace input.trimEnd()
at() Read a character and support negative indexes text.at(-1)
charCodeAt() Return the UTF-16 code-unit number text.charCodeAt(0)
Easy memory trick
at(-1) is a convenient way to ask for the last character. charCodeAt() gives a number rather than the
character itself.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

19
Related String Methods III
Formatting helpers
Method Use Example
repeat() Repeat a pattern "-".repeat(5)
padStart() Pad the beginning to a target length "7".padStart(3,"0")
padEnd() Pad the end to a target length "7".padEnd(3,"0")
matchAll() Walk through all regex matches and capture groups text.matchAll(/go/g)
Practical use
These helpers are useful for formatting labels, IDs, reports and repeated text patterns.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

20
String Method Pattern + Immutability
How to think about method calls
original string
↓
method call
↓
returned string
↓
save the result if you need it later
Important
Strings are primitive values. String methods return new string values; they do not mutate the original
primitive in place.
let text = " hello ";
let cleaned = text.trim();
let upper = cleaned.toUpperCase();
console.log(text);
console.log(upper);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

21
Number Checking
Global isNaN() vs the Number.is* family
Tool Question it answers
isNaN(value) Does conversion of this value produce NaN?
Number.isNaN(value) Is this value actually NaN?
Number.isFinite(value) Is this a finite number?
Number.isInteger(value) Is this an integer?
Number.isSafeInteger(value) Is this an integer in the safe range?
Why this matters
Validation should ask a precise question. The Number.is* methods avoid the broad coercion behavior of
global isNaN().
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

22
isNaN()
Global numeric validity check
Read it
Because 4 is a number, isNaN(4) is false and the else block runs.
Caution
The global isNaN() converts its argument before checking. That can be surprising for strings.
let value = 4;
console.log(isNaN(value));
if (isNaN(value)) {
  console.log("Invalid number");
} else {
  console.log("Valid number");
}
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

23
Number.isNaN(), isFinite(), isInteger(), isSafeInteger()
More explicit checks
Output
true
true
true
true
Rule of thumb
Prefer Number.is* methods when you want a specific numeric check without broad type coercion.
console.log(Number.isNaN(NaN));
console.log(Number.isFinite(12));
console.log(Number.isInteger(12));
console.log(Number.isSafeInteger(12));
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

24
Number Conversion Overview
The same input string can be interpreted in different ways
Function Simple rule
Number() The complete value must represent a valid number.
parseInt() Read an integer prefix.
parseFloat() Read a decimal-number prefix.
const value = "12.34abc";
Number(value);
parseInt(value, 10);
parseFloat(value);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

25
Number()
Whole-value conversion
Output
12.34
NaN
0
Simple rule
Use Number() when the whole input is supposed to be numeric. Validate the result when input may be
invalid.
console.log(Number("12.34"));
console.log(Number("12.34abc"));
console.log(Number(""));
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

26
parseInt()
Read an integer prefix
Output
12
5
42
Important
parseInt() stops when the next character cannot belong to the integer. The radix argument is
recommended.
console.log(parseInt("12.34abc", 10));
console.log(parseInt("101", 2));
console.log(parseInt("42px", 10));
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

27
parseFloat()
Read a decimal prefix
Output
12.34
42
3.14
Pair it with parseInt()
parseInt() is for an integer prefix. parseFloat() is for a decimal prefix.
console.log(parseFloat("12.34abc"));
console.log(parseFloat("42px"));
console.log(parseFloat("3.14"));
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

28
Number Conversion Comparison
Memorize the pattern
Input Number() parseInt() parseFloat()
"12.34abc" NaN 12 12.34
"42px" NaN 42 42
"12.34" 12.34 12 12.34
"" 0 NaN NaN
Memory map
Number() = whole value | parseInt() = integer prefix | parseFloat() = decimal prefix
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

29
DOM Basics - getElementById()
Select one element by its id
The DOM is the browser representation of the HTML document. JavaScript uses document
methods to find elements and read their properties.
Think in two steps
1) Find the element. 2) Read a property such as .value or .textContent.
Important
An id is intended to identify one element in a document.
const input = document.getElementById("name");
console.log(input.value);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

30
getElementsByTagName()
Collect elements by tag name
What you get
An HTMLCollection containing all matching paragraph elements. It can contain zero, one, or many items.
const paragraphs = document.getElementsByTagName("p");
console.log(paragraphs);
console.log(paragraphs[0].textContent);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

31
Related DOM Selectors
Natural next steps from id and tag selectors
Method Purpose Example
querySelector() First element matching a CSS selector document.querySelector("p")
querySelectorAll() All matching elements as a NodeList document.querySelectorAll("p")
getElementsByClassName() Elements sharing a class name document.getElementsByClassName("item")
Easy rule
querySelector() gives the first match. querySelectorAll() gives all matches.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

32
Collections + Duplicate IDs
Why the HTML structure matters
Problem in the supplied HTML
The source uses id="para1" twice. IDs are meant to be unique. Use unique ids, or use a class for repeated
elements.
Collection thinking
Tag and class selectors can return collections. An id selector is intended to point to one specific element.
<div id="para1">1</div>
<div id="para1">2</div>
<p>Lorem ipsum dolor sit.</p>
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

getData() Walkthrough
Read the input and inspect paragraph elements
Step What happens
1 The user types in the input.
2 The button calls getData().
3 getElementById("name") finds the input.
4 .value reads the current text.
5 getElementsByTagName("p") returns the paragraph collection.
HTML connection
The supplied button uses onclick="getData()", so the function runs when the button is clicked.
function getData() {
  const res = document.getElementById("name").value;
  console.log("res", res);
  const para1 = document.getElementsByTagName("p");
  console.log("para1 =>", para1);
}

39
Mini Project - Input Validator
Combine the Session 4 ideas
User enters text
↓
trim() → empty check
↓
empty → show message
↓
else → uppercase + length + startsWith/endsWith
↓
numeric branch → Number() + Number.isNaN()
Requirement Concept
Read input getElementById() + .value
Clean input trim()
Text analysis length, includes(), startsWith(), endsWith()
Text transform toUpperCase(), replaceAll()
Numeric branch Number() + Number.isNaN()
Output console.log() or a DOM update
Why this is useful
It combines DOM reading, string cleanup, string methods and condition-based thinking in one small
browser task.
function validateInput() {
  const raw = document.getElementById("name").value;
  const value = raw.trim();
  if (value === "") {
    console.log("Please enter something");
    return;
  }
  console.log("Upper:", value.toUpperCase());
  console.log("Length:", value.length);
  console.log("Starts with J:", value.startsWith("J"));
}
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

40
Interview Revision
Fast answers
Question Answer
What does length return? String length as UTF-16 code units.
indexOf() vs lastIndexOf()? First matching index vs last matching index.
What does slice() do? Extracts a portion; end index is excluded.
replace() vs replaceAll()? First occurrence vs all occurrences for a string search.
What does isNaN() do? Checks whether a value becomes NaN after numeric conversion.
Number.isNaN()? Checks whether the value itself is NaN without broad coercion.
Number() vs parseInt()? Whole-value conversion vs integer-prefix parsing.
getElementById()? Selects the element with a matching id.
getElementsByTagName()? Returns matching elements as an HTMLCollection.
Why avoid duplicate ids? An id is intended to be unique in the document.
