DOM Element Selection
Start by selecting the element you want to change
Most browser updates begin with selecting an element. The supplied page gives the image
id="img", so JavaScript can find it with getElementById().
Mental model
document → find element → receive an Element object → read or modify its properties.
Remember
Choose a selector that matches the HTML. An id is intended to identify one element.
04
const image = document.getElementById("img");
console.log(image);
console.log(image.src);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

innerHTML
Insert or replace HTML inside an element
What happens
The supplied string is parsed as HTML, so a new child div is created inside #main.
Safer text
For plain text, textContent is usually better. Avoid inserting untrusted user input through innerHTML because
it parses HTML.
05
const box = document.getElementById("main");
box.innerHTML = "<div id="hey">Hey</div>";
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

style Property
Change CSS directly from JavaScript
CSS JavaScript style name
background-color backgroundColor
font-size fontSize
border-left borderLeft
margin-top marginTop
Memory trick
CSS names usually become camelCase when used through element.style.
06
const box = document.getElementById("main");
box.style.color = "cyan";
box.style.borderLeft = "5px solid cyan";
box.style.borderRight = "5px solid purple";
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

style.backgroundColor + fontSize
Update specific CSS properties
Read it
backgroundColor changes the background. fontSize changes the text size. Both are inline styles.
Better for larger UIs
When a design has reusable states, CSS classes and classList are usually easier to maintain than many inline
styles.
07
const title = document.getElementById("hey");
title.style.backgroundColor = "purple";
title.style.fontSize = "35px";
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

setAttribute()
Create or change an HTML attribute
Simple rule
setAttribute(name, value) writes the attribute. If the attribute exists, its value changes; if it does not exist, the
attribute is created.
Source connection
The supplied change() and reverse() functions use setAttribute("src", ...).
08
const image = document.getElementById("img");
image.setAttribute("src", "new-image.jpg");
image.setAttribute("alt", "A space image");
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

getAttribute()
Read an attribute value
Return value
You get the attribute value as a string. If that attribute is missing, the result is null.
Think
setAttribute = write. getAttribute = read.
09
const image = document.getElementById("img");
console.log(image.getAttribute("src"));
console.log(image.getAttribute("alt"));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

hasAttribute() + removeAttribute()
Check or remove an attribute
What happens
hasAttribute() returns true or false. removeAttribute() removes the named attribute.
Useful pattern
Check first when your UI logic depends on whether an attribute exists.
10
const image = document.getElementById("img");
console.log(image.hasAttribute("alt"));
image.removeAttribute("alt");
console.log(image.hasAttribute("alt"));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Image Attribute Switching
Change an image when the user clicks a button
click button
↓
run function
↓
select image
↓
change src
↓
browser loads new resource
Important
Changing src swaps the image resource. A CSS transition does not automatically animate a src swap itself.
11
function changeImage() {
  const image = document.getElementById("img");
  image.style.transition = "1s linear";
  image.setAttribute("src", "supernova.jpg");
}
function restoreImage() {
  const image = document.getElementById("img");
  image.setAttribute("src", "original.jpg");
}
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

onclick + addEventListener()
Connect user clicks to JavaScript
Source style
The supplied HTML uses inline onclick attributes. That is useful for learning the event flow.
Modern style
addEventListener("click", handler) keeps JavaScript behavior in JavaScript and allows multiple listeners.
Memory rule
onclick is the event-handler attribute name. With addEventListener(), use "click" without the on prefix.
12
<button id="btn" onclick="changeImage()">Change</button>
const btn = document.getElementById("btn");
btn.addEventListener("click", changeImage);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Array Fundamentals
Arrays are ordered, mutable collections
Group Methods in this session
Add / remove ends push, pop, shift, unshift
Edit by index splice
Convert / rearrange join, reverse, sort
Read / search length, indexing, includes, indexOf, lastIndexOf
Process forEach, map, filter, find, findIndex, some, every
Source correction
The supplied code writes li = [...] without let / const / var. Declare the variable explicitly.
13
const values = [11, 22, 33, 44, 55, 66];
console.log(values[0]);
console.log(values.length);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

push()
Add items at the end
Result
newLength is 5 and values becomes [11,22,33,44,55].
Return value
push() returns the new array length, not the array. It mutates the original array.
14
const values = [11, 22, 33];
const newLength = values.push(44, 55);
console.log(newLength);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

pop()
Remove the last item
Result
removed is 44. values becomes [11,22,33].
Return value
pop() returns the removed last element.
15
const values = [11, 22, 33, 44];
const removed = values.pop();
console.log(removed);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

shift()
Remove the first item
Result
first is 11. values becomes [22,33,44].
Source correction
The removed first value comes from shift(), not unshift().
16
const values = [11, 22, 33, 44];
const first = values.shift();
console.log(first);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

unshift()
Add items at the beginning
Result
newLength is 4. values becomes [11,22,33,44].
Important
Calling unshift() with no arguments does not add or replace anything. It simply returns the current length.
17
const values = [22, 33, 44];
const newLength = values.unshift(11);
console.log(newLength);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

shift() vs unshift()
The two front-of-array methods
Method Action Returns
shift() Remove first element Removed element
unshift(value) Add at beginning New array length
Memory trick
shift = take away from the front. unshift = put onto the front.
18
const values = [22, 33, 44];
const removed = values.shift();
values.unshift(11);
console.log(removed);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

splice()
The all-purpose array editor
Goal Pattern
Insert splice(index, 0, value)
Delete splice(index, count)
Replace splice(index, count, replacement)
Important
splice() mutates the original array and returns an array containing removed elements.
19
array.splice(start, deleteCount, item1, item2);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

splice() - Add
Insert without deleting
Result
[22, 888, 33, 44, 55]
How it works
Start at index 1. Delete zero items. Insert 888. Existing values move right.
20
const values = [22, 33, 44, 55];
values.splice(1, 0, 888);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

splice() - Delete
Remove a chosen number of items
Result
removed = [888,33,44,55] and values = [22].
Think
start + count = which items are removed.
21
const values = [22, 888, 33, 44, 55];
const removed = values.splice(1, 4);
console.log(removed);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

splice() - Replace
Delete and insert in one step
Result
[22, 999, 345]
Think
Start at index 1 → remove one item → put 999 in that position.
22
const values = [22, 66, 345];
values.splice(1, 1, 999);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

join()
Convert an array into one string
Result
22_345_345
Mutation?
No. join() returns a string and does not change the array.
23
const values = [22, 345, 345];
const text = values.join("_");
console.log(text);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

reverse()
Reverse the order in place
Result
Both result and values now show the reversed order because reverse() mutates the original array.
Safer copy
If the original order matters, copy first: const copy = [...values].
24
const values = [22, 345, 345];
const result = values.reverse();
console.log(result);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

sort()
Sort arrays correctly
Why the comparator?
Default sort compares values as strings. For ascending numbers, return a - b.
Source clarification
The supplied file creates sortArray but never calls sort(). The important numeric rule is still worth learning.
25
const nums = [10, 2, 30, 4];
nums.sort();
console.log(nums);
nums.sort((a, b) => a - b);
console.log(nums);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Array Indexing + Second-Last
Use length arithmetic
length = 3
↓
last index = 3 - 1 = 2
↓
second-last = 3 - 2 = 1
↓
values[1] = 345
Memory rule
Last index = length - 1. Second-last index = length - 2.
26
const values = [345, 345, 22];
console.log(values[values.length - 2]);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

slice() + concat()
Non-mutating array companions
Method Purpose Mutates?
slice(start,end) Copy a portion of an array No
concat(values) Create a combined array No
Why learn these?
Use them when you want to read or combine values without changing the original array.
27
const values = [11,22,33,44];
console.log(values.slice(1, 3));
console.log(values.concat([55,66]));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

includes() / indexOf() / lastIndexOf()
Find values in an array
Method Meaning
includes() Does the value exist?
indexOf() First matching index
lastIndexOf() Last matching index
Output
true, 1, 3
28
const values = [11, 22, 33, 22];
console.log(values.includes(22));
console.log(values.indexOf(22));
console.log(values.lastIndexOf(22));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

forEach()
Do something for every element
Callback data
The callback commonly receives value, index and the full array.
Important
forEach() is for side effects. It does not build the transformed array for you. Use map() for transformation.
29
const values = [10, 20, 30];
values.forEach((value, index) => {
  console.log(index, value);
});
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

map()
Create a transformed array
Result
[20, 40, 60]
Rule
One input element → one output element. map() returns a new array.
30
const values = [10, 20, 30];
const doubled = values.map(value => value * 2);
console.log(doubled);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

filter()
Keep only values that pass a test
Result
[10, 20]
Rule
The callback should return true for items you want to keep.
31
const values = [10, 15, 20, 25];
const even = values.filter(value => value % 2 === 0);
console.log(even);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

find() + findIndex()
Find the first match
Method Returns
find(predicate) First matching value or undefined
findIndex(predicate) Index of first match or -1
Output
20 and 2
32
const values = [10, 15, 20, 25];
console.log(values.find(v => v > 18));
console.log(values.findIndex(v => v > 18));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

some() + every()
Ask Boolean questions about an array
Method Question
some() Does at least one element pass?
every() Do all elements pass?
Connection to earlier sessions
These return Boolean results and work naturally with comparison and logical operators.
33
const values = [2, 4, 6];
console.log(values.some(v => v > 5));
console.log(values.every(v => v % 2 === 0));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Modern Non-Mutating Array Options
Useful when you want immutable-style code
Simple idea
toSorted() and toReversed() return new arrays, so the original nums stays unchanged.
Why add this page?
The supplied session focuses on sort() and reverse(). Current JavaScript also provides non-mutating
alternatives for the same jobs.
34
const nums = [10, 2, 30, 4];
const sorted = nums.toSorted((a, b) => a - b);
const reversed = nums.toReversed();
console.log(nums);
console.log(sorted);
console.log(reversed);

Key correction
The two unshift() calls do not add or replace anything because no values were supplied. The original source

// add end -> push()
// remove end -> pop()

// add front -> unshift()
// insert / delete / replace -> splice()
// array -> string -> join()
// reverse order -> reverse()
// numeric order -> sort((a,b) => a-b)
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Interview Revision
Fast answers for Session 5
Question Answer
What does push() return? The new array length.
What does pop() return? The removed last element.
shift() vs unshift()? Remove first vs add at beginning.
What does splice() do? Insert, delete or replace values at an index.
Does join() mutate? No. It returns a string.
Does reverse() mutate? Yes. It reverses the original array.
Why use a comparator with sort()? Default sorting is lexicographic; a-b gives numeric ascending order.
setAttribute()? Sets or replaces an attribute.
getAttribute()? Reads an attribute value.
innerHTML? Parses HTML and replaces an element’s contents.
onclick? Runs a handler when the element is clicked.
forEach() vs map()? forEach performs work; map creates a new transformed array.
filter()? Creates a new array containing items that pass a test.
find() vs findIndex()? First matching value vs first matching index.
43

Quick Revision Sheet
Session 5 at a glance
Memory map
DOM → select → read/change. Arrays → add/remove/edit → rearrange → process.
44
DOM
getElementById -> select one element by id
innerHTML -> insert / replace HTML
style.* -> set inline CSS properties
setAttribute -> set / change attribute
getAttribute -> read attribute
hasAttribute -> test attribute existence
removeAttribute -> remove attribute
onclick -> run handler after click
addEventListener -> modern event wiring
ARRAYS
push -> add end
pop -> remove end
shift -> remove front
unshift -> add front
splice -> insert / delete / replace
join -> array to string
reverse -> reverse in place
sort -> sort in place
slice / concat -> new arrays
forEach -> process each item
map -> transform
filter -> keep matches
find / findIndex -> first match
some / every -> Boolean tests

Source Corrections & Clarifications
Important notes from the supplied code
Source item Clarification
li = [..] Declare the variable with const or let.
li.unshift() with no arguments It does not change the content; it returns the current length.
"Get the first removed value using unshift()" The removed first value comes from shift(), not unshift().
"To replace the first" + unshift() unshift adds at the beginning. It does not replace an existing item.
sortArray created but not sorted A sort call was missing; numeric sorting needs (a,b) => a-b.
reverse() Mutates the original array.
setAttribute(src) Changes the image resource when the URL is valid.
onclick Works for learning; addEventListener is cleaner for larger applications.
Why this page exists
The supplied material is useful practice, but a few comments do not match the actual behavior of the
method. These notes keep the original intent while making the examples technically usable.

8
FILE: s1\s1.js
TYPE: Text
======================================================================

/* ==========================================================================
   SESSION 1: JAVASCRIPT VARIABLES, SCOPE RULES & DATA TYPES
   ========================================================================== */

console.log("=== SESSION 1: VARIABLES & DATA TYPES ===");

// ==========================================================================
// 1. VARIABLE DECLARATION & INITIALIZATION
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * CONCEPT: Variable Declaration
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Telling the computer that a variable exists and giving it a name (reserving an empty box).
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   An instruction introducing an identifier into a scope and allocating a slot for 
   it in the execution context's lexical environment prior to value evaluation.
 */

// Standalone declarations (without initialization) work only with var and let
var a; // Function-scoped (hoisted as undefined)
let b; // Block-scoped (resides in Temporal Dead Zone until initialized)


/* 
 * CONCEPT: Constant Variable Requirement
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   A 'const' variable cannot be created empty; it must be assigned a value immediately.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   Constants must be initialized at declaration time. Omitting initialization raises a 
   SyntaxError ("Missing initializer in const declaration") at parse time.
 */

const c = 10; // Must declare and initialize at the same time

/* 
 * CONCEPT: Variable Initialization
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Assigning the very first value to a variable that was previously declared.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   The initial binding of a value to an allocated memory address during execution context setup.
 */

a = 10;
b = 20;


/* 
 * CONCEPT: Combined Declaration + Initialization
 */

var x = 70;
let y = 20;
const z = 43;


/* 
 * CONCEPT: Re-declaration vs Re-assignment
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   - Re-declaration: Creating a variable with the same name again (allowed ONLY for var).
   - Re-assignment: Changing the value inside an existing variable (allowed for var and let).
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   - Re-declaration: Re-defining an identifier in the same lexical scope; disallowed for let/const.
   - Re-assignment: Mutating the value binding of an identifier reference; disallowed for const.
 */

// Re-declaration (Only allowed with 'var')
var x = 40;

// Re-assignment (Allowed for 'var' and 'let', but NOT 'const')
x = 66;
y = 44;


// ==========================================================================
// 2. PRIMITIVE DATA TYPES
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * CONCEPT: Primitive Data Types
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Basic, single values that are immutable (cannot be altered directly in memory).
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   Data that is not an object and has no methods. Stored directly on the stack 
   by value. There are 7 primitive data types in JavaScript.
 */

// a) String: Represents textual data enclosed in quotes
let str = "Hello";

// b) Number: Handles both integers and floating-point decimal numbers
let num = 56;       // Integer
let num1 = 12.43;   // Floating-point decimal

// c) Boolean: Represents logical states (true or false)
let isReady = true;
isReady = false;

// d) Undefined: Represents a variable that has been declared but not assigned a value
let un;

// e) Null: Represents an intentional absence of any object value
// Note: typeof null returns "object" due to a historical JavaScript bug
let nullVal = null;

// f) BigInt: Used for handling arbitrarily large integers beyond Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER
let bigNum = 2342323534523453464575686785745n;

// g) Symbol: Guaranteed unique and immutable primitive value, often used as unique object keys
let sym = Symbol("id");


// ==========================================================================
// 3. NON-PRIMITIVE (REFERENCE) DATA TYPES
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * CONCEPT: Non-Primitive / Reference Data Types
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Complex data structures (like lists or collections) that store multiple values.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   Objects, Arrays, and Functions stored on the heap memory and accessed by memory reference. 
   They are mutable—their properties/elements can be modified even when declared with 'const'.
 */

// Array: Ordered list of values
const arr = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 6, 7];

// Object: Collection of key-value pairs
const obj = {
    id: 4,
    key: 78
};


// ==========================================================================
// 4. INTERACTIVE TASK: PROMPT INPUT ADDITION
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * CONCEPT: Explicit Type Casting with prompt()
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   prompt() always returns user input as text (String). We must wrap it in Number() 
   so JavaScript performs math addition instead of joining strings together.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   The prompt() Web API returns a String primitive. Performing arithmetic operations 
   requires explicit type coercion (e.g., Number(value)) to prevent trigger of 
   the '+' operator's string concatenation overload.
 */

if (typeof prompt !== "undefined") {
    let s = Number(prompt("Enter first number:"));
    let g = Number(prompt("Enter second number:"));

    console.log("Addition: ", s + g, "\nType of Number: ", typeof (s + g));
}

JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 3
03
Introduction to JavaScript
The language that adds behavior and logic to web pages.
What is JavaScript?
JavaScript is a programming language widely used to make web pages interactive and dynamic. In this session, we focus
on basic language building blocks: variables, data types, input, output, conversion and simple arithmetic.
Where JavaScript is commonly used
 Browser-based web applications and interactive interfaces
 Server-side applications through JavaScript runtimes such as Node.js
 Small scripts, automation and many other programming environments
Session goal
By the end of this session, you should be able to create variables, identify common data types, take simple browser input,
convert text input into numbers and display results.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 4
04
First Program - console.log()
Start with output: tell JavaScript what to display.
The simplest program in this session prints text to the console.
 Basic output
console.log("Hello");
What happens here?
`console.log()` sends a value to the console. The text inside quotes is a string. In a browser, the result can be viewed in
the Developer Tools console.
Expected output
Hello
Remember
A semicolon is commonly used to terminate a JavaScript statement. JavaScript can often insert semicolons automatically,
but consistent semicolon use improves readability.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 5
05
Variables in JavaScript
A variable gives a name to data that your program needs to work with.
JavaScript provides three variable declaration keywords: `var`, `let` and `const`. Their rules differ, especially for
redeclaration and reassignment.
Three declarations
var a;
let b;
const c = 10;
Core idea
Think of a variable as a named place your program can use to access a value. The keyword you choose determines what
changes are permitted later.
Keyword
Beginner meaning
var
Older declaration style; can be redeclared and reassigned.
let
Use when the variable value may be reassigned.
const
Use when the binding should not be reassigned; initialize it immediately.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 6
06
Declaration
Create the variable name before assigning a value.
Declaration
var a;
let b;
Declaration means introducing a variable to the program. At this point, no explicit value has been assigned.
Important
`var a;` and `let b;` are valid declarations. `const` is different: a `const` declaration must include an initializer.
const rule
const c; // SyntaxError
const c = 10; // Valid


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 7
07
Initialization
Give a declared variable its first value.
Declaration followed by assignment
var a;
let b;
a = 10;
b = 20;
Initialization is the act of giving a variable an initial value. For `var` and `let`, declaration and value assignment
may be separated.
Mental model
Declaration creates the named variable. Initialization gives it its first useful value.
Flow
 Simple flow
let b;   ->   b = 20;


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 8
08
Declaration + Initialization
Both steps can happen in one statement.
Combined form
var x = 70;
let y = 20;
const z = 43;
In these statements, the variable is declared and given its first value immediately. This is the most common way
you will write variables in everyday code.
Read this line
`let y = 20;` -> `let` is the keyword, `y` is the variable name, and `20` is the initial value.
Structure
let y = 20;
|   |   |
|   |   +-- value
|   +------ variable name
+---------- keyword


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 9
09
var, let and const
The three declaration keywords have different rules.
Feature
var
let
const
Declaration without initialization
Yes
Yes
No
Redeclaration in same scope
Yes
No
No
Reassignment
Yes
Yes
No
Must initialize immediately
No
No
Yes
Practical rule
For modern JavaScript, `let` and `const` are generally preferred. Use `let` when reassignment is needed and `const` when
the binding should not be reassigned.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 10
10
Re-declaration
Declare the same variable name again.
Examples
var x = 70;
var x = 40;
let y = 20;
// let y = 40; // Error
const z = 43;
// const z = 50; // Error
Definition
Redeclaration means creating another declaration using the same variable name in the same scope. `var` permits this;
`let` and `const` do not.
Exam point
Do not confuse redeclaration with reassignment. They are different operations.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 11
11
Re-assignment
Change the value of an existing variable.
Reassignment
var x = 40;
let y = 20;
const z = 43;
x = 66;
y = 44;
// z = 50; // Error
Concept
Meaning
Example
Redeclaration
Declare the same variable name again.
`var x; var x;`
Reassignment
Change the current value of an existing variable.
`x = 66;`
Key rule
`var` and `let` can be reassigned. A `const` binding cannot be reassigned after initialization.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 12
12
Data Types Overview
JavaScript values can be classified as primitive values or objects/reference types.
Category
Types in this session
Main idea
Primitive
String, Number, Boolean, Undefined, Null, BigInt
Represent individual primitive values.
Reference / object
types
Array, Object
Objects can hold collections or structured data.
Hierarchy
JavaScript Data Types
|
+-- Primitive
|   +-- String
|   +-- Number
|   +-- Boolean
|   +-- Undefined
|   +-- Null
|   +-- BigInt
|
+-- Reference / object types
    +-- Array
    +-- Object
Accuracy note
For beginner study, arrays and objects are commonly discussed as reference types. Avoid reducing JavaScript memory
behavior to a single slogan such as "primitive = stored directly, object = stored by reference"; the language model is more
nuanced.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 13
13
String
Textual data enclosed in quotes.
String examples
let str = "Hello";
let name = "Roshan";
let city = 'Mumbai';
A string represents text. JavaScript allows string literals using single quotes or double quotes.
Value
Type
Purpose
"Hello"
string
Text
'Mumbai'
string
Text
Remember
Quotes are part of the syntax used to create the string literal; the actual string value does not include the surrounding
quote characters.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 14
14
Number
The JavaScript `number` type covers integers and floating-point values.
Number examples
let num = 56;
let num1 = 12.43;
Example
Kind
JavaScript type
56
Integer
number
12.43
Floating-point
number
Remember
You do not use separate JavaScript types called "integer" and "float" in this beginner-level model. Both examples above
have the type `number`.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 15
15
Boolean
A Boolean value expresses one of two logical states.
Boolean examples
let isReady = true;
isReady = false;
Value
Beginner-friendly interpretation
true
condition is satisfied / on
false
condition is not satisfied / off
Important
Boolean is a real data type with exactly two values: `true` and `false`. The words "yes" and "no" are only useful analogies
for learning.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 16
16
Undefined
A variable can exist without having an assigned value.
Undefined example
let value;
console.log(value);
console.log(typeof value);
A declared `let` or `var` variable that has not been assigned a value evaluates to `undefined`. The `typeof`
operator reports its type as `"undefined"`.
Expected output
undefined "undefined"


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 17
17
Null
An intentional representation of "no value".
Null example
let nullVal = null;
console.log(nullVal);
console.log(typeof nullVal);
Value
Typical meaning
undefined
No value has been assigned in the usual declaration case.
null
The programmer intentionally represents an absent or empty value.
JavaScript quirk
`typeof null` returns `"object"`. This is a long-standing historical behavior in JavaScript; it does not mean that `null` is
actually an object.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 18
18
BigInt
Represent integers that are too large for exact ordinary `number` handling.
BigInt examples
let bigNum = 2342323534523453464575686785745n;
let safeBoundary = 9007199254740991n;
BigInt is a separate numeric type for integers beyond the exact integer range of ordinary JavaScript `Number`
values. The trailing `n` identifies a BigInt literal.
Remember
BigInt is for integers. Do not treat it as just a longer decimal `number`; it is a separate type with its own arithmetic rules.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 19
19
Non-Primitive / Reference Types
Arrays and objects are the structured values used in this session.
An array stores an ordered collection. An object stores related information using properties (key-value pairs). In
beginner-level discussions, both are commonly described as reference types because variables refer to object
values.
Focus for Session 1
You only need two examples today: arrays and objects. Later JavaScript sessions can build on these concepts with
methods, nested structures and more advanced object behavior.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 20
20
Array
Store multiple ordered values in one collection.
Array example
const arr = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 6, 7];
console.log(arr[0]); // 1
console.log(arr[3]); // 4
Index
0
1
2
3
4
5
6
7
Value
1
2
3
4
5
6
6
7
Remember
Array indexing starts at `0`, not `1`. Duplicate values are allowed.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 21
21
Object
Represent related information using properties.
Object example
const obj = {
    id: 4,
    key: 78
};
console.log(obj.id);
console.log(obj["id"]);
Part
Meaning
id
Property / key
4
Value stored for `id`
obj.id
Dot notation
obj["id"]
Bracket notation
Pattern
Object data is written as `key: value`. This key-value structure makes objects useful for representing records and grouped
information.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 22
22
prompt()
Collect simple user input in a browser environment.
User input
let s = prompt("Enter Num1: ");
let g = prompt("Enter Num2: ");
In a browser, `prompt()` displays a dialog box and returns the user input as text (or `null` if the dialog is
cancelled). For this session, the key point is that normal input arrives as a string.
Important
If the user enters `10`, the returned value is the string `"10"`, not the numeric value `10`. Convert it before arithmetic when
you want numeric behavior.


JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 23
23
Number() Conversion
Convert numeric text into a JavaScript number.
Conversion
let s = Number(prompt("Enter Num1: "));
let g = Number(prompt("Enter Num2: "));
Conversion flow
"10"
  |
  +-- Number("10")
        |
        +-- 10
Expression
Result
"10" + "20"
"1020" (string concatenation)
Number("10") + Number("20")
30 (numeric addition)
Remember
`Number()` asks JavaScript to convert a value to the `number` type. Invalid numeric text can produce `NaN`, but that edge
case is outside the main task in this session.

                         |

JavaScript - Session 1 Notes
Page 26
26
Common Beginner Mistakes
Use these warning boxes as a last-minute checklist.
1. `const` without initialization
`const x;` is invalid. Initialize it when declared.
2. Reassigning a `const`
`const x = 10; x = 20;` is an error.
3. Assuming `prompt()` returns a number
User input is text, so use `Number()` when numeric input is required.
4. Forgetting string concatenation
`"10" + "20"` gives `"1020"`, not `30`.
5. Confusing redeclaration with reassignment
Redeclaration creates another declaration; reassignment changes an existing value.
6. Starting array indexing at 1
The first array element is at index `0`.
7. Calling `null` an object
`typeof null` reports `"object"`, but `null` represents an intentional absence of value.

Quick Revision Sheet
One page to scan before a quiz, viva or practical exam.
Variables
`var` -> redeclaration + reassignment allowed `let` -> reassignment allowed, same-scope redeclaration not allowed
`const` -> initialization required; reassignment not allowed
Data Types
Primitive in this session -> String, Number, Boolean, Undefined, Null, BigInt Reference / object types -> Array, Object

reports the type category as a string
Three distinctions to remember
Declaration != Initialization Redeclaration != Reassignment String input != Numeric value

Answer Key - 1 to 15
Concise answers for self-checking.
1. `console.log("Hello JavaScript");`
2. `var a;` and `let b;`
3. `const value = 25;`
4. Declaration introduces the variable; initialization gives it its initial value.
5. Yes. Example: `var x = 1; var x = 2;`
6. Yes. Example: `let x = 1; x = 2;`
7. `const` requires an initializer at declaration time.
8. `let course = "JavaScript";`
9. Example: `let a = 10; let b = 12.5;`
10. `let ready = true; ready = false;`
11. `undefined`.
12. Example: `const nums = [10,20,30,40,50]; console.log(nums[0]);`
13. Example: `const user = { id: 1, name: "Asha" }; console.log(user.name);`
14. Redeclaration: `var x = 1; var x = 2;` Reassignment: `x = 3;`
15. Example: `const big = 12345678901234567890n;` The `n` suffix identifies a BigInt literal.

Answer Key - 16 to 25
Concise answers for the remaining questions.
16. Redeclaration creates another declaration with the same name; reassignment changes the value of an existing variable.
17. `undefined` commonly means no value has been assigned; `null` is an intentional absence value.
18. Because `prompt()` returns text, and numeric arithmetic requires numeric values.
19. `"1020"`.
20. `30`.
21. `"boolean"`.
22. `"bigint"`.
23. `typeof null` is `"object"`; this is a historical JavaScript behavior, not evidence that null is an object.
24. Index `2`.

(a + b));`

+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                                      JAVASCRIPT SESSION 1: EXECUTION ENGINE                                       |
+--------------------------------------------------------+---------------------------------------------------------+
                                                         |
        +------------------------------------------------+------------------------------------------------+
        |                                                |                                                |
        v                                                v                                                v
+---------------+                                +---------------+                                +---------------+
| 1. VARIABLES  |                                | 2. DATA TYPES |                                | 3. USER INPUT |
+---------------+                                +---------------+                                +---------------+
        |                                                |                                                |
        +---> [var]                                      +---> [Primitives (Stack / Value)]               +---> prompt()
        |     ├── Scope: Function                        |     ├── String ("Hello")                       |     └── Returns: String ("10")
        |     ├── Hoisted: Yes (undefined)               |     ├── Number (56, 12.43)                     |             |
        |     ├── Redeclare: YES                         |     ├── Boolean (true/false)                   +---> Conversion Check
        |     └── Reassign:  YES                         |     ├── Undefined (declared, unassigned)       |     ├── NO  -> "10"+"20" = "1020"
        |                                                |     ├── Null (intentional empty)               |     │          (Concatenation)
        +---> [let]                                      |     |   └── typeof null = "object" (Bug)       |     └── YES -> Number("10") = 10
        |     ├── Scope: Block                           |     ├── BigInt (234...n)                       |                (Numeric Coercion)
        |     ├── Hoisted: Temporal Dead Zone            |     └── Symbol (Symbol("id"))                  |             |
        |     ├── Redeclare: NO (SyntaxError)            |                                                +---> Operation
        |     └── Reassign:  YES                         +---> [Reference Types (Heap / Ref)]             |     ├── 10 + 20 = 30
        |                                                      ├── Array ([1, 2, 3])                      |     ├── console.log(30)
        +---> [const]                                          └── Object ({ id: 4, key: 78 })             |     └── typeof 30 = "number"
              ├── Scope: Block                                                                            +--------------------------------
              ├── Requires Initializer: YES
              ├── Redeclare: NO (SyntaxError)
              └── Reassign:  NO (TypeError)

FILE: s2\s2.js
TYPE: Text

/* ==========================================================================
   SESSION 2: JAVASCRIPT OPERATORS, EXPRESSIONS & PRACTICE PROBLEMS
   ========================================================================== */

console.log('=== SESSION 2: OPERATORS & LOGIC ===')

// ==========================================================================
// 1. MATHEMATICAL / ARITHMETIC OPERATORS
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Math symbols (+, -, *, /, %, **) used to calculate numbers, along with 
   shortcuts (++ and --) that add or subtract 1 from a variable.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   Unary and binary operators that take numerical operands and evaluate to 
   a single numeric value. Arithmetic operations with string operands invoke 
   implicit coercion algorithms (e.g., string concatenation with '+').
 */

// Basic Arithmetic Operations
let a = 10
let b = 5

let res = a + b
console.log('Addition result variable:', res) // 15

let x = 2
let y = 4

console.log('Addition (+):', x + y) // Output: 6
console.log('Subtraction (-):', x - y) // Output: -2
console.log('Multiplication (*):', x * y) // Output: 8
console.log('Division (/):', x / y) // Output: 0.5
console.log('Exponentiation (**):', x ** y) // 2^4 = 16

/* 
 * CONCEPT: Pre-Increment/Decrement vs. Post-Increment/Decrement
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition:
   - Pre-increment (++x): Modifies the variable FIRST, then uses the new value.
   - Post-increment (x++): Uses the current value FIRST, then modifies the variable.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition:
   Unary mutation operators. Pre-operators (++x / --x) mutate the variable in memory 
   and evaluate to the newly updated value. Post-operators (x++ / x--) evaluate to 
   the original primitive value before executing the side-effect mutation.
 */

// Increments
console.log('Pre-Increment (++x):', ++x) // Increments x to 3 FIRST, then prints 3
console.log('Post-Increment (y++):', y++) // Evaluates current y (4) FIRST, then increments y to 5
console.log('Value of y after post-increment:', y) // Output: 5

// Pre & Post Increment Logic Breakdown
// State before execution: x = 3, y = 5
console.log(++x, x++) // ++x makes x=4 (returns 4). x++ returns 4, then makes x=5. Output: 4 4
console.log('Value of x after pre & post increment:', x) // Output: 5

// Complex Compound Expression Breakdown:
// State before execution: x = 5, y = 5
// Expression: x++ (uses 5, x becomes 6) + y++ (uses 5, y becomes 6) + ++y (y becomes 7, uses 7) + y++ (uses 7, y becomes 8)
// Sum: 5 + 5 + 7 + 7 = 24
console.log('Compound Expression Result:', x++ + y++ + ++y + y++) // Output: 24
console.log('Value of y after expression execution:', y) // Output: 8

// Decrements
console.log('Pre-Decrement (--x):', --x) // Decrements x from 6 to 5 FIRST, then returns 5
console.log('Post-Decrement (x--):', x--) // Evaluates current x (5) FIRST, then decrements x to 4

// ==========================================================================
// 2. ASSIGNMENT OPERATORS
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Math shortcuts (+=, -=, *=, /=) that perform an operation and automatically 
   save the updated result back into the same variable.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   Operators that evaluate an expression on the right-hand side, apply an arithmetic 
   operation to the left-hand operand, and re-bind the new value to the variable.
 */

let s = 30
let g = 40

s += g // Equivalent to: s = s + g (30 + 40 = 70)
console.log('Addition Assignment (s += g):', s) // 70

s -= g // Equivalent to: s = s - g (70 - 40 = 30)
console.log('Subtraction Assignment (s -= g):', s) // 30

s *= g // Equivalent to: s = s * g (30 * 40 = 1200)
console.log('Multiplication Assignment (s *= g):', s) // 1200

s /= g // Equivalent to: s = s / g (1200 / 40 = 30)
console.log('Division Assignment (s /= g):', s) // 30

// ==========================================================================
// 3. COMPARISON OPERATORS
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Symbols used to compare two values, returning true or false. == checks if 
   values look identical; === checks if both value and data type match.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   Relational operators returning boolean predicates. Loose equality (==) executes 
   the Abstract Equality Comparison Algorithm (performing type coercion). 
   Strict equality (===) compares operands without type conversion.
 */

let d = 22
let e = '22'

// Abstract vs Strict Equality
console.log('Loose Equality (d == e):', d == e) // true (coerces string "22" to number 22)
console.log('Strict Equality (d === e):', d === e) // false (Number vs String)

// Relational Comparison
console.log('Greater Than (d > e):', d > e) // false
console.log('Less Than (d < e):', d < e) // false
console.log('Greater Than or Equal (d >= e):', d >= e) // true
console.log('Less Than or Equal (d <= e):', d <= e) // true

// Inequality
console.log("Abstract Inequality (10 != '10'):", 10 != '10') // false (coerces types; values match)
console.log("Strict Inequality (10 !== '10'):", 10 !== '10') // true (types differ)

// ==========================================================================
// 4. LOGICAL OPERATORS
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Used to combine multiple boolean conditions. && requires all conditions to 
   be true, || requires at least one true, and ! flips true to false.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   Operators used to evaluate conditional logic with short-circuiting capability. 
   Evaluation stops as soon as the final outcome is determined without checking 
   remaining operands.
 */

let hasAge = true
let hasID = false

console.log('Logical AND (&&):', hasAge && hasID) // false (both conditions must be true)
console.log('Logical OR (||):', hasAge || hasID) // true (at least one condition is true)
console.log('Logical NOT (!):', !hasID) // true (flips false to true)

// ==========================================================================
// 5. TERNARY OPERATOR & PRACTICE EXERCISES
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   A compact one-line version of an if-else statement: (condition ? trueResult : falseResult).
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   The only JavaScript operator accepting three operands. Unlike if-else statements, 
   the ternary operator is an expression that evaluates directly to a value.
 */

// Example: Voting Eligibility
let age = 34
let isEligible = age >= 18 ? 'You can Vote' : 'You are not eligible for voting'
console.log('Eligibility:', isEligible)

// --- SESSION 2 PRACTICE QUESTIONS ---

// Practice 1: Positive or Negative Number Check
let c = -10
const isPositive = c >= 0 ? 'Positive' : 'Negative'
console.log('Is Positive:', isPositive)

// Practice 2: Find Largest Number
let f = 10
let h = 20
const findLargest = f > h ? 'f is greater' : 'h is greater'
console.log('Largest:', findLargest)

// Practice 3: Even or Odd Number Check
let num = 21
const isEven = num % 2 === 0 ? 'even' : 'odd'
console.log('Is Even:', isEven)

03
Session Overview
What Session 2 adds to the JavaScript fundamentals from Session 1.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 3
This session moves from storing and reading values to working with those values. You will
learn how operators form expressions, produce results, change variables, compare values,
combine conditions and make compact decisions.
LEARNING ROADMAP
Operators
↓
Expressions
↓
Calculations
↓
Comparisons
↓
Logical Decisions
↓
Ternary Conditions
↓
Problem Solving
CORE VOCABULARY
Operator: a symbol or keyword that performs an operation.   Operand: a value used by an
operator.   Expression: code that evaluates to a value.


04
Operators & Expressions
Build the language before using individual operators.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 4
Operator, Operand, Expression
Start with the three words you will use throughout this session.
EXAMPLE
10 + 5
OPERATOR
+ is the operator. It tells JavaScript to perform addition.
OPERANDS
10 and 5 are the operands. They are the values being operated on.
EXPRESSION
10 + 5 is an expression because JavaScript evaluates it and produces a value: 15.


05
Arithmetic Operators
Symbols used to perform numerical operations.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 5
Arithmetic Operators
Arithmetic operators let you calculate values. The source session also introduces increment
and decrement as unary operators.
Operator
Name
Purpose
Example
+
Addition
Adds values
10 + 5
-
Subtraction
Subtracts values
10 - 5

Multiplication
Multiplies values
10 * 5
/
Division
Divides values
10 / 5

Returns remainder
10 % 3

Raises a value to a power
2 ** 3

Increment
Adds 1 to a variable
x++

Arithmetic can involve unary operators such as ++ and --, and binary operators such as +, -, *,
/.


06

07
Modulus Operator (%)
Returns the remainder after division.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 7
Modulus
The modulus operator is especially useful when you need to test divisibility or identify even
and odd numbers.
EXAMPLE
10 % 3
Expected output
1
HOW IT WORKS
10 divided by 3 produces a quotient of 3 with a remainder of 1. The % operator returns that
remainder.
PRACTICAL USE
21 % 2 produces 1. Because the remainder is not 0, 21 is odd.
EVEN-NUMBER TEST
21 % 2 === 0


08
Pre-Increment vs Post-Increment
The placement of ++ changes the value produced by the expression.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 8
Two Forms of Increment
Both forms increase a variable by one, but they differ in when the updated value is produced.
PRE-INCREMENT ++x
Change first, then use the updated value.
x = 2 → ++x → 3
POST-INCREMENT x++
Use the current value first, then increase
the variable.
x = 2 → x++ → 2, then x = 3
REMEMBER
The difference matters when ++x or x++ is part of a larger expression.


09
Pre-Increment (++x)
Change the variable first, then use the new value.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 9
Pre-Increment
The increment happens before the expression produces its value.
EXAMPLE
let x = 2;
console.log(++x);
console.log(x);
EXECUTION
Initial x = 2 → ++x changes x to 3 → the expression produces 3 → x remains 3 after the
statement.
Expected output
3
3
KEY TAKEAWAY
++x means: increment first, then use.


10
Post-Increment (x++)
Use the current value first, then change the variable.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 10
Post-Increment
The expression produces the old value; the variable is incremented as a side effect.
EXAMPLE
let y = 4;
console.log(y++);
console.log(y);
EXECUTION
Initial y = 4 → y++ produces 4 → then y becomes 5.
Expected output
4
5
KEY TAKEAWAY
x++ means: use first, then increment.


11
Pre-Decrement vs Post-Decrement
The same timing rule applies when a variable is reduced by one.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 11
Decrement Operators
Both --x and x-- subtract 1, but the expression produces a different value depending on
placement.
PRE-DECREMENT --x
Decrement first, then use the updated
value.
x = 5 → --x → 4
POST-DECREMENT x--
Use the current value first, then
decrement.
x = 5 → x-- → 5, then x = 4
SOURCE PATTERN
let x = 5;
console.log(--x);
console.log(x--);
REMEMBER
--x → change first.   x-- → use first.


12
Pre/Post Operator Execution Trace
Read the value produced and the variable state separately.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 12
Execution Trace
Tracing makes increment and decrement expressions easier to predict.
TRACE EXAMPLE
let x = 3;
console.log(++x, x++);
console.log(x);
Step
Operation
x Before
Value Produced
x After
1
++x
3
4
4
2
x++
4
4
5
Expected output
4 4
5
TRACE RULE
When you see ++ or --, write down two things: what value the expression produces and what
value the variable has afterward.


13
Complex Compound Expressions
Break a long expression into individual operations.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 13
Compound Increment Expression
The source session combines multiple post/pre operators in one expression. Read it one
operator at a time.
EXPRESSION
x++ + y++ + ++y + y++
Expression
Value Used
Variable After
x++
5
x = 6
y++
5
y = 6
++y
7
y = 7
y++
7
y = 8
RESULT
5 + 5 + 7 + 7 = 24
Expected output
Compound Expression Result: 24
Value of y after expression execution: 8
EXAM TIP
Do not try to mentally evaluate a compound ++/-- expression in one jump. Track each operator
in left-to-right order and record the variable state after each step.


14
Assignment Operators
Short forms that update and store a new value in the same variable.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 14
Assignment Operators
Assignment operators combine an arithmetic operation with assignment.
Operator
Equivalent
Meaning
=
x = value
Assign
+=
x = x + value
Add and assign
-=
x = x - value
Subtract and assign
*=
x = x * value
Multiply and assign
/=
x = x / value
Divide and assign
CORE IDEA
s += g updates s by adding g to its current value, then stores the new value back into s.
EQUIVALENT FORMS
s = s + g;
// same effect
s += g;
INTERVIEW NOTE
Assignment operators are still expressions: the right-hand calculation happens first, then the
updated value is stored in the left-hand variable.

Comparison Operators
Comparison operators are used to form conditions. Their result is typically true or false.

17
Loose vs Strict Equality
The distinction between == and === is an important interview concept.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 17
Equality Operators
Use the source example with values that look the same but have different data types.
EXAMPLE
let d = 22;
let e = "22";
console.log(d == e);
console.log(d === e);
== LOOSE EQUALITY
Allows type coercion in the comparison.
22 == "22" evaluates to true.
=== STRICT EQUALITY
Does not convert the operands before
comparing. 22 === "22" evaluates to
false.
INTERVIEW REMINDER
=== checks both value and type as part of the strict comparison.
Expected output
true
false


18
Relational Operators
Compare whether one value is greater, smaller or equal in a directional sense.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 18
Relational Comparisons
The relational operators from the source are >, <, >= and <=.
SOURCE EXAMPLE
let d = 22;
let e = "22";
console.log(d > e);
console.log(d < e);
console.log(d >= e);
console.log(d <= e);
Expression
Result
Reason
22 > "22"
false
22 is not greater than 22
22 < "22"
false
22 is not less than 22
22 >= "22"
true
Values are equal
22 <= "22"
true
Values are equal
NOTE
Relational comparisons can involve type coercion when the operands have different types. Keep
the result and the rule separate when explaining your answer.


19
Inequality Operators
Check whether values are different using loose or strict comparison.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 19
Inequality
The source demonstrates the same type distinction for != and !==.
EXAMPLE
console.log(10 != "10");
console.log(10 !== "10");
!= LOOSE INEQUALITY
Allows coercion. Because 10 and "10"
compare as equal after coercion, 10 !=
"10" is false.
!== STRICT INEQUALITY
Checks without type conversion. Because
the types differ, 10 !== "10" is true.
REMEMBER
Equality and inequality come in loose and strict pairs: == / != and === / !==.


20
Logical Operators
Combine Boolean conditions to build decisions.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 20
Logical Operators
The three logical operators in this session are &&, || and !.
&&
AND
 Both conditions must be true.
||
OR
At least one condition must be
 true.
!
NOT
 Reverses a Boolean result.
SOURCE EXAMPLE
let hasAge = true;
let hasID = false;
console.log(hasAge && hasID);
console.log(hasAge || hasID);
console.log(!hasID);
Expected output
false
true
true

MEMORY RULE
AND needs all true. OR needs at least one true. NOT simply flips the Boolean value.


22
Logical Operators in Practice
Translate a simple real-world rule into Boolean logic.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 22
Practical Example
The source uses age and ID as two Boolean conditions.
EXAMPLE
let hasAge = true;
let hasID = false;
console.log(hasAge && hasID);
console.log(hasAge || hasID);
console.log(!hasID);
AND
hasAge && hasID
Result: false
Both must be true.
OR
hasAge || hasID
Result: true
At least one is true.
NOT
!hasID
Result: true
false becomes true.
REAL-WORLD PATTERN
Example: Can enter exam? A rule might require both a valid ID and valid registration, which
maps naturally to &&.


23
Short-Circuit Evaluation
Logical operators can stop once the final outcome is already known.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 23
Short-Circuit Evaluation
When JavaScript can already determine the result of a logical expression, it does not need to
evaluate the remaining operand.
AND EXAMPLE
false && something
WHY?
The left side is already false. Because false && anything is false, the final result is known
without needing the right side.
OR EXAMPLE
true || something
WHY?
The left side is already true. Because true || anything is true, the final result is known
immediately.
BEGINNER NOTE
This session only needs the basic rule: logical evaluation can stop early when the final Boolean
outcome is already determined.


24
Ternary Operator
A compact expression that chooses one of two values based on a condition.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 24
Ternary Operator
The ternary operator is the JavaScript operator that takes three operands: a condition, a true
result and a false result.
SYNTAX
condition ? valueIfTrue : valueIfFalse
SOURCE EXAMPLE
let age = 34;
let isEligible =
    age >= 18
        ? "You can Vote"
        : "You are not eligible for voting";
HOW IT WORKS
1. Evaluate the condition.   2. If true, produce the value after ?.   3. Otherwise, produce the
value after :.


25
Ternary vs if-else
The same two-way decision can be expressed in different forms.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 25
Choose a Form
Ternary is an expression that directly produces a value; if-else is a statement used to control
program flow.
TERNARY
let result = age >= 18 ? "Eligible" : "Not Eligible";
IF-ELSE
let result;
if (age >= 18) {
    result = "Eligible";
} else {
    result = "Not Eligible";
}
PRACTICAL RULE
Use a ternary when the decision is short and produces one of two values. Avoid deeply nested
ternaries because they become hard to read.


26
Practice 1 - Positive or Negative
Apply comparison + ternary.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 26
Positive or Negative Number
Determine the sign of a number using a condition and the ternary operator.
SOLUTION FROM SOURCE
let c = -10;
const isPositive =
    c >= 0 ? "Positive" : "Negative";
console.log(isPositive);
LOGIC
Condition: c >= 0. For -10, the condition is false, so the false branch is selected.
Expected output
Negative


27
Practice 2 - Find Largest Number
Apply comparison + ternary.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 27
Find the Larger Value
Compare two numbers and select the larger one.
SOLUTION FROM SOURCE
let f = 10;
let h = 20;
const findLargest =
    f > h ? "f is greater" : "h is greater";
console.log(findLargest);
LOGIC
Condition: f > h. Because 10 > 20 is false, the expression chooses "h is greater".
Expected output
h is greater


28
Practice 3 - Even or Odd
Use modulus + strict equality + ternary.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 28
Even or Odd Number
The remainder after division by 2 is the key to the decision.
SOLUTION FROM SOURCE
let num = 21;
const isEven =
    num % 2 === 0 ? "even" : "odd";
console.log(isEven);
WHY % WORKS
21 % 2 = 1. The remainder is not zero, so the equality check is false and the ternary selects
"odd".
Expected output
odd

32
Answer Key - Questions 1 to 5
Solutions after the practice section.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 32
QUESTION 1
Output: 4, 6, 6. Why: x++ first produces 5 then increments to 6; ++x then increments to 7?
Wait: starting x=5, x++ outputs 5 and x=6, ++x outputs 7, final x=7.
QUESTION 2
Output: true, true. Both variables contain the same numeric value and the same type.
QUESTION 3
Output: 26. Start at 10 → +=5 gives 15 → *=2 gives 30 → -=4 gives 26.
QUESTION 4
Output: true. AND is evaluated first: true && false gives false; false || true gives true.
QUESTION 5
Output: Not Eligible. 17 >= 18 is false, so the false branch is selected.


33
Answer Key - Questions 6 to 13
Solutions for the ten additional exercises.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 33
QUESTION 06
Example: let x = 10; x += 25; → x becomes 35.
QUESTION 07
Example: let x = 30; x -= 10; → x becomes 20.
QUESTION 08
Example: let x = 12; x *= 2; → x becomes 24.
QUESTION 09
Use a === b. The result is true only when the value and type both match.
QUESTION 10
Use a !== b. It is true when the value or type differs under strict comparison.
QUESTION 11
Example: hasPassword && isVerified → true when both are true.
QUESTION 12
Example: isAdmin || isOwner → true when either condition is true.
QUESTION 13
Example: age >= 18 ? "Adult" : "Minor".


34
Challenge Answers
Reasoning matters more than the final output.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 34
Challenge 1
Output: 5, 7, 7. Start x=5 → x++ produces 5 and x becomes 6 → ++x makes x=7 and produces
7 → final x=7.
Challenge 2
Output: true, true. Both a and b are the number 10, so loose and strict equality both succeed.
Challenge 3
Output: 26. 10 + 5 = 15; 15 × 2 = 30; 30 - 4 = 26.
Challenge 4
Output: true. Evaluate AND first: true && false → false. Then false || true → true.
Challenge 5
Output: Not Eligible. The condition 17 >= 18 is false.


35
Interview Revision - Core
Short, direct answers you can revise quickly.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 35
What is an operator?
A symbol or keyword that performs an operation on one or more operands.
What is an operand?
A value used by an operator, such as 10 and 5 in 10 + 5.
What is an expression?
Code that JavaScript evaluates to produce a value.
Difference between == and ===?
== allows type coercion in many comparisons; === performs a strict comparison without
converting the operands.
Difference between != and !==?
!= is loose inequality; !== is strict inequality.
Pre-increment vs post-increment?
++x changes the variable before the value is produced; x++ produces the current value first,
then increments.


36
Interview Revision - Applied
Practical operator questions and concise answers.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 36
What is the difference between += and +?
+ performs addition; += adds and stores the new value back into the variable.
What does % return?
The remainder left after division.
What does && do?
It requires both sides to be truthy for the logical result to be true and can short-circuit when the
result is already known.
What does || do?
It produces a truthy result when at least one side is truthy and can short-circuit when the result
is already known.
What does ! do?
It negates a Boolean result: true becomes false and false becomes true.
What is the ternary operator?
A compact conditional expression written as condition ? trueValue : falseValue.
Why is it called a conditional / ternary operator?
It is called conditional because it chooses a result based on a condition, and ternary because it
has three operands.


37
Quick Revision Sheet
One-page cheat sheet for fast recall.
JavaScript - Session 2 Notes
Page 37
ARITHMETIC
+ - * / % **
INCREMENT / DECREMENT
++x → change first x++ → use first --x →
change first x-- → use first
ASSIGNMENT
= += -= *= /=
COMPARISON
== === != !== > < >= <=
LOGICAL
&& || !
TERNARY
condition ? trueValue : falseValue
IMPORTANT REMINDER
== → loose equality
=== → strict equality
&& → all conditions must be true for a true AND result
|| → at least one condition must be true for a true OR result

03
 Session Roadmap
from symbols to problem solving

Session 1 taught us how to store values. Session 2 is about what we can DO with
those values: calculate, compare, combine conditions and choose a result.

MY RULE FOR THIS SESSION
Study trick: every time an operator appears, ask two questions: "What value
comes in?" and "What value comes out?" That habit makes ++, == and && much
easier to trace.


04
 Operator, Operand, Expression
three words to keep separate
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 4
Return to Index
TINY EXAMPLE
10 + 5
Operator
The symbol that tells
JavaScript what to do.
+ means add.
Operands
The values that the operator
works on.
10 and 5 are the operands.
Expression
A piece of code that
evaluates to a value.
10 + 5 becomes 15.
REMEMBER
An expression can be as small as `5 + 2`, or bigger, like `age >= 18 ? "adult" :
"minor"`. The important part is that JavaScript can evaluate it and obtain a result.


05
 Arithmetic Operators
the operators you use for calculations
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 5
Return to Index
Operator
Name
What it does
Example
+
addition
adds values
8 + 3 -> 11
-
subtraction
subtracts
8 - 3 -> 5
*
multiplication
multiplies
8 * 3 -> 24
/
division
divides
8 / 4 -> 2
%
remainder
gives the remainder
8 % 3 -> 2
**
power
raises a number to a power
2 ** 3 -> 8
++
increment
adds 1
x++
--
decrement
subtracts 1
x--
THREE EASY TRAPS
`/` can produce a decimal. Example: `5 / 2` gives `2.5`. `**` is power, so `3 ** 2` means 3
raised to 2 = 9. `%` is not a percentage operator - it is remainder.


06
 Arithmetic in Action
write the result into a variable, then print it
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 6
Return to Index
BEST BEGINNER EXAMPLE
let price = 120;
let quantity = 3;
let total = price * quantity;
console.log("Total:", total);
HOW TO READ THE CODE
Step 1 -> `price` stores 120. Step 2 -> `quantity` stores 3. Step 3 -> `price * quantity`
calculates 360. Step 4 -> `total` stores 360. Step 5 -> `console.log()` prints it.
QUICK PRACTICE
let a = 2;
let b = 4;
console.log(a + b);
console.log(a - b);
console.log(a * b);
console.log(a / b);
console.log(a ** b);
CHECK YOURSELF
Expected values: 6, -2, 8, 0.5 and 16. Do not memorize the numbers - learn what
each symbol asks JavaScript to calculate.


07
 Remainder Operator (%)
the quickest path to divisibility checks
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 7
Return to Index
START HERE
console.log(10 % 3);
// 1
10 / 3
quotient = 3     remainder = 1
WHY % IS USEFUL
When checking even/odd, divide by 2. If the remainder is 0, the number is even. If
the remainder is 1 (for whole numbers), it is odd.
EVEN / ODD CHECK
let num = 42;
const result = num % 2 === 0;
console.log(result); // true
let other = 21;
console.log(other % 2 === 0); // false
PATTERN TO REMEMBER
The pattern is: `value % divisor === 0`. If the comparison is true, the value is exactly
divisible by that divisor.


08
 Operator Precedence
when more than one operator appears
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 8
Return to Index
FIRST EXAMPLE
let result = 2 + 3 * 4;
console.log(result); // 14
WHAT HAPPENED?
JavaScript does not simply read every operator from left to right. Some operators
are applied before others. Multiplication happens before addition here, so `3 * 4`
becomes 12, then 2 + 12 becomes 14.
USE PARENTHESES WHEN YOU WANT A DIFFERENT GROUPING
let result = (2 + 3) * 4;
console.log(result); // 20
NOTEBOOK RULE
Parentheses are your clarity tool. Even when JavaScript already knows the
precedence, parentheses can make your intention obvious to another programmer.
Small beginner priority list
-
 parentheses `( )`
-
 power `**`
-
 multiplication / division / remainder `* / %`
-
 addition / subtraction `+ -`
-
 comparisons such as `>`, `<`, `===`
-
 logical `&&`, then `||`
-
 ternary `? :`


09
 Increment and Decrement
same symbol, different timing
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 9
Return to Index
CORE IDEA
`++` adds 1. `--` subtracts 1. The important question is not only "what changes?" but
also "when is the new value used?"
Form
What changes?
What value does the expression produce?
++x
x increases first
new value
x++
x increases after
old value
--x
x decreases first
new value
x--
x decreases after
old value
NORMAL USE: JUST CHANGE THE VARIABLE
let score = 10;
score++;
console.log(score);
// 11
GOOD HABIT
Most beginner code is easier to read when you use `score++` or `score += 1` on its
own line. The pre/post difference matters when the expression itself is used to
produce a value.


10
 Pre-Increment (++x)
change first, use second
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 10
Return to Index
TRACE THIS SLOWLY
let x = 2;
console.log(++x);
console.log(x);
Start: x = 2
 1. ++x -> x becomes 3
2. expression value -> 3
3. x stays 3 after the statement
ONE-LINE MEMORY
Think of `++x` as: "increase x, then hand me the updated value."
STORED RESULT EXAMPLE
let points = 9;
let shown = ++points;
console.log(shown);  // 10
console.log(points); // 10


11
 Post-Increment (x++)
use the old value, then change the variable
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 11
Return to Index
TRACE THIS SLOWLY
let x = 4;
console.log(x++);
console.log(x);
Start: x = 4
 1. expression value -> 4
2. then x becomes 5
3. next console.log(x) prints 5
ONE-LINE MEMORY
Think of `x++` as: "give me the current x, then increase x."
STORED RESULT EXAMPLE
let count = 7;
const before = count++;
console.log(before); // 7
console.log(count);  // 8


12
 Pre vs Post - Side by Side
same update, different returned value
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 12
Return to Index
Code
Step 1
Step 2
Value produced
++x
x = x + 1
use x
new x
x++
use x
x = x + 1
old x
--x
x = x - 1
use x
new x
x--
use x
x = x - 1
old x
COMPARE THE OUTPUTS
let n = 5;
console.log(++n); // 6
console.log(n);   // 6
let m = 5;
console.log(m++); // 5
console.log(m);   // 6
IMPORTANT
If `++x` or `x++` is on its own line, both increase x by 1. The difference only becomes
visible when JavaScript needs the value produced by the expression.


13
 Execution Tracing
make a tiny table instead of guessing
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 13
Return to Index
EXAMPLE TO TRACE
let x = 3;
console.log(++x, x++);
console.log(x);
Step
Expression
x before
value produced
x after
1
++x
3
4
4
2
x++
4
4
5
RESULT
The output is `4 4` on the first line and `5` on the second. When a pre/post operator
confuses you, write the variable state before and after.
Tracing recipe
-
 write the starting value
-
 read one operator from left to right
-
 write the value produced
-
 update the variable
-
 move to the next operator


14
 Compound ++ Expression
slow down - one operator at a time
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 14
Return to Index
SOURCE-INSPIRED CHALLENGE
let x = 5;
let y = 5;
const result = x++ + y++ + ++y + y++;
console.log(result);
console.log(y);
Part
value used
variable after
x++
5
x = 6
y++
5
y = 6
++y
7
y = 7
y++
7
y = 8
HOW TO SOLVE IT
Add the values that were USED by each expression: 5 + 5 + 7 + 7 = 24. The final y is
8. Do not try to calculate the whole thing in one jump.
READABLE-CODE NOTE
This kind of dense expression is useful for learning how the operator works, but
clear production code is usually easier to maintain when the updates are split into
separate lines.


15
 Decrement Operators
the same timing rule, but subtract 1
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 15
Return to Index
PRE AND POST DECREMENT TOGETHER
let value = 5;
console.log(--value); // 4
console.log(value--); // 4
console.log(value);    // 3
READ THE ORDER
`--value` changes first, so the expression gives 4. `value--` gives the current 4 first,
then changes the variable to 3.
Form
Meaning
--x
x = x - 1, then use x
x--
use x, then x = x - 1
MIRROR RULE
Increment and decrement are mirror images: `++` adds one, `--` subtracts one. The
pre/post timing rule is identical.


16
 Assignment Operators
calculate and save the new value
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 16
Return to Index
Operator
Same as
Simple meaning
=
x = y
put a value into x
+=
x = x + y
add, then store
-=
x = x - y
subtract, then store
*=
x = x * y
multiply, then store
/=
x = x / y
divide, then store
%=
x = x % y
remainder, then store
**=
x = x ** y
power, then store
SHORT FORM VS LONG FORM
let total = 30;
let extra = 40;
total += extra;
console.log(total); // 70
// same idea written longhand
total = total + extra;
REMEMBER
The shortcut is not a brand-new operation. It is a compact way of saying: calculate
with the current value and assign the result back to the same variable.

READING METHOD
Shortcut thinking: first look at the left variable, then look at the right value, then
perform the operation. Finally store the result back in the left variable.


18
 Comparison Operators
comparisons usually answer with true or false
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 18
Return to Index
Operator
Meaning
Example
==
loose equality
5 == "5" -> true
===
strict equality
5 === "5" -> false
!=
loose inequality
5 != "5" -> false
!==
strict inequality
5 !== "5" -> true
>
greater than
10 > 3 -> true
<
less than
10 < 3 -> false
>=
greater or equal
10 >= 10 -> true
<=
less or equal
10 <= 10 -> true
WHY COMPARISONS MATTER
Comparison operators are the bridge to conditions. Example: `marks >= 40` is a
question. JavaScript evaluates it and gets either true or false.
SMALL CONDITION
let marks = 72;
const passed = marks >= 40;
console.log(passed); // true


19
 Loose vs Strict Equality
the famous == vs === question
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 19
Return to Index
SAME VISIBLE CHARACTERS, DIFFERENT TYPES
console.log(22 == "22");
console.log(22 === "22");
Expression
Result
Why?
22 == "22"
true
loose comparison may convert types
22 === "22"
false
strict comparison keeps the type difference
MENTAL PICTURE
A number `22` and a string `"22"` are not the same type. Strict equality asks for both
a matching value and a matching type.
WHY STRICT EQUALITY IS EASIER TO REASON ABOUT
let userInput = "18";
let age = 18;
console.log(userInput == age);  // true
console.log(userInput === age); // false
STUDY NOTE
For beginner code, using `===` makes the type check visible instead of hiding a
conversion inside the comparison.


20
 Inequality Operators
the opposite side of equality
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 20
Return to Index
SAME PAIR, NOW CHECKING "DIFFERENT"
console.log(10 != "10");
console.log(10 !== "10");
Operator
Think of it as
!=
not equal after loose comparison
!==
not equal under strict comparison
EXAMPLE EXPLAINED
`10 != "10"` is false because loose equality can consider them equal after conversion.
`10 !== "10"` is true because the types differ.
QUICK TEST
const entered = "10";
const expected = 10;
console.log(entered !== expected); // true


21
 Relational Operators
greater, smaller, or equal in one direction
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 21
Return to Index
SAME PAIR AS THE LESSON
let a = 22;
let b = "22";
console.log(a > b);
console.log(a < b);
console.log(a >= b);
console.log(a <= b);
Expression
Result
Plain-language reason
22 > "22"
false
22 is not greater than 22
22 < "22"
false
22 is not smaller than 22
22 >= "22"
true
they compare as equal in value
22 <= "22"
true
they compare as equal in value
IMPORTANT
When different types are compared with `<`, `>`, `<=` or `>=`, JavaScript may convert
values before comparing them. Learn the result AND the rule that produced it.


22
 Logical Operators
combine conditions into one decision
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 22
Return to Index
Operator
Name
Beginner rule
&&
AND
both sides must be true
||
OR
at least one side must be true
!
NOT
flip the Boolean result
REAL-WORLD ACCESS EXAMPLE
let hasPassword = true;
let isVerified = false;
console.log(hasPassword && isVerified); // false
console.log(hasPassword || isVerified); // true
console.log(!isVerified); // true
SIMPLE PICTURE
AND is like a gate with two locks: both conditions must open it. OR is like two
different entry routes: one working route is enough. NOT changes true to false, and
false to true.

the fastest way to memorize boolean combinations

true
false
true
