false
false
false
NOT
A
!A
true
false
false
true
ONE SENTENCE
Memory line: AND needs all true. OR needs at least one true. NOT simply flips
the Boolean value.


24
 Short-Circuit Evaluation
sometimes JavaScript stops early
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 24
Return to Index
WHAT "SHORT-CIRCUIT" MEANS
Logical operators are evaluated from left to right. If the answer is already known,
JavaScript may not evaluate the remaining side.
WATCH THE RIGHT-HAND SIDE
false && console.log("this never runs");
true || console.log("this never runs");
TWO MEMORY PATTERNS
`false && anything` is already false, so there is no reason to evaluate `anything`. `true
|| anything` is already true, so JavaScript can stop there too.
PROOF BY SIDE EFFECT
function check(){
    console.log("check ran");
    return true;
}
console.log(true || check());
WHY YOU SHOULD CARE
The function call on the right is skipped because the left side of `||` is already truthy.
This is useful for understanding both performance and side effects.


25
 Truthy, Falsy and Returned Values
a small but important upgrade to the basic AND/OR rule
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 25
Return to Index
THE DEEPER RULE
In simple Boolean examples, `&&` and `||` look like they return true or false. In real
JavaScript, they can return one of their actual operands. What decides the path is
truthiness.
Value
Falsy?
false
yes
0
yes
""
yes
null
yes
undefined
yes
NaN
yes
42
no
"hello"
no
[]
no
{}
no
LOGICAL OPERATORS CAN SELECT VALUES
console.log("" || "Guest");
// "Guest"
console.log(5 && 8);
// 8
console.log(0 && 8);
// 0
EXAM-SAFE EXPLANATION
For the session-level rule, learn the Boolean cases first. Then remember this
advanced fact: `&&`, `||` and `??` are value-selection operators when used with
non-Boolean operands.


26
 Ternary Operator
a compact way to choose between two values
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 26
Return to Index
BEST BEGINNER EXAMPLE
const age = 21;
const status = age >= 18 ? "Adult" : "Minor";
console.log(status);
condition
 ? 
if true
 : 
if false
HOW TO READ IT
Read it as: "If the condition is true, use the value after `?`. Otherwise, use the value
after `:`." The whole ternary expression produces one value, which can be stored in a
variable.
ANOTHER PRACTICAL EXAMPLE
const marks = 76;
const result = marks >= 40 ? "Pass" : "Fail";
console.log(result); // Pass


27
 Ternary vs if / else
same decision, different shape
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 27
Return to Index
TERNARY
const result = age >= 18 ? "Adult" : "Minor";
IF / ELSE
let result;
if (age >= 18) {
    result = "Adult";
} else {
    result = "Minor";
}
READABLE-CODE RULE
Use a ternary when the decision is short and you simply need one of two values.
Use a normal `if / else` block when the logic has multiple steps or becomes harder
to read.
DO NOT OVERUSE IT
Avoid turning one line into a puzzle by nesting many ternaries inside each other.
Shorter code is not automatically clearer code.


28
 Problem Solving: Positive or Negative
comparison + ternary
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 28
Return to Index
SOLUTION
const number = -10;
const sign = number >= 0 ? "Positive" : "Negative";
console.log(sign);
EXPLAIN THE CODE
`number >= 0` is false for -10, so JavaScript chooses the value after `:`. The final
value is `"Negative"`.
SLIGHTLY BETTER REUSABLE VERSION
function getSign(number){
    return number >= 0 ? "Positive" : "Negative";
}
console.log(getSign(25)); // Positive
console.log(getSign(-3)); // Negative
PATTERN
The important problem-solving pattern is more general than this example: build a
Boolean question first, then decide what text or value should be used when the
answer is true or false.


29
 Problem Solving: Largest Number
comparison + ternary, one decision
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 29
Return to Index
BETTER OUTPUT: RETURN THE ACTUAL NUMBER
const a = 10;
const b = 20;
const larger = a > b ? a : b;
console.log(larger); // 20
READ THE LOGIC
The condition `a > b` is false because 10 is not greater than 20. So the ternary gives
us `b`, which is 20.
REALISTIC EXAMPLE
const first = 45;
const second = 32;
const largest = first > second ? first : second;
console.log("Largest:", largest);
WHY THIS VERSION IS USEFUL
A useful improvement over the source-style example is to return the number itself
rather than only printing which variable is larger. That makes the result reusable
later.


30
 Problem Solving: Even or Odd
modulus + strict equality + ternary
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 30
Return to Index
CLEAN SOLUTION
const number = 21;
const type = number % 2 === 0 ? "Even" : "Odd";
console.log(type);
21 % 2
-> 1
-> 1 === 0 is false
-> Odd
THREE OPERATORS WORKING TOGETHER
The `%` operator finds the remainder. The `=== 0` part turns that remainder into a
Boolean check. The ternary turns the Boolean check into a readable result.
TINY PRACTICE LOOP
for (const n of [12, 17, 40, 55]) {
    const kind = n % 2 === 0 ? "Even" : "Odd";
    console.log(n, kind);
}


31
 Problem Solving: Access Check
logical AND in a real rule
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 31
Return to Index
TWO LOCKS, ONE GATE
const hasPassword = true;
const isVerified = true;
const canEnter = hasPassword && isVerified;
console.log(canEnter); // true
EXPLAIN IT
Because both conditions are true, AND produces true. If either condition becomes
false, the final Boolean result becomes false.
OR EXAMPLE
const isAdmin = false;
const isOwner = true;
const canManage = isAdmin || isOwner;
console.log(canManage); // true
PROBLEM-SOLVING PATTERN
This is how logical operators connect simple checks into a larger rule: one
condition describes one fact; `&&` or `||` describes how those facts must work
together.


32
 Common Mistakes
the things that usually cause wrong answers
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 32
Return to Index
1. Confusing `=` with `===`
`=` assigns. `===` compares.
2. Forgetting that `x++` produces the old value
The variable changes after the expression produces its value.
3. Reading `%` as "percent"
In JavaScript it is the remainder operator.
4. Treating `&&` and `||` as always returning Boolean
With non-Boolean values, they can return an operand.
5. Writing a long ternary puzzle
If it becomes hard to read, use `if / else`.
6. Doing mental math on a compound ++ expression
Trace one operator at a time.
7. Ignoring parentheses
Use them when grouping should be obvious.

35
 Answer Key A
solutions with the reason, not just the output
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 35
Return to Index
1. 18 + 7
The expression evaluates to 25.
2. 17 % 5
2, because 15 goes into the division and 2 remains.
3. 5 then 5?
For `x = 4`, x++ prints 4 and then x becomes 5. The second print is 5.
4. score = score + 10
That is the long form of `score += 10`.
5. == vs ===
`==` may coerce types; `===` compares without converting the operands.
6. true
10 and "10" differ in type, so strict inequality is true.
7. true
The relational comparison can coerce the string "12" to a numeric value for
comparison.
8. number % 2 === 0
A zero remainder means the number is divisible by 2.
9. hasPassword && isVerified
Both facts must be true.
10. age >= 18 ? "Adult" : "Minor"
The condition selects the correct text value.


36
 Answer Key B
the useful reasoning behind the harder set
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 36
Return to Index
11. true
`&&` is evaluated before `||`: true && false -> false; false || true -> true.
12. "Guest"
The empty string is falsy, so OR moves to the right-hand value.
13. 14
Multiplication happens before addition: 3 * 4 = 12, then 2 + 12 = 14.
14. total *= 2
That is the compound-assignment form.
15. a < b ? a : b
If a is smaller, use a; otherwise use b.
16. marks >= 40 ? "Pass" : "Fail"
The comparison creates the decision.
17. 6, 6
Starting at 5: ++x makes 6 and produces 6; x++ then produces 6 and moves x to 7
after the expression.
18. name || "Guest"
When name is falsy, the fallback string is selected.
19. The call may be skipped
If the left side already determines the result, the right side is not evaluated.
20. Example below
Use all four pieces in one small program.


37
 Interview Revision
short answers to say confidently
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 37
Return to Index
What is an operator?
A symbol or keyword that performs an operation on one or more operands.
What is an operand?
A value that an operator works with.
What is an expression?
A piece of code that JavaScript evaluates to produce a value.
What does % do?
It returns the remainder after division.
++x vs x++?
`++x` increments first and produces the new value. `x++` produces the old value first,
then increments.
+= vs +?
`+` calculates a sum. `+=` calculates a sum and stores the new value back in the left
variable.
== vs ===?
Loose equality may coerce types. Strict equality compares without type conversion.
&& vs ||?
AND needs all conditions to be true; OR needs at least one. Both can short-circuit.
What is short-circuiting?
Stopping the evaluation when the final logical result is already known.
What is a ternary?
A three-part expression: condition ? trueValue : falseValue.


38
 One-Page Quick Revision
last-minute scan before a quiz or viva
JavaScript Session 2 - Handwritten Notes
Page 38
Return to Index
ARITHMETIC
+ - * / % **
++ --
ASSIGNMENT
= += -= *= /= %= **=
COMPARISON
== === != !==
> < >= <=
LOGICAL
&& || !
AND / OR / NOT
TERNARY
condition ?
valueIfTrue : valueIfFalse
TRACE
pre -> change first
post -> use first
TEN-SECOND SCAN
Fast memory: `=` assigns. `===` compares. `%` finds remainder. `++x` changes first.
`x++` uses first. `&&` needs all true. `||` needs one true. `!` flips. `?:` chooses one value.
EXAM HABIT
Before answering an output question, write the variable state. Before choosing an
operator, say what you want the program to do in plain English.

console.log("=== Operators Practice ===");

console.log("Before:", score);
console.log("Post:", score++);
console.log("After:", score);
console.log("Pre:", ++score);

points += 20;
points -= 5;
console.log("Points:", points);

WHAT THIS PROGRAM DEMONSTRATES
This final example combines the session ideas into one readable file: arithmetic,
pre/post operators, compound assignment, strict/loose equality, AND, modulus and
ternary. The variables are named by meaning instead of single letters, so the
program is easier to read later.

17
FILE: s3\s3.js
TYPE: Text

/* ==========================================================================
   SESSION 3: MATH OBJECT, SPREAD OPERATOR & CONDITIONAL CONTROL FLOW
   ========================================================================== */

console.log("=== SESSION 3: MATH OBJECT, SPREAD OPERATOR & CONDITIONALS ===");

// ==========================================================================
// 1. BUILT-IN MATH OBJECT METHODS
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * CONCEPT: The Math Object
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   A built-in toolbox in JavaScript that gives you pre-made math formulas 
   and utility functions (like rounding, generating random numbers, and finding powers).
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   A static standard built-in namespace object (not a constructor function) 
   that exposes properties and static methods for mathematical constants and functions.
 */

// Math.random() -> Generates a pseudo-random floating-point number between 0 (inclusive) and 1 (exclusive)
let randomVal = Math.random();
console.log("Random Value (0 to 1):", randomVal);

// Combining Math.random() and Math.floor() to generate a random integer from 0 to 9
// Math.floor() rounds a decimal DOWN to the nearest whole integer
let randomInt = Math.floor(Math.random() * 10);
console.log("Random Integer (0 to 9):", randomInt);

// Math.sqrt(x) -> Calculates the square root of a given number x
let squareRoot = Math.sqrt(16);
console.log("Square Root of 16:", squareRoot); // 4

// Math.pow(base, exponent) -> Calculates base raised to the power of exponent (equivalent to base ** exponent)
let powerVal = Math.pow(2, 5);
console.log("2 raised to power 5:", powerVal); // 32

// Math.min(...values) -> Returns the smallest value among the provided numeric arguments
let minVal = Math.min(10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4);
console.log("Minimum Value:", minVal); // 1

// Math.max(...values) -> Returns the largest value among the provided numeric arguments
let maxVal = Math.max(10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4);
console.log("Maximum Value:", maxVal); // 44


// ==========================================================================
// 2. SPREAD OPERATOR WITH MATH METHODS
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * CONCEPT: Spread Operator (...) with Functions
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Unpacks an array of values into individual standalone items.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   An ES6 syntax feature that expands an iterable (like an Array) into individual 
   arguments in a function call or elements in an array literal.
 */

let numberList = [10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4];

// Math.max/min expect individual comma-separated arguments, not a single array instance.
// Using '...numberList' unpacks the array elements into individual parameters.
let arrayMax = Math.max(...numberList);
console.log("Max using Spread Operator:", arrayMax); // 44

let arrayMin = Math.min(...numberList);
console.log("Min using Spread Operator:", arrayMin); // 1


// ==========================================================================
// 3. CONDITIONAL STATEMENTS (if / else & Ternary)
// ==========================================================================

/* 
 * CONCEPT: Conditional Statements (if / else if / else)
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Decision-making statements used to run different blocks of code depending on whether a condition is true or false.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   Control flow statements that evaluate boolean expressions to determine which branch of code to execute during runtime.
 */

let checkAge = 18;

// Standard if-else statement
if (checkAge >= 18) {
    console.log("You are eligible to vote");
} else {
    console.log("You are not eligible to vote");
}

/* 
 * CONCEPT: Ternary Operator
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   A one-line shortcut for writing an if-else condition: (condition ? trueResult : falseResult).
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   An inline conditional expression accepting three operands, returning a value based on boolean evaluation.
 */

let ternaryAge = 18;
let voteEligibility = (ternaryAge >= 18) ? "You are eligible to vote" : "You are not eligible to vote";
console.log("Ternary Result:", voteEligibility);


// ==========================================================================
// 4. PRACTICE EXERCISES & BUG FIXES
// ==========================================================================

console.log("\n--- Practice Questions ---");

// --- Question 3: Voting Eligibility with Input Validation ---
let userAge = 28;

if (userAge < 0) {
    console.log("Q3: Age must be a positive number.");
} else if (userAge >= 18) {
    console.log("Q3: Eligible to Vote");
} else {
    console.log("Q3: Not eligible to vote");
}


// --- Question 4: Largest of Two Numbers (Ternary) ---
let n1 = 23;
let n2 = 34;
let largerNum = (n1 > n2) ? n1 : n2;
console.log("Q4: Larger number is ->", largerNum);


// --- Question 5: Largest of Three Numbers using Math.max() ---
let num1 = 23;
let num2 = 34;
let num3 = 56;

let largestOfThree = Math.max(num1, num2, num3);
console.log("Q5: Largest of three numbers ->", largestOfThree);


// --- Question 6: Divisibility by both 3 and 5 ---
let testNumber1 = 15;
const divA1 = 3;
const divB1 = 5;

if (testNumber1 % divA1 === 0 && testNumber1 % divB1 === 0) {
    console.log("Q6: Number is divisible by BOTH 3 and 5.");
} else if (testNumber1 % divA1 === 0 || testNumber1 % divB1 === 0) {
    console.log("Q6: Number is divisible by ONLY ONE of the numbers.");
} else {
    console.log("Q6: Number is not divisible by any of the numbers.");
}


// --- Question 7: Divisibility by 5 and 10 ---
let testNumber2 = 15;
const fNum1 = 5;
const sNum1 = 10;

if (testNumber2 % fNum1 === 0 && testNumber2 % sNum1 === 0) {
    console.log("Q7: Number is divisible by BOTH 5 and 10.");
} else if (testNumber2 % fNum1 === 0 || testNumber2 % sNum1 === 0) {
    console.log("Q7: Number is divisible by ONLY ONE of the numbers (5 or 10).");
} else {
    console.log("Q7: Number is not divisible by any of the numbers.");
}


// --- Question 8: Student Grading System ---
let marks = 99;
let total = 100;
let studentGrade = "";

if (marks >= 0 && marks <= total) {
    if (marks >= 90) {
        studentGrade = 'A+';
    } else if (marks >= 70) {
        studentGrade = 'A';
    } else if (marks >= 40) {
        studentGrade = 'B';
    } else if (marks >= 30) {
        studentGrade = 'C';
    } else {
        console.log("Q8: Student has Failed.");
    }

    if (studentGrade) {
        console.log("Q8: Student Grade is =>", studentGrade);
    }
} else if (marks < 0) {
    console.log("Q8: Marks must be positive.");
} else {
    console.log("Q8: Marks cannot be greater than total marks!");
}


// --- Question 9: Categorize Person by Age (Child, Teenager, Adult) ---
let personAge = 0;

if (personAge <= 0) {
    console.log("Q9: Age must be a positive integer.");
} else if (personAge < 12) {
    console.log("Q9: Child");
} else if (personAge <= 18) {
    console.log("Q9: Teenager");
} else {
    console.log("Q9: Adult");
}


// --- Question 10: Check if Number is between 10 and 50 ---
let n3 = 34;

if (n3 >= 10 && n3 <= 50) {
    console.log("Q10:", n3, "is within the range of 10 to 50");
} else {
    console.log("Q10:", n3, "is outside the range of 10 to 50");
}


// --- Question 11: Check Username and Password ---
let inputUsername = "admin_user";
let inputPassword = "SecurePassword123";

const validUsername = "admin_user";
const validPassword = "SecurePassword123";

if (inputUsername === validUsername && inputPassword === validPassword) {
    console.log("Q11: Username and password are correct.");
} else {
    console.log("Q11: Invalid username or password.");
}


// --- Question 12: Restricted Area Access (Age 18+ and ID) ---
let visitorAge = 20;
let hasValidID = true;

if (visitorAge >= 18 && hasValidID) {
    console.log("Q12: Access granted to restricted area.");
} else {
    console.log("Q12: Access denied. Must be 18+ and possess an ID.");
}


// --- Question 13: Check if Number is Positive and Even ---
let checkNumPositiveEven = 14;

if (checkNumPositiveEven > 0 && checkNumPositiveEven % 2 === 0) {
    console.log("Q13:", checkNumPositiveEven, "is Positive and Even.");
} else {
    console.log("Q13:", checkNumPositiveEven, "is NOT both Positive and Even.");
}


// --- Question 14: Check if Number is Negative or Odd ---
let checkNumNegOdd = -7;

if (checkNumNegOdd < 0 || checkNumNegOdd % 2 !== 0) {
    console.log("Q14:", checkNumNegOdd, "is Negative or Odd.");
} else {
    console.log("Q14:", checkNumNegOdd, "is neither Negative nor Odd (It is Positive and Even).");
}


// --- Question 15: Temperature Classification (Hot, Normal, Cold) ---
let tempCelsius = 28;

if (tempCelsius > 35) {
    console.log("Q15: Temperature Classification -> Hot");
} else if (tempCelsius >= 15) {
    console.log("Q15: Temperature Classification -> Normal");
} else {
    console.log("Q15: Temperature Classification -> Cold");
}


// --- Question 16: Ticket Type via Ternary Operator ---
let ticketAge = 10;
let ticketType = (ticketAge < 12) ? "Child Ticket" : "Adult Ticket";
console.log("Q16: Ticket Assigned ->", ticketType);


// --- Question 17: Admin Panel Access Check ---
let isLoggedIn = true;
let isAdmin = true;

if (isLoggedIn && isAdmin) {
    console.log("Q17: Access granted to Admin Panel.");
} else {
    console.log("Q17: Access denied. User must be logged in AND have Admin status.");
}


// --- Question 18: Check if Passed All Subjects (Passing Marks = 35) ---
let mathMarks = 45;
let scienceMarks = 38;
let englishMarks = 50;
const passingMarks = 35;

if (mathMarks >= passingMarks && scienceMarks >= passingMarks && englishMarks >= passingMarks) {
    console.log("Q18: Student has passed all subjects.");
} else {
    console.log("Q18: Student has failed one or more subjects.");
}


// --- Question 19: Positive, Negative, or Zero Check ---
let valueToCheck = 0;

if (valueToCheck > 0) {
    console.log("Q19:", valueToCheck, "is Positive.");
} else if (valueToCheck < 0) {
    console.log("Q19:", valueToCheck, "is Negative.");
} else {
    console.log("Q19: Number is Zero.");
}


// --- Question 20: ATM Withdrawal Simulation ---
let correctPin = 4567;
let withdrawalAmount = 10000;
let accountBalance = 50000;

// Prompt execution conditional on environment availability
if (typeof prompt !== "undefined") {
    let enteredPin = Number(prompt("Enter PIN:"));

    if (enteredPin === correctPin) {
        console.log("Q20: PIN is correct.");

        if (withdrawalAmount > 0 && withdrawalAmount <= accountBalance) {
            accountBalance -= withdrawalAmount;
            console.log("Q20: Withdrawal successful! Withdrawn Amount:", withdrawalAmount);
            console.log("Q20: Remaining Balance:", accountBalance);
        } else if (withdrawalAmount > accountBalance) {
            console.log("Q20: Insufficient Balance!");
        } else {
            console.log("Q20: Invalid withdrawal amount!");
        }
    } else {
        console.log("Q20: Pin is Incorrect. Cannot Withdraw.");
    }
}

JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 3
03
Session Overview
What this session adds after operators and basic conditions.
Session 3 builds practical control-flow skills around math
utilities, array expansion and decisions.
This session introduces three connected ideas: the built-in Math object, the spread operator and
conditional control flow.
Learning path
Math helpers -> unpack values -> make decisions -> solve practice problems.
Area
What you learn
Math Object
Generate random values, round down, find square roots, powers, minimums and
maximums.
Spread Operator
Expand an array into individual arguments for a function call.
Conditionals
Run different code depending on whether a condition is true or false.
Practice
Apply the ideas to validation, grading, access checks, ranges and simple simulations.
Session focus
The notes keep the source terminology and examples, while presenting each major concept on its own page for
focused study.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 4
04
Math Object
A built-in toolbox for common mathematical operations.
The Math object exposes pre-made mathematical constants and
static methods.
Simple words
A built-in toolbox in JavaScript that gives you pre-made math formulas and utility functions such as rounding,
generating random numbers and finding powers.
Interview prep
A static standard built-in namespace object (not a constructor function) that exposes properties and static
methods for mathematical constants and functions.
Code
Math.random();
Math.floor(value);
Math.sqrt(value);
Math.pow(base, exponent);
Math.min(a, b, c);
Math.max(a, b, c);
Remember
Math methods are called on the Math object. They are ready-made tools; you do not create a Math instance
with new.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 5
05
Math.random()
Generate a pseudo-random floating-point value.
Math.random() returns a value from 0 (inclusive) up to, but not
including, 1.
Code
let randomVal = Math.random();
console.log("Random Value (0 to 1):", randomVal);
How it works
The result is a floating-point number in the range [0, 1). The lower bound can be exactly 0, while 1 is not
returned.
Example output
Random Value (0 to 1): 0.538214...
Important
The exact value changes on each execution. Use Math.random() when the problem needs a pseudo-random
value, not a fixed constant.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 6
06
Math.floor() + Random Integer
Round down and build a random integer from 0 to 9.
Math.floor() rounds a decimal down to the nearest whole
number. Combined with Math.random() * 10, it produces an
integer from 0 through 9.
Code
let randomInt = Math.floor(Math.random() * 10);
console.log("Random Integer (0 to 9):", randomInt);
Step by step
Math.random() produces a value from 0 up to 1. Multiplying by 10 produces a value from 0 up to 10. Math.floor()
removes the decimal part by rounding downward.
Example output
Random Integer (0 to 9): 7
Remember
Math.floor() always moves toward the lower whole number. This is different from normal rounding.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 7
07
Math.sqrt()
Calculate the square root of a number.
Math.sqrt(x) returns the square root of x.
Code
let squareRoot = Math.sqrt(16);
console.log("Square Root of 16:", squareRoot);
Expected output
Square Root of 16: 4
Concept
The square root of 16 is 4 because 4 * 4 = 16.
Use case
Useful when a problem asks you to calculate the root of a numeric value without manually implementing the
operation.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 8
08
Math.pow()
Raise a base to a power.
Math.pow(base, exponent) calculates base raised to the given
exponent.
Code
let powerVal = Math.pow(2, 5);
console.log("2 raised to power 5:", powerVal);
Expected output
2 raised to power 5: 32
Expression
Meaning
Result
Math.pow(2, 5)
2 raised to the 5th power
32
2 ** 5
Equivalent exponentiation expression
32
Source note
The session explicitly states that Math.pow(base, exponent) is equivalent to the exponentiation operator ** for
the example shown.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 9
09
Math.min()
Find the smallest value among multiple arguments.
Math.min(...values) returns the smallest numeric value passed
as an argument.
Code
let minVal = Math.min(10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4);
console.log("Minimum Value:", minVal);
Expected output
Minimum Value: 1
Important
Math.min() expects separate numeric arguments. An array needs to be expanded with the spread operator
before being passed to Math.min().


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 10
10
Math.max()
Find the largest value among multiple arguments.
Math.max(...values) returns the largest numeric value passed as
an argument.
Code
let maxVal = Math.max(10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4);
console.log("Maximum Value:", maxVal);
Expected output
Maximum Value: 44
Connection
Math.max() becomes especially useful with the spread operator when the values are already stored in an array.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 11
11
Spread Operator (...)
Expand iterable values into individual items.
The spread operator unpacks an array so its elements can be
used as individual arguments in a function call.
Simple words
Unpacks an array of values into individual standalone items.
Interview prep
An ES6 syntax feature that expands an iterable, such as an Array, into individual arguments in a function call or
elements in an array literal.
Code
const numberList = [10, 20, 30];
Math.max(...numberList);
Mental model
Read ...numberList as: "take the items inside numberList and pass them one by one here."


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 12
12
Spread with Math.max() / Math.min()
Use an array with functions that expect separate arguments.
Spread solves the mismatch between an array value and a
function call that expects individual arguments.
Code
let numberList = [10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4];
let arrayMax = Math.max(...numberList);
console.log("Max using Spread Operator:", arrayMax);
let arrayMin = Math.min(...numberList);
console.log("Min using Spread Operator:", arrayMin);
Expected output
Max using Spread Operator: 44
Min using Spread Operator: 1
Key idea
Without spread, numberList is one array argument. With spread, the call receives 10, 20, 30, ... as individual
arguments.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 13
13
Conditional Statements
Decision-making control flow with if / else if / else.
Conditional statements choose which block of code to run
based on whether a condition is true or false.
Simple words
Decision-making statements used to run different blocks of code depending on whether a condition is true or
false.
Interview prep
Control flow statements that evaluate boolean expressions to determine which branch of code to execute during
runtime.
Code
if (condition) {
    // runs when condition is true
} else if (anotherCondition) {
    // runs when the next condition is true
} else {
    // runs when earlier conditions are false
}
Flow
Condition -> choose a branch -> run that block. Once a matching branch is selected, later else-if / else blocks
are skipped.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 14
14
if / else Example
Check voting eligibility using a condition.
The if block runs for an age of 18 or above; otherwise the else
block runs.
Code
let checkAge = 18;
if (checkAge >= 18) {
    console.log("You are eligible to vote");
} else {
    console.log("You are not eligible to vote");
}
Expected output
You are eligible to vote
How to read it
checkAge >= 18 is the condition. If it evaluates to true, the first block executes. Otherwise, the else block
executes.
Important
A condition used in if / else evaluates to a Boolean decision: true or false.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 15
15
Ternary Operator
A compact conditional expression that returns a value.
The ternary operator is an inline form of a two-way condition.
Simple words
A one-line shortcut for writing an if-else condition: condition ? trueResult : falseResult.
Interview prep
An inline conditional expression accepting three operands, returning a value based on boolean evaluation.
Code
let ternaryAge = 18;
let voteEligibility = (ternaryAge >= 18)
    ? "You are eligible to vote"
    : "You are not eligible to vote";
console.log("Ternary Result:", voteEligibility);
Expected output
Ternary Result: You are eligible to vote
Pattern
condition ? valueWhenTrue : valueWhenFalse


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 16
16
if / else vs Ternary
Same decision idea, two different styles.
Use if / else for multi-step or multi-branch logic; use ternary
when a simple condition selects between two values.
Approach
Pattern
Best fit in this session
if / else
if (condition) { ... } else { ... }
Running different blocks of code.
ternary
condition ? trueValue : falseValue
Choosing one value from two alternatives.
Source example
The session uses ternaryAge >= 18 to assign voteEligibility to one of two strings.
Remember
The ternary operator is an expression that produces a value. The source describes it as a one-line shortcut for
if-else.

17
Practice Q3 - Voting Eligibility with
Validation
Source practice exercise
Check that the age is valid before deciding voting eligibility.
Code
let userAge = 28;
if (userAge < 0) {
    console.log("Q3: Age must be a positive number.");
} else if (userAge >= 18) {
    console.log("Q3: Eligible to Vote");
} else {
    console.log("Q3: Not eligible to vote");
}
How it works
The first condition rejects negative ages. If the age is valid and at least 18, the second branch runs. Otherwise
the final else branch runs.
Expected output
Q3: Eligible to Vote
Remember
This example combines validation with if / else if / else.

Code
let n1 = 23;
let n2 = 34;
let largerNum = (n1 > n2) ? n1 : n2;
console.log("Q4: Larger number is ->", largerNum);
How it works
If n1 is greater than n2, largerNum receives n1. Otherwise it receives n2.
Expected output
Q4: Larger number is -> 34

Practice Q5 - Largest of Three Numbers
Source practice exercise
Use Math.max() to find the largest value among three numbers.
Code
let num1 = 23;
let num2 = 34;
let num3 = 56;
let largestOfThree = Math.max(num1, num2, num3);
console.log("Q5: Largest of three numbers ->", largestOfThree);
How it works
Math.max() compares all supplied numeric arguments and returns the highest value.
Expected output
Q5: Largest of three numbers -> 56


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 20
20
Practice Q6 - Divisibility by 3 and 5
Source practice exercise
Use modulus plus logical operators to decide whether a number
is divisible by both, only one, or neither.
Code
let testNumber1 = 15;
const divA1 = 3;
const divB1 = 5;
if (testNumber1 % divA1 === 0 && testNumber1 % divB1 === 0) {
    console.log("Q6: Number is divisible by BOTH 3 and 5.");
} else if (testNumber1 % divA1 === 0 || testNumber1 % divB1 === 0) {
    console.log("Q6: Number is divisible by ONLY ONE of the numbers.");
} else {
    console.log("Q6: Number is not divisible by any of the numbers.");
}
How it works
A remainder of 0 means the number is divisible by the divisor. && checks whether both divisibility tests are true;
|| checks whether at least one is true.
Expected output
Q6: Number is divisible by BOTH 3 and 5.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 21
21
Practice Q7 - Divisibility by 5 and 10
Source practice exercise
Apply the same logical pattern to two different divisors.
Code
let testNumber2 = 15;
const fNum1 = 5;
const sNum1 = 10;
if (testNumber2 % fNum1 === 0 && testNumber2 % sNum1 === 0) {
    console.log("Q7: Number is divisible by BOTH 5 and 10.");
} else if (testNumber2 % fNum1 === 0 || testNumber2 % sNum1 === 0) {
    console.log("Q7: Number is divisible by ONLY ONE of the numbers (5 or 10).");
} else {
    console.log("Q7: Number is not divisible by any of the numbers.");
}
How it works
The same modulus-plus-logical pattern is reused with 5 and 10. For 15, only division by 5 leaves remainder 0.
Expected output
Q7: Number is divisible by ONLY ONE of the numbers (5 or 10).

22
Practice Q8 - Student Grading System
Source practice exercise
Validate marks first, then assign the grade using nested
conditions.
Code
let marks = 99;
let total = 100;
let studentGrade = "";
if (marks >= 0 && marks <= total) {
    if (marks >= 90) {
        studentGrade = 'A+';
    } else if (marks >= 70) {
        studentGrade = 'A';
    } else if (marks >= 40) {
        studentGrade = 'B';
    } else if (marks >= 30) {
        studentGrade = 'C';
    } else {
        console.log("Q8: Student has Failed.");
    }
    if (studentGrade) {
        console.log("Q8: Student Grade is =>", studentGrade);
    }
} else if (marks < 0) {
    console.log("Q8: Marks must be positive.");
} else {
    console.log("Q8: Marks cannot be greater than total marks!");
}
How it works
The outer condition validates the marks range. Inside it, the nested if / else-if chain chooses the grade. For 99
marks, the first grade rule matches.
Expected output
Q8: Student Grade is => A+


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 23
23
Practice Q9 - Categorize Person by Age
Source practice exercise
Classify a person as child, teenager or adult using else-if
branches.
Code
let personAge = 0;
if (personAge <= 0) {
    console.log("Q9: Age must be a positive integer.");
} else if (personAge < 12) {
    console.log("Q9: Child");
} else if (personAge <= 18) {
    console.log("Q9: Teenager");
} else {
    console.log("Q9: Adult");
}
How it works
The first condition validates the age. Because personAge is 0, the validation branch is selected before the
category checks.
Expected output
Q9: Age must be a positive integer.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes

24
Practice Q10 - Range Check
Source practice exercise
Check whether a number is between 10 and 50, inclusive.
Code
let n3 = 34;
if (n3 >= 10 && n3 <= 50) {
    console.log("Q10:", n3, "is within the range of 10 to 50");
} else {
    console.log("Q10:", n3, "is outside the range of 10 to 50");
}
How it works
The number must satisfy both bounds. && makes the two comparisons one combined condition.
Expected output
Q10: 34 is within the range of 10 to 50


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 25
25
Practice Q11 - Username and Password
Source practice exercise

    console.log("Q11: Username and password are correct.");
} else {
    console.log("Q11: Invalid username or password.");
}
How it works
Both the username and password comparisons must be true. Strict equality is used so the compared values
and types must match.
Expected output
Q11: Username and password are correct.

34
Practice Q20 - ATM Withdrawal
Simulation
Source practice exercise
Validate the PIN first, then validate the withdrawal amount and
update the balance.
Code
let correctPin = 4567;
let withdrawalAmount = 10000;
let accountBalance = 50000;
if (typeof prompt !== "undefined") {
    let enteredPin = Number(prompt("Enter PIN:"));
    if (enteredPin === correctPin) {
        console.log("Q20: PIN is correct.");
        if (withdrawalAmount > 0 && withdrawalAmount <= accountBalance) {
            accountBalance -= withdrawalAmount;
            console.log("Q20: Withdrawal successful! Withdrawn Amount:", withdrawalAmount);
            console.log("Q20: Remaining Balance:", accountBalance);
        } else if (withdrawalAmount > accountBalance) {
            console.log("Q20: Insufficient Balance!");
        } else {
            console.log("Q20: Invalid withdrawal amount!");
        }
    } else {
        console.log("Q20: Pin is Incorrect. Cannot Withdraw.");
    }
}
How it works
The source checks whether prompt exists before requesting input. After the PIN is converted to a number, strict
equality validates it. A nested condition then checks the withdrawal amount against the balance.
Expected output
Browser example with correct PIN 4567: PIN is correct; withdrawal successful;
remaining balance = 40000.
Important
The source intentionally guards prompt() with typeof prompt !== "undefined" so the example does not fail in
environments where prompt is unavailable.

JavaScript - Session 3 Notes

40
Interview Revision
Short answers for common Session 3 questions.
Keep these answers concise enough to revise before an
interview or viva.
What is the Math object?
A built-in namespace object that provides mathematical constants and static methods.
What does Math.random() return?
A pseudo-random floating-point number from 0 inclusive to 1 exclusive.
What does Math.floor() do?
It rounds a number down to the nearest whole number.
What does the spread operator do?
It expands an iterable such as an array into individual arguments in a function call.
What is a conditional statement?
A control-flow construct that chooses which branch of code to execute based on a condition.
What is the ternary operator?
An inline conditional expression with three operands that returns one of two values.

a raised to b

Smallest argument

Largest argument

Unpack iterable values

Choose a code branch
ternary

Both conditions must be true

At least one condition must be true

Core flow

Session Summary
What you should be able to do after Session 3.
Session 3 connects built-in Math tools with data expansion and
conditional decision-making.
  Use Math.random() to generate pseudo-random floating-point values.
  Build random integers with Math.floor().
  Calculate square roots and powers with Math.sqrt() and Math.pow().
  Find minimum and maximum values with Math.min() and Math.max().
  Use the spread operator to pass array elements as individual function arguments.
  Write if / else if / else decision logic.
  Use the ternary operator when a condition selects between two values.
  Combine comparisons, modulus and logical operators inside practical conditions.
  Trace validation, grading, access and simulation-style problems.
 
Ready for the next session?
You should now be able to read a small problem, identify the needed Math method or condition, and translate
the logic into JavaScript step by step.

   SESSION 3: MATH OBJECT, SPREAD OPERATOR & CONDITIONAL CONTROL FLOW
   ========================================================================== */
console.log("=== SESSION 3: MATH OBJECT, SPREAD OPERATOR & CONDITIONALS ===");
// ==========================================================================
// 1. BUILT-IN MATH OBJECT METHODS
// ==========================================================================
/* 
 * CONCEPT: The Math Object
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   A built-in toolbox in JavaScript that gives you pre-made math formulas 
   and utility functions (like rounding, generating random numbers, and finding powers).
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   A static standard built-in namespace object (not a constructor function) 
   that exposes properties and static methods for mathematical constants and functions.


JavaScript - Session 3 Notes
Page 49
44
Clean Session Code
Source appendix - original Session 3 code formatted for reading.
 */
// Math.random() -> Generates a pseudo-random floating-point number between 0 (inclusive) and 1 (exclusive)
let randomVal = Math.random();
console.log("Random Value (0 to 1):", randomVal);
// Combining Math.random() and Math.floor() to generate a random integer from 0 to 9
// Math.floor() rounds a decimal DOWN to the nearest whole integer
let randomInt = Math.floor(Math.random() * 10);
console.log("Random Integer (0 to 9):", randomInt);
// Math.sqrt(x) -> Calculates the square root of a given number x
let squareRoot = Math.sqrt(16);
console.log("Square Root of 16:", squareRoot); // 4
// Math.pow(base, exponent) -> Calculates base raised to the power of exponent (equivalent to base ** exponent)
let powerVal = Math.pow(2, 5);
console.log("2 raised to power 5:", powerVal); // 32
// Math.min(...values) -> Returns the smallest value among the provided numeric arguments

let maxVal = Math.max(10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4);
console.log("Maximum Value:", maxVal); // 44

 * CONCEPT: Spread Operator (...) with Functions
 * 
 * Simple Words Definition: 
   Unpacks an array of values into individual standalone items.
 * 
 * Interview Prep Definition: 
   An ES6 syntax feature that expands an iterable (like an Array) into individual

Clean Session Code

   arguments in a function call or elements in an array literal.
 */
let numberList = [10, 20, 30, 32, 44, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4];
// Math.max/min expect individual comma-separated arguments, not a single array instance.
// Using '...numberList' unpacks the array elements into individual parameters.
let arrayMax = Math.max(...numberList);
console.log("Max using Spread Operator:", arrayMax); // 44
let arrayMin = Math.min(...numberList);
console.log("Min using Spread Operator:", arrayMin); // 1

let englishMarks = 50;
const passingMarks = 35;
if (mathMarks >= passingMarks && scienceMarks >= passingMarks && englishMarks >= passingMarks) {
    console.log("Q18: Student has passed all subjects.");
} else {
    console.log("Q18: Student has failed one or more subjects.");
}
// --- Question 19: Positive, Negative, or Zero Check ---
let valueToCheck = 0;
if (valueToCheck > 0) {
    console.log("Q19:", valueToCheck, "is Positive.");
} else if (valueToCheck < 0) {
    console.log("Q19:", valueToCheck, "is Negative.");

Clean Session Code

} else {
    console.log("Q19: Number is Zero.");
}
// --- Question 20: ATM Withdrawal Simulation ---
let correctPin = 4567;
let withdrawalAmount = 10000;
let accountBalance = 50000;
// Prompt execution conditional on environment availability
if (typeof prompt !== "undefined") {
    let enteredPin = Number(prompt("Enter PIN:"));
    if (enteredPin === correctPin) {
        console.log("Q20: PIN is correct.");
        if (withdrawalAmount > 0 && withdrawalAmount <= accountBalance) {
            accountBalance -= withdrawalAmount;
            console.log("Q20: Withdrawal successful! Withdrawn Amount:", withdrawalAmount);

            console.log("Q20: Remaining Balance:", accountBalance);
        } else if (withdrawalAmount > accountBalance) {
            console.log("Q20: Insufficient Balance!");
        } else {
            console.log("Q20: Invalid withdrawal amount!");
        }
    } else {
        console.log("Q20: Pin is Incorrect. Cannot Withdraw.");
    }
}

03 Session roadmap
Math tools -> spread values -> make decisions -> solve practical problems.
1. MATH OBJECT
A ready-made toolbox for common calculations.
2. SPREAD (...)
Open an array and place its items one-by-one where JavaScript expects separate values.
3. CONDITIONALS
Ask a true/false question and run the branch that matches.
The reference lesson follows this same sequence and then turns the ideas into validation, grading, access,
range and simulation problems.
Study habit
Before writing code, say the rule in plain English. Example: 'The person needs age 18+ AND a valid ID.' Then
translate each part into a condition.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 4
RETURN TO INDEX
04 Math Object
A built-in toolbox for mathematical constants and static methods.
Think of Math like a calculator shelf that already exists inside JavaScript. You call the methods directly.
Common forms
M a t h . r a n d o m ( ) ; 
M a t h . f l o o r ( 8 . 9 ) ; 
M a t h . s q r t ( 8 1 ) ; 
M a t h . p o w ( 2 ,  5 ) ; 
M a t h . m i n ( 4 ,  9 ,  1 ) ; 
M a t h . m a x ( 4 ,  9 ,  1 ) ; 
Remember
Math is not something you create with new. Its methods are called directly on Math.
MDN confirms Math is a namespace-style built-in object whose properties and methods are static.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 5
RETURN TO INDEX
05 Math.random()
Returns a pseudo-random floating-point value from 0 inclusive to 1 exclusive.
c o n s t  v a l u e  =  M a t h . r a n d o m ( ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( v a l u e ) ; 
Memory rule
0 <= value < 1
You may see 0.14, 0.73 or 0.999... The exact value changes between runs.
Security note
Math.random() is not for cryptographic security. Use a Web Crypto API when security-grade randomness is
needed.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 6
RETURN TO INDEX
06 Random integer patterns
Turn Math.random() into the ranges you actually need.
/ /  0 . . 9 
c o n s t  d i g i t  =  M a t h . f l o o r ( M a t h . r a n d o m ( )  *  1 0 ) ; 
/ /  1 . . 6  ( d i c e ) 
c o n s t  d i c e  =  M a t h . f l o o r ( M a t h . r a n d o m ( )  *  6 )  +  1 ; 
/ /  1 . . 1 0 0 
c o n s t  s c o r e  =  M a t h . f l o o r ( M a t h . r a n d o m ( )  *  1 0 0 )  +  1 ; 
1..6 step-by-step
random() -> 0 <= x < 1
x * 6 -> 0 <= x < 6
floor -> 0,1,2,3,4,5
+1 -> 1,2,3,4,5,6

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 7
RETURN TO INDEX
07 Math.floor()
Always rounds down toward the lower integer.
M a t h . f l o o r ( 4 . 9 ) ;    / /  4 
M a t h . f l o o r ( 4 . 1 ) ;    / /  4 
M a t h . f l o o r ( - 4 . 1 ) ;   / /  - 5 
Easy trap
Do not think 'floor = remove decimals'. For negative values, floor can move farther from zero.
Formally, Math.floor(x) returns the greatest integer that is less than or equal to x.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 8
RETURN TO INDEX
08 Math.sqrt()
Find the positive square root.
c o n s t  r o o t  =  M a t h . s q r t ( 1 4 4 ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( r o o t ) ;  / /  1 2 
Think it like this
What number multiplied by itself gives 144? 12 × 12 = 144, so the answer is 12.
Real use
/ /  D i s t a n c e  b e t w e e n  t w o  p o i n t s 
c o n s t  d  =  M a t h . s q r t ( ( x 2 - x 1 ) * * 2  +  ( y 2 - y 1 ) * * 2 ) ; 

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 9
RETURN TO INDEX
09 Math.pow()
Raise a base to an exponent.
M a t h . p o w ( 2 ,  5 ) ;  / /  3 2 
/ /  m o d e r n  e q u i v a l e n t 
2  * *  5 ;           / /  3 2 
Read Math.pow(2, 5) as '2 to the power 5'.
Interview tip
Math.pow(base, exponent) and ** both express exponentiation; ** is the shorter modern form.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 10
RETURN TO INDEX
10 Math.min()
Return the smallest value from the arguments.
c o n s t  s m a l l e s t  =  M a t h . m i n ( 1 8 ,  4 ,  2 9 ,  1 1 ,  7 ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( s m a l l e s t ) ;  / /  4 
Important
Math.min() receives individual numeric arguments. An array needs to be expanded first.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 11
RETURN TO INDEX
11 Math.max()
Return the largest value from the arguments.
c o n s t  l a r g e s t  =  M a t h . m a x ( 1 8 ,  4 ,  2 9 ,  1 1 ,  7 ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( l a r g e s t ) ;  / /  2 9 
When the numbers already live inside an array, spread connects naturally with Math.max().

12 Math quick table
Fast recall before solving questions.
Method Meaning Example
Math.random() 0 <= value < 1 Math.random()
Math.floor(x) round down Math.floor(4.9) -> 4
Math.sqrt(x) square root Math.sqrt(81) -> 9
Math.pow(a,b) a raised to b 2 ** 5 -> 32
Math.min(...) smallest Math.min(8,2,5) -> 2
Math.max(...) largest Math.max(8,2,5) -> 8
Memory line
random -> floor -> sqrt -> pow -> min/max

13 Spread operator (...)
Expand an iterable such as an array into individual items.
c o n s t  m a r k s  =  [ 7 2 ,  8 8 ,  9 1 ] ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( . . . m a r k s ) ; 
/ /  7 2  8 8  9 1 
Mental model
Read ...marks as: 'take the items inside marks and put them here one-by-one.'
MDN: spread syntax can expand iterable values in function calls and array literals; object spread has its own behavior.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 14
RETURN TO INDEX
14 Spread in function calls
Why spread helps when a function expects separate arguments.
f u n c t i o n  t o t a l ( a ,  b ,  c )  { 
    r e t u r n  a  +  b  +  c ; 
} 
c o n s t  v a l u e s  =  [ 1 0 ,  2 0 ,  3 0 ] ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( t o t a l ( . . . v a l u e s ) ) ;  / /  6 0 
Without spread, total(values) passes one array. With spread, JavaScript effectively receives total(10, 20,
30).

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 15
RETURN TO INDEX
15 Spread + Math.max/min
The key Session 3 connection.
c o n s t  n u m b e r s  =  [ 1 0 ,  4 4 ,  2 ,  7 ,  3 1 ,  5 ] ; 
c o n s t  h i g h e s t  =  M a t h . m a x ( . . . n u m b e r s ) ; 
c o n s t  l o w e s t  =  M a t h . m i n ( . . . n u m b e r s ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( h i g h e s t ) ;  / /  4 4 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( l o w e s t ) ;   / /  2 
Why it works
Math.max(10, 44, 2, ...) is valid. Math.max(numbers) is not the same call. Spread turns the array into separate
arguments.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 16
RETURN TO INDEX
16 Spread in arrays + copying
A useful extension: combine arrays and make shallow copies.
c o n s t  f i r s t  =  [ 1 ,  2 ,  3 ] ; 
c o n s t  c o m b i n e d  =  [ 0 ,  . . . f i r s t ,  4 ] ; 
/ /  [ 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 ] 
c o n s t  c o p y  =  [ . . . f i r s t ] ; 
c o p y . p u s h ( 9 9 ) ; 
Shallow copy
The new array is separate at the outer level. Nested objects/arrays are still shared references.

JavaScript - Session 3 Page 17
RETURN TO INDEX
17 Conditional statements
A condition is a question that becomes true or false.
i f  ( c o n d i t i o n )  { 
    / /  t r u e  b r a n c h 
}  e l s e  i f  ( a n o t h e r C o n d i t i o n )  { 
    / /  n e x t  b r a n c h 
}  e l s e  { 
    / /  f a l l b a c k  b r a n c h 
} 
Flow
Check condition -> choose one branch -> run it -> continue.
The reference lesson defines conditional statements as control-flow constructs that choose different blocks
based on a boolean condition.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 18
RETURN TO INDEX
18 if statement
Run a block only when the condition is true.
c o n s t  a g e  =  2 1 ; 
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A d u l t " ) ; 
} 
Read the question
age >= 18 asks: 'Is age at least 18?' Since 21 satisfies the condition, the block runs.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 19
RETURN TO INDEX
19 if / else
Two possible paths.
c o n s t  a g e  =  1 6 ; 
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " C a n  v o t e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " C a n n o t  v o t e " ) ; 
} 
Exactly one branch runs. If the condition is false, control jumps to else.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 20
RETURN TO INDEX
20 else if chains
Use multiple branches for ranges or categories.
c o n s t  m a r k s  =  7 8 ; 
i f  ( m a r k s  > =  9 0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A + " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  7 5 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  4 0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " P a s s " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " F a i l " ) ; 
} 
Order matters
JavaScript checks from top to bottom and stops at the first matching branch. Put stronger/higher rules first.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 21
RETURN TO INDEX
21 Nested conditions
An if can contain another if.
c o n s t  a g e  =  2 0 ; 
c o n s t  h a s I D  =  t r u e ; 
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  { 
    i f  ( h a s I D )  { 
        c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A c c e s s  g r a n t e d " ) ; 
    } 
} 
Cleaner when the rule is simple
If both checks must pass, combine them with && to make the rule easier to read.
Cleaner version
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8  & &  h a s I D )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A c c e s s  g r a n t e d " ) ; 
} 

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 22
RETURN TO INDEX
22 Branch order
The first true branch wins.
c o n s t  t e m p  =  4 0 ; 
i f  ( t e m p  >  3 5 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " H o t " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( t e m p  > =  1 5 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N o r m a l " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " C o l d " ) ; 
} 
At 40, the first condition is true, so JavaScript never evaluates the later branches for execution.
Remember
Branch order is part of the logic, not just formatting.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 23
RETURN TO INDEX
23 Ternary operator
A compact two-way conditional that produces a value.
c o n s t  a g e  =  1 9 ; 
c o n s t  s t a t u s  =  a g e  > =  1 8 
    ?  " A d u l t " 
    :  " M i n o r " ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( s t a t u s ) ;  / /  A d u l t 
Pattern
condition ? valueWhenTrue : valueWhenFalse
It is called ternary because it has three operands: the condition, the true result and the false result.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 24
RETURN TO INDEX
24 if / else vs ternary
Same decision idea, different style.
Style Best fit
if / else Run blocks, perform several steps, or handle multiple branches.
ternary Choose one value/message from two alternatives.
/ /  i f  /  e l s e 
l e t  r e s u l t ; 
i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  r e s u l t  =  " E l i g i b l e " ; 
e l s e  r e s u l t  =  " N o t  e l i g i b l e " ; 
/ /  t e r n a r y 
c o n s t  r e s u l t 2  =  a g e  > =  1 8  ?  " E l i g i b l e "  :  " N o t  e l i g i b l e " ; 
Practical rule
Keep ternaries short. If the expression becomes hard to scan, use if/else.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 25
RETURN TO INDEX
25 Common decision patterns
Turn plain-English rules into code.
/ /  E v e n 
n  %  2  = = =  0 
/ /  I n s i d e  a  r a n g e 
n  > =  1 0  & &  n  < =  5 0 
/ /  B O T H  r e q u i r e m e n t s 
a g e  > =  1 8  & &  h a s I D 
/ /  E I T H E R  r e q u i r e m e n t 
i s A d m i n  | |  i s O w n e r 
/ /  P o s i t i v e  /  n e g a t i v e  /  z e r o 
n  >  0 
n  <  0 
e l s e  - >  z e r o 
Best habit
Write the English rule first. Then convert each part into a comparison. Finally connect the parts with && or ||.

26 Q3 - Voting + validation
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  a g e  =  2 8 ; 
i f  ( a g e  <  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A g e  m u s t  b e  p o s i t i v e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( a g e  > =  1 8 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " E l i g i b l e  t o  v o t e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N o t  e l i g i b l e  t o  v o t e " ) ; 
} 
How it works
Validation comes first, so invalid values are rejected before eligibility is decided.
Expected result
Eligible to vote

27 Q4 - Larger of two
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.

c o n s o l e . l o g ( l a r g e r ) ; 
How it works
The ternary selects a only when a is bigger. Otherwise b is used.

29 Q6 - Divisible by 3 and 5
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  n  =  1 5 ; 
i f  ( n  %  3  = = =  0  & &  n  %  5  = = =  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " B o t h " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( n  %  3  = = =  0  | |  n  %  5  = = =  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " O n l y  o n e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N e i t h e r " ) ; 
} 
How it works
Remainder 0 means divisible. && checks both rules; || checks whether at least one rule is true.
Expected result
Both

30 Q7 - Divisible by 5 and 10
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  n  =  1 5 ; 
i f  ( n  %  5  = = =  0  & &  n  %  1 0  = = =  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " B o t h " ) ; 
}  e l s e  i f  ( n  %  5  = = =  0  | |  n  %  1 0  = = =  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " O n l y  o n e " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N e i t h e r " ) ; 
} 
How it works
15 is divisible by 5, but not by 10, so the 'Only one' branch runs.
Expected result
Only one

31 Q8 - Student grading
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  m a r k s  =  9 9 ; 
l e t  g r a d e  =  " " ; 
i f  ( m a r k s  > =  0  & &  m a r k s  < =  1 0 0 )  { 
    i f  ( m a r k s  > =  9 0 )  g r a d e  =  " A + " ; 
    e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  7 0 )  g r a d e  =  " A " ; 
    e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  4 0 )  g r a d e  =  " B " ; 
    e l s e  i f  ( m a r k s  > =  3 0 )  g r a d e  =  " C " ; 
    e l s e  g r a d e  =  " F a i l " ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    g r a d e  =  " I n v a l i d  m a r k s " ; 
} 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( g r a d e ) ; 
How it works
First validate the allowed range. Then the nested grade ladder chooses the first matching band.
Expected result
A+

33 Q10 - Range check

34 Q11 - Username + password
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  u s e r n a m e  =  " a d m i n _ u s e r " ; 
c o n s t  p a s s w o r d  =  " S e c u r e P a s s w o r d 1 2 3 " ; 
i f  ( 
    u s e r n a m e  = = =  " a d m i n _ u s e r "  & & 
    p a s s w o r d  = = =  " S e c u r e P a s s w o r d 1 2 3 " 
)  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " L o g i n  a c c e p t e d " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " L o g i n  r e j e c t e d " ) ; 
} 
How it works
Use strict equality for both fields and && because both credentials must match.
Expected result
Login accepted

36 Q13 - Positive and even
Practical exercise rewritten from the Session 3 reference set.
c o n s t  n  =  1 4 ; 
i f  ( n  >  0  & &  n  %  2  = = =  0 )  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " P o s i t i v e  a n d  e v e n " ) ; 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " N o t  b o t h " ) ; 
} 
How it works
One condition checks sign; the other checks whether division by 2 leaves remainder 0.
Expected result
Positive and even

c o n s t  c o r r e c t P i n  =  4 5 6 7 ;

i f  ( e n t e r e d P i n  = = =  c o r r e c t P i n )  { 
    i f  ( w i t h d r a w a l  >  0  & &  w i t h d r a w a l  < =  b a l a n c e )  { 
        b a l a n c e  - =  w i t h d r a w a l ; 
        c o n s o l e . l o g ( " W i t h d r a w a l  s u c c e s s f u l " ) ; 
        c o n s o l e . l o g ( " B a l a n c e : " ,  b a l a n c e ) ; 
    }  e l s e  i f  ( w i t h d r a w a l  >  b a l a n c e )  { 
        c o n s o l e . l o g ( " I n s u f f i c i e n t  b a l a n c e " ) ; 
    }  e l s e  { 
        c o n s o l e . l o g ( " I n v a l i d  a m o u n t " ) ; 
    } 
}  e l s e  { 
    c o n s o l e . l o g ( " W r o n g  P I N " ) ; 
} 
How it works
Authentication is checked first. Only a correct PIN lets the withdrawal rules run.
Expected result
Withdrawal successful / Balance: 40000

46 Challenge questions
Predict first; then verify by running the code.
1) What integers can Math.floor(Math.random() * 10) produce?
Answer
0 through 9
2) What reaches Math.max(...[8, 2, 17, 5]) and what is the result?
Answer
8, 2, 17, 5 are individual arguments; result = 17
3) Marks = 72 in the grading ladder. Which grade matches?
Answer
A
4) age = 20 and hasID = false. What happens with age >= 18 && hasID?
Answer
Access denied
5) temp = 40. Which branch wins?
Answer
Hot

49 Interview revision
Short answers for a viva or interview.
What is the Math object?
A built-in namespace object that provides mathematical constants and static methods.
What does Math.random() return?
A pseudo-random floating-point number from 0 inclusive to 1 exclusive.
What does Math.floor() do?
It returns the greatest integer less than or equal to the input - in simple words, it rounds down.
What does spread do?
It expands iterable values, such as array items, into argument positions or array elements.
Why use ...array with Math.max()?
Because Math.max expects separate numeric arguments, while an array is one value.
What is a conditional statement?
A control-flow structure that chooses which block should run based on a condition.
What is ternary?
A three-part expression: condition ? trueValue : falseValue.

JS SESSION 3
JavaScript - Session 3 Page 50

50 Quick revision sheet
One-page cheat sheet.
Concept Quick memory
Math.random() 0 <= value < 1
Math.floor(x) round down
Math.sqrt(x) square root
Math.pow(a,b) a raised to b
Math.min(...) smallest argument
Math.max(...) largest argument
Spread (...) expand iterable items
if / else choose a branch
else if try another condition
ternary condition ? true : false
&& both must be true
|| at least one must be true
% === 0 common divisibility check
n >= min && n <= max inclusive range
Memory chain
Math -> Spread -> Conditions -> Practice

Combine Math, spread and conditions in one realistic exercise.
c o n s t  n u m b e r s  =  [ 1 2 ,  7 ,  3 4 ,  1 8 ,  5 ] ; 
c o n s t  h i g h e s t  =  M a t h . m a x ( . . . n u m b e r s ) ; 
c o n s t  l o w e s t  =  M a t h . m i n ( . . . n u m b e r s ) ; 
l e t  t o t a l  =  0 ; 
f o r  ( c o n s t  n  o f  n u m b e r s )  { 
    t o t a l  + =  n ; 
} 
c o n s t  a v e r a g e  =  t o t a l  /  n u m b e r s . l e n g t h ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( " H i g h e s t : " ,  h i g h e s t ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( " L o w e s t : " ,  l o w e s t ) ; 
c o n s o l e . l o g ( " A v e r a g e : " ,  a v e r a g e ) ; 
f o r  ( c o n s t  n  o f  n u m b e r s )  { 
    i f  ( n  >  2 0 )  c o n s o l e . l o g ( n ,  " - >  h i g h " ) ; 
    e l s e  i f  ( n  > =  1 0 )  c o n s o l e . l o g ( n ,  " - >  m e d i u m " ) ; 
    e l s e  c o n s o l e . l o g ( n ,  " - >  l o w " ) ; 
} 
What this project uses
Spread + Math.max/min + arithmetic + if/else-if/else. It is a good bridge from isolated syntax to actual problem
solving.

54 Common mistakes + final checklist
Use this before moving to Session 4.
- Math.random() never returns exactly 1. - Math.floor(-4.1) is -5. - Math.max(numbers) is not the same as
Math.max(...numbers). - Spread expands; rest collects. - Avoid deeply nested ternaries. - else-if branches
are checked top to bottom. - BOTH -> think &&. EITHER -> think ||. - Validate input before applying
business rules. - Write the rule in plain English before writing the condition.
Final self-test
Can you explain Math.random(), Math.floor(), Math.sqrt(), Math.pow(), Math.min(), Math.max(), spread,
if/else-if/else, ternary, &&, || and range checks without looking?

// String Methods

let str = "I am learning Javascript learning";
// Creates a variable named 'str' and stores the string in it.
// String value: "I am learning Javascript learning"

console.log(str.length);
// .length returns the total number of characters in the string.
// Spaces are also counted.
// Output: 30

console.log(str.toLowerCase());
// Converts the entire string to lowercase.
// Output: "i am learning javascript learning"

console.log(str.toUpperCase());
// Converts the entire string to uppercase.
// Output: "I AM LEARNING JAVASCRIPT LEARNING"

console.log(str.split());
// Splits the string into an array.
// Without an argument, the entire string becomes one array element.
// Output: ["I am learning Javascript learning"]

console.log(str.indexOf("r"));
// Finds the position (index) of the first occurrence of "r".
// JavaScript uses zero-based indexing.
// Output: 8

console.log(str.lastIndexOf("m"));
// Finds the position of the last occurrence of "m".
// Output: 3

console.log(str.slice(-1));
// Extracts characters from the given position to the end.
// -1 means the last character.
// Output: "g"

console.log(str.match(/learning/g));
// Finds all occurrences of "learning" using a regular expression.
// The 'g' flag means "global", so all matches are returned.
// Output: ["learning", "learning"]

console.log(str.concat(", Great"));
// Joins another string to the end of the original string.
// Output: "I am learning Javascript learning, Great"

console.log(str.replace("learning", "hey"));
// Replaces only the FIRST occurrence of "learning".
// Output: "I am hey Javascript learning"

console.log(str.replaceAll("learning", "hey"));
// Replaces ALL occurrences of "learning".
// Output: "I am hey Javascript hey"

console.log(str.startsWith("I"));
// Checks whether the string starts with "I".
// Returns true or false.
// Output: true

console.log(str.endsWith("g"));
// Checks whether the string ends with "g".
// Returns true or false.
// Output: true

console.log(str.charAt(0));
// Returns the character at index 0.
// Index 0 is the first character.
// Output: "I"

console.log(str.isWellFormed());
// Checks whether the string contains well-formed Unicode characters.
// Normal text like this returns true.
// Output: true


// Number Checking

let a = 4;
// Creates a variable 'a' and assigns the number 4 to it.

console.log(isNaN(a));
// isNaN() checks whether a value is "Not a Number".
// Since 4 is a valid number, the result is false.
// Output: false

if (isNaN(a)) {
    // If 'a' is Not a Number, this block will execute.

    console.log("Enter a valid number");
    // Displays a message asking the user to enter a valid number.

} else {
    // If 'a' is a valid number, this block will execute.

    console.log("Valid number");
    // Displays "Valid number".
}


// Number Conversion

let num1 = "12.34abc";
// Stores the text "12.34abc" in the variable num1.
// Notice that this is a STRING, not a number.

console.log(Number(num1));
// Number() tries to convert the entire string into a number.
// "12.34abc" contains non-numeric characters, so conversion fails.
// Output: NaN

console.log(parseInt(num1));
// parseInt() reads the number from the beginning of the string.
// It stops when it encounters a character that is not part of the integer.
// Output: 12

function getData() {
    const res = document.getElementById("name").value
    console.log('res', res)

    const para1 = document.getElementsByTagName('p')
    console.log("para1 => ", para1)


}

How the concepts connect
A practical path from text processing to browser data access.
 JavaScript Session 4
|
+-- String values
|   +-- inspect -> search -> extract -> replace -> test
|
+-- Number values
|   +-- check validity -> convert -> compare parsing strategies
|
+-- DOM
    +-- find element -> read value -> work with collection -> use logic
CORE IDEA
Session 4 connects data handling with the browser. Strings are manipulated as values; numbers are checked
and converted; DOM APIs let JavaScript read data from HTML.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

05
String Methods
Overview of the session text toolkit
String methods let you inspect, search, transform and combine text.
JavaScript string operations normally return a new value rather than
changing the original string.
 let str = "I am learning Javascript learning";
|
+-- Inspect: length, charAt, at
+-- Search: indexOf, lastIndexOf, includes, search, match
+-- Extract: slice, substring
+-- Transform: toLowerCase, toUpperCase, replace, replaceAll
+-- Combine: concat
+-- Test: startsWith, endsWith, isWellFormed
+-- Format: trim, repeat, padStart, padEnd
REMEMBER
Most String methods return a result. They do not rewrite the original primitive string in place. Store the result
when you need the transformed value later.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 5 of 47
Return to Index


06
length
String methods
Returns the number of UTF-16 code units in the string. In ordinary text
this behaves like a character count. Spaces are counted.
 let n = str.length;
WHAT IT DOES
Use `.length` when you need the size of a string or array. It is a property, not a function - do not write
`str.length()`.
let str = "I am learning Javascript learning";
console.log(str.length);
EXPECTED RESULT
30
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 6 of 47
Return to Index


07
toLowerCase() / toUpperCase()
String methods
Creates lowercase or uppercase versions of the string.
 str.toLowerCase();
str.toUpperCase();
WHAT IT DOES
Both methods return a new string value; the original `str` remains unchanged.
console.log(str.toLowerCase());
console.log(str.toUpperCase());
EXPECTED RESULT
i am learning javascript learning I AM LEARNING JAVASCRIPT LEARNING
COMMON PITFALL
Locale-specific casing rules can be more complex than simple ASCII text; these methods are suitable for the
basic English examples used here.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 7 of 47
Return to Index


08
split()
String methods
Splits a string into an array. With no separator, the whole string becomes
one array element.
 str.split(separator);
WHAT IT DOES
The separator controls how the string is divided. `split(" ")` creates words; `split("")` creates UTF-16 code units,
which is not always the same as user-perceived characters.
console.log(str.split());
console.log(str.split(" "));
EXPECTED RESULT
["I am learning Javascript learning"] ["I", "am", "learning", "Javascript", "learning"]
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 8 of 47
Return to Index


09
indexOf() / lastIndexOf()
String methods
Finds the first or last occurrence of a search value and returns its
zero-based index. If nothing is found, the result is `-1`.
 str.indexOf(searchValue);
str.lastIndexOf(searchValue);
WHAT IT DOES
Do not use a truthy check like `if (str.indexOf("x"))` because index `0` is a valid match but is falsy. Compare with
`!== -1` when checking existence.
console.log(str.indexOf("r"));
console.log(str.lastIndexOf("m"));
EXPECTED RESULT
8 3
COMMON PITFALL
Case matters: `indexOf("R")` and `indexOf("r")` are different searches.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 9 of 47
Return to Index


10
slice()
String methods
Extracts part of a string from `start` up to, but not including, `end`.
Negative indexes count from the end.
 str.slice(start, end);
WHAT IT DOES
The end index is excluded. `slice(-1)` returns the final character.
console.log(str.slice(-1));
console.log(str.slice(5, 12));
EXPECTED RESULT
g learning
COMMON PITFALL
Because the end position is exclusive, `slice(0, 3)` returns indexes 0, 1 and 2.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 10 of 47
Return to Index


11
match()
String methods
Searches using a regular expression and returns matched text. With the
global `g` flag, all matches are returned.
 str.match(pattern);
WHAT IT DOES
`match()` is useful when searching with patterns rather than a simple literal string. When there is no match, the
result can be `null`.
console.log(str.match(/learning/g));
EXPECTED RESULT
["learning", "learning"]
COMMON PITFALL
The regular expression flag `g` means global matching.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 11 of 47
Return to Index


12
concat()
String methods
Joins additional strings to the end of the original string and returns the
combined string.
 str.concat(value1, value2, ...);
WHAT IT DOES
For simple concatenation, the `+` operator is also common: `str + ", Great"`. `concat()` is a direct string method.
console.log(str.concat(", Great"));
EXPECTED RESULT
I am learning Javascript learning, Great
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 12 of 47
Return to Index


13
replace()
String methods
Replaces the first occurrence of the search value.
 str.replace(searchValue, newValue);
WHAT IT DOES
With a plain string search, only the first matching occurrence is replaced.
console.log(str.replace("learning", "hey"));
EXPECTED RESULT
I am hey Javascript learning
COMMON PITFALL
Use `replaceAll()` or a global regular expression when every occurrence must be replaced.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 13 of 47
Return to Index


14
replaceAll()
String methods
Replaces every occurrence of the search value.
 str.replaceAll(searchValue, newValue);
WHAT IT DOES
Use `replaceAll()` when you want a direct all-occurrences replacement.
console.log(str.replaceAll("learning", "hey"));
EXPECTED RESULT
I am hey Javascript hey
COMMON PITFALL
Do not confuse `replace()` with `replaceAll()` when repeated text is possible.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

Return to Index


15
startsWith() / endsWith()
String methods
Tests whether a string begins or ends with a specified sequence. Both
return Boolean values.
 str.startsWith(search);
str.endsWith(search);
WHAT IT DOES
These methods are ideal for clear prefix/suffix checks.
console.log(str.startsWith("I"));
console.log(str.endsWith("g"));
EXPECTED RESULT
true true
COMMON PITFALL
They are case-sensitive and return only `true` or `false`.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 15 of 47
Return to Index


16
charAt()
String methods
Returns the character at a specified zero-based index.
 str.charAt(index);
WHAT IT DOES
For modern code, `str[index]` and `str.at(index)` are also common ways to access characters.
console.log(str.charAt(0));
console.log(str.charAt(5));
EXPECTED RESULT
I m
COMMON PITFALL
An out-of-range `charAt()` call returns an empty string.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 16 of 47
Return to Index


17
isWellFormed()
String methods
Checks whether the string contains well-formed Unicode code units.
Normal text returns `true`.
 str.isWellFormed();
WHAT IT DOES
This method is useful when code may receive arbitrary Unicode text and you need to detect lone surrogate code
units.
console.log(str.isWellFormed());
EXPECTED RESULT
true
COMMON PITFALL
It is more specialized than the other beginner methods, but it is included because it appears in the session
source.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

18
Related String Methods I
Useful methods not present in the original source
The source session already covered the core methods. The following
related methods complete the same conceptual family.
 Method
Purpose
Example
includes()
Checks whether a string contains a sequence.
str.includes("learning")
search()
Returns the first regex match index, or -1.
str.search(/learning/)
substring()
Extracts from one index to another; negative values are
treated as 0.
str.substring(0, 2)
console.log(str.includes("learning"));
console.log(str.search(/learning/));
console.log(str.substring(0, 4));
WHEN TO USE WHICH
Use `includes()` for a simple yes/no containment check, `search()` when you want a regular-expression search
index, and `substring()` when you need a non-negative index-based extraction rule.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

19
Related String Methods II
Whitespace, indexing and character inspection
Method
Purpose
Example
trim()
Removes whitespace from both ends.
input.trim()
trimStart()
Removes leading whitespace.
input.trimStart()
trimEnd()
Removes trailing whitespace.
input.trimEnd()
at()
Reads a character by index and supports negative indexes.
str.at(-1)
charCodeAt()
Returns the UTF-16 code unit at an index.
str.charCodeAt(0)
let input = "   Hello   ";
console.log(input.trim());
console.log(str.at(-1));
console.log(str.charCodeAt(0));
IMPORTANT
`at(-1)` is a convenient modern way to read the final character. `charCodeAt()` returns a numeric UTF-16 code
unit, so it is lower-level than `charAt()`.

20
Related String Methods III
Formatting and repeated-pattern helpers
Method
Purpose
Example
repeat()
Repeats a string a chosen number of times.
"-".repeat(5)
padStart()
Pads the beginning until a target length is reached.
"7".padStart(3,"0")
padEnd()
Pads the end until a target length is reached.
"7".padEnd(3,"0")
matchAll()
Returns an iterator over all regex matches with capture
groups.
str.matchAll(/learning/g)
console.log("-".repeat(5));
console.log("7".padStart(3, "0"));
console.log("7".padEnd(3, "0"));
PRACTICAL USE
These helpers are useful for formatting, report output and controlled text patterns. `matchAll()` becomes
particularly useful when capture groups are needed from multiple regex matches.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

21
String Method Patterns + Mutation Note
How to think about String APIs
A string is a primitive value. Methods such as `toUpperCase()`, `replace()`
and `trim()` return another string value instead of mutating the original
primitive.
 let text = " hello ";
let cleaned = text.trim();
let upper = cleaned.toUpperCase();
console.log(text);    // original value
console.log(upper);   // transformed result
PATTERN
Think in terms of: original value -> method call -> returned value. Save the returned value in a variable
whenever the transformed result is needed later.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 21 of 47
Return to Index


22
Number Checking
Validation family overview
JavaScript provides the global `isNaN()` function and a `Number.is*`
family for more explicit numeric checks.
 Tool
Main question
isNaN(value)
Does conversion of value result in NaN?
Number.isNaN(value)
Is value actually the NaN value?
Number.isFinite(value)
Is value a finite number?
Number.isInteger(value)
Is value an integer?
Number.isSafeInteger(val
ue)
Is value a safe integer?
WHY THIS MATTERS
Validation should answer a precise question. For modern code, the `Number.is*` methods are often easier to
reason about because they do not perform the broad coercion behavior of the global `isNaN()`.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 22 of 47
Return to Index


23
isNaN()
Global numeric validity check
The global `isNaN()` function checks whether a value becomes NaN after
numeric conversion.
 let a = 4;
console.log(isNaN(a));
if (isNaN(a)) {
    console.log("Enter a valid number");
} else {
    console.log("Valid number");
}
EXPECTED RESULT
false Valid number
WARNING
Because the global function converts its argument first, it can produce results that are surprising for strings such
as `"123"`.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 23 of 47
Return to Index

Number.isNaN(), isFinite(), isInteger(),
isSafeInteger()
More explicit number checks
Method
Example
Result meaning
Number.isNaN(x)
Number.isNaN(NaN)
true only for NaN
Number.isFinite(x)
Number.isFinite(12)
true for finite numbers
Number.isInteger(x)
Number.isInteger(12)
true for integers
Number.isSafeInteger(x)
Number.isSafeInteger(12)
true within safe integer range
console.log(Number.isNaN(NaN));
console.log(Number.isFinite(12));
console.log(Number.isInteger(12));
console.log(Number.isSafeInteger(12));
RULE OF THUMB
Prefer the `Number.is*` methods when you want an explicit check against a numeric condition without broad
type coercion.
