25
Number Conversion
Why `Number()` and parsing are different
The source demonstrates a string containing digits followed by letters:
`"12.34abc"`. Different conversion functions interpret that value
differently.
 let num1 = "12.34abc";
Number(num1);      // NaN
parseInt(num1);    // 12
parseFloat(num1);  // 12.34
CONCEPT
`Number()` expects the whole string to represent a valid number. `parseInt()` and `parseFloat()` read a numeric
prefix and stop when parsing cannot continue.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 25 of 47
Return to Index


26
Number()
Whole-value conversion
`Number(value)` attempts to convert the entire value to a JavaScript
number.
 console.log(Number("12.34"));
console.log(Number("12.34abc"));
console.log(Number(""));
OUTPUT
12.34 NaN 0
TIP
For user input that should represent an entire numeric value, `Number()` is a straightforward choice. Validate
the result when input may be invalid.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 26 of 47
Return to Index


27
parseInt()
Parse an integer prefix
`parseInt(value, radix)` reads an integer from the beginning of a string.
The radix argument is recommended when interpreting non-decimal
strings.
 console.log(parseInt("12.34abc", 10));
console.log(parseInt("101", 2));
console.log(parseInt("42px", 10));
OUTPUT
12 5 42
IMPORTANT
`parseInt()` stops at the first character that cannot be part of the parsed integer. It is a parsing function, not a
strict full-string validator.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 27 of 47
Return to Index


28
parseFloat()
Parse a decimal prefix
`parseFloat()` reads a floating-point number from the beginning of a
string.
 console.log(parseFloat("12.34abc"));
console.log(parseFloat("42px"));
console.log(parseFloat("3.14"));
OUTPUT
12.34 42 3.14
PAIR WITH parseInt()
Use `parseInt()` when you want an integer prefix; use `parseFloat()` when a decimal prefix is expected.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

30
DOM Basics - getElementById()
Read an element by its unique id
The DOM represents the HTML document as objects that JavaScript can
access. `document.getElementById()` returns the element whose `id`
matches the given string.
 const res = document.getElementById("name");
console.log(res.value);
SOURCE CONNECTION
The session HTML contains an input element with id="name", and the JavaScript reads its .value inside
getData().
IMPORTANT
An `id` should identify one element in a document. If the same id appears on multiple elements, id-based
selection becomes invalid or misleading.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes
Page 30 of 47
Return to Index


31
getElementsByTagName()
Collect elements by tag name
`document.getElementsByTagName("p")` returns a collection of all
matching paragraph elements.
 const paragraphs = document.getElementsByTagName("p");
console.log(paragraphs);
console.log(paragraphs[0].textContent);
SOURCE CONNECTION
The supplied code uses `document.getElementsByTagName("p")` and logs the returned collection.
COLLECTION NOTE
The result is an HTMLCollection. It can contain zero, one or many matching elements. Access an individual item
by index when needed.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

Method

Returns all matching elements as a NodeList.

getElementsByClassNa

Returns elements with a given class name.
document.getElementsByClassNa

WHY ADD THESE
They are natural next steps from `getElementById()` and `getElementsByTagName()`. They let you select

33
Collections + Duplicate ID Warning
Important DOM behavior
<div id="para1">1</div>
<div id="para1">2</div>
<p>Lorem ipsum dolor sit.</p>
PROBLEM IN THE SOURCE HTML
The provided HTML declares `id="para1"` twice. IDs are intended to be unique. Replace repeated ids with
classes or unique ids when multiple elements share a role.
COLLECTION THINKING
Tag/class selectors may return collections. An id selector is intended to return one specific element.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

34
getData() Walkthrough
Read input + inspect paragraph elements
function getData() {
    const res = document.getElementById("name").value;
    console.log("res", res);
 
    const para1 = document.getElementsByTagName("p");
    console.log("para1 => ", para1);
}
Step
What happens
1
User enters text in the input.
2
getData() is triggered by the button click.
3
getElementById("name") finds the input element.
4
.value reads the current input text.
5
getElementsByTagName("p") retrieves paragraph elements.
SOURCE HTML CONNECTION
The button uses `onclick="getData()"`, so the JavaScript function runs when the user clicks Enter.

35
HTML -> JavaScript Data Flow
The browser interaction model
HTML input
   |
   +-- id="name"
   |
button onclick="getData()"
   |
   v
getData()
   |
   +-- document.getElementById("name")
   |       +-- .value
   |
   +-- document.getElementsByTagName("p")
           +-- HTMLCollection
KEY IDEA
The DOM is the bridge between the HTML structure and JavaScript logic. Select an element first; then read a
property such as `.value` or `.textContent`.

41
Interview Revision
Fast answers for Session 4
Question
Answer
What does length return?
The string length as UTF-16 code units.
indexOf() vs lastIndexOf()?
First match vs last match index.
slice() end index?
The end position is exclusive.
replace() vs replaceAll()?
First replacement vs all occurrences.
What does isNaN() do?
Checks whether a value becomes NaN after numeric conversion.
Number.isNaN()?
Checks whether the value itself is NaN without broad coercion.
Number() vs parseInt()?
Whole-value conversion vs integer-prefix parsing.
getElementById()?
Selects the element with a matching id.
getElementsByTagName()?
Returns matching elements as an HTMLCollection.
Why avoid duplicate ids?
An id is intended to be unique in the document.
INTERVIEW TIP
Give a one-sentence definition first, then a one-line example. Clear terminology matters more than memorizing
long descriptions.
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

Return to Index

44
Clean Source Code - Part 1
Appendix: original Session 4 JavaScript
// String Methods
 
let str = "I am learning Javascript learning";
// Creates a variable named 'str' and stores the string in it.
// String value: "I am learning Javascript learning"
 
console.log(str.length);
// .length returns the total number of characters in the string.
// Spaces are also counted.
// Output: 30
 
console.log(str.toLowerCase());
// Converts the entire string to lowercase.
// Output: "i am learning javascript learning"
 
console.log(str.toUpperCase());
// Converts the entire string to uppercase.
// Output: "I AM LEARNING JAVASCRIPT LEARNING"
 
console.log(str.split());
// Splits the string into an array.
// Without an argument, the entire string becomes one array element.
// Output: ["I am learning Javascript learning"]
 
console.log(str.indexOf("r"));
// Finds the position (index) of the first occurrence of "r".
// JavaScript uses zero-based indexing.
// Output: 8
 
console.log(str.lastIndexOf("m"));
// Finds the position of the last occurrence of "m".
// Output: 3
 
console.log(str.slice(-1));
// Extracts characters from the given position to the end.
// -1 means the last character.
// Output: "g"
 
console.log(str.match(/learning/g));
// Finds all occurrences of "learning" using a regular expression.
// The 'g' flag means "global", so all matches are returned.
// Output: ["learning", "learning"]
 
console.log(str.concat(", Great"));
// Joins another string to the end of the original string.
// Output: "I am learning Javascript learning, Great"
 
console.log(str.replace("learning", "hey"));
// Replaces only the FIRST occurrence of "learning".
// Output: "I am hey Javascript learning"
 
console.log(str.replaceAll("learning", "hey"));
// Replaces ALL occurrences of "learning".
// Output: "I am hey Javascript hey"
 
JavaScript - Session 4 Notes

Return to Index


45
Clean Source Code - Part 2
Appendix: original Session 4 JavaScript
console.log(str.startsWith("I"));
// Checks whether the string starts with "I".
// Returns true or false.
// Output: true
 
console.log(str.endsWith("g"));
// Checks whether the string ends with "g".
// Returns true or false.
// Output: true
 
console.log(str.charAt(0));
// Returns the character at index 0.
// Index 0 is the first character.
// Output: "I"
 
console.log(str.isWellFormed());
// Checks whether the string contains well-formed Unicode characters.
// Normal text like this returns true.
// Output: true
 
 
// Number Checking
 
let a = 4;
// Creates a variable 'a' and assigns the number 4 to it.
 
console.log(isNaN(a));
// isNaN() checks whether a value is "Not a Number".
// Since 4 is a valid number, the result is false.
// Output: false
 
if (isNaN(a)) {
    // If 'a' is Not a Number, this block will execute.
 
    console.log("Enter a valid number");
    // Displays a message asking the user to enter a valid number.
 
} else {
    // If 'a' is a valid number, this block will execute.
 
    console.log("Valid number");
    // Displays "Valid number".
}
 
 
// Number Conversion
 
let num1 = "12.34abc";
// Stores the text "12.34abc" in the variable num1.
// Notice that this is a STRING, not a number.
 
console.log(Number(num1));
// Number() tries to convert the entire string into a number.
// "12.34abc" contains non-numeric characters, so conversion fails.
// Output: NaN

03
Session 4 Roadmap
How the ideas connect
STRING VALUES
↓
inspect → search → extract → replace → test
↓
NUMBER VALUES
↓
check validity → convert → compare parsing
↓
DOM
↓
find element → read value → work with collections
Core idea
Session 4 connects data handling with the browser. A string is manipulated as text, a number is checked/
converted, and the DOM lets JavaScript read data from HTML.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

04
String Methods Overview
A practical toolkit for working with text
String methods help you inspect, search, extract, transform and format text. Most
methods return a new value instead of rewriting the original string.
Group Methods
Inspect length, charAt, at
Search indexOf, lastIndexOf, includes, search, match
Extract slice, substring
Transform toLowerCase, toUpperCase, replace, replaceAll
Combine concat
Test startsWith, endsWith, isWellFormed
Format trim, repeat, padStart, padEnd
Remember
Think: original text → method call → returned result. Store the returned result when you need it later.
let str = "I am learning Javascript learning";
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

05
length
String methods
Gives the size of the string. Spaces count too.
Example
const text = "JavaScript is fun";
console.log(text.length);
What happens
You get a number. For ordinary English text this behaves like a character count.
Remember
length is a property, not a function. Write text.length, not text.length().
str.length
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

06
toLowerCase() / toUpperCase()
String methods
Makes a lowercase or uppercase version.
Example
const text = "JavaScript";
console.log(text.toLowerCase());
console.log(text.toUpperCase());
What happens
javascript
JAVASCRIPT
Remember
The original string stays unchanged.
str.toLowerCase();
str.toUpperCase();
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

07
split()
String methods
Turns one string into an array using a separator.
Example
const line = "red,green,blue";
console.log(line.split(","));
What happens
["red", "green", "blue"]
Remember
With no separator, the whole string becomes one array item.
Common pitfall
split("") works at UTF-16 code-unit level, so it is not always the same as user-perceived characters.
str.split(separator)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

08
indexOf() / lastIndexOf()
String methods
Finds the first or last matching position.
Example
const text = "banana";
console.log(text.indexOf("a"));
console.log(text.lastIndexOf("a"));
What happens
1
5
Remember
If the value is not found, the result is -1.
Common pitfall
Do not use if (text.indexOf("a")) to test existence because index 0 is a valid match. Use !== -1.
str.indexOf(value);
str.lastIndexOf(value);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

09
slice()
String methods
Extracts part of a string. The end index is excluded.
Example
const text = "JavaScript";
console.log(text.slice(0, 4));
console.log(text.slice(-3));
What happens
Java
ipt
Remember
Negative indexes count from the end.
Common pitfall
slice(0, 4) gives indexes 0,1,2,3. The 4 is not included.
str.slice(start, end)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

10
match()
String methods
Finds matches using a regular expression.
Example
const text = "learn learn";
console.log(text.match(/learn/g));
What happens
["learn", "learn"]
Remember
The g flag means global matching. No match can produce null.
str.match(pattern)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

11
concat()
String methods
Joins strings and returns the combined string.
Example
const first = "Hello";
console.log(first.concat(" JavaScript"));
What happens
Hello JavaScript
Remember
For simple joining, the + operator is also common.
str.concat(value1, value2, ...)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

12
replace()
String methods
Replaces the first matching occurrence when the search value is a string.
Example
const text = "hi hi";
console.log(text.replace("hi", "hello"));
What happens
hello hi
Remember
Use replaceAll() or a global pattern when every occurrence should change.
str.replace(searchValue, newValue)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

13
replaceAll()
String methods
Replaces every matching string occurrence.
Example
const text = "hi hi";
console.log(text.replaceAll("hi", "hello"));
What happens
hello hello
Remember
replace() and replaceAll() answer different questions: first occurrence vs all occurrences.
str.replaceAll(searchValue, newValue)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

14
startsWith() / endsWith()
String methods
Checks the beginning or ending of a string.
Example
const file = "notes.pdf";
console.log(file.startsWith("notes"));
console.log(file.endsWith(".pdf"));
What happens
true
true
Remember
Both return true or false and are case-sensitive.
str.startsWith(value);
str.endsWith(value);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

15
charAt()
String methods
Reads a character at a zero-based index.
Example
const word = "Code";
console.log(word.charAt(0));
console.log(word.charAt(3));
What happens
C
e
Remember
Modern alternatives include word[0] and word.at(0). Out-of-range charAt() returns an empty string.
str.charAt(index)
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

16
isWellFormed()
String methods
Checks whether a string has well-formed Unicode code units.
Example
const text = "Hello";
console.log(text.isWellFormed());
What happens
true
Remember
It is more specialized than the other beginner methods, but it appears in this session.
str.isWellFormed()
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

17
Related String Methods I
Useful methods that complete the same family
Method Simple meaning Example
includes() Does the text contain this value? text.includes("JS")
search() Find the first regex match index text.search(/JS/)
substring() Extract using two non-negative indexes text.substring(0,4)
When to use which
Use includes() for a clear yes/no check, search() when a regular expression is useful, and substring()
when you specifically want substring-style index rules.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

18
Related String Methods II
Whitespace, indexing and character information
Method Use Example
trim() Remove whitespace from both ends input.trim()
trimStart() Remove leading whitespace input.trimStart()
trimEnd() Remove trailing whitespace input.trimEnd()
at() Read a character and support negative indexes text.at(-1)
charCodeAt() Return the UTF-16 code-unit number text.charCodeAt(0)
Easy memory trick
at(-1) is a convenient way to ask for the last character. charCodeAt() gives a number rather than the
character itself.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

19
Related String Methods III
Formatting helpers
Method Use Example
repeat() Repeat a pattern "-".repeat(5)
padStart() Pad the beginning to a target length "7".padStart(3,"0")
padEnd() Pad the end to a target length "7".padEnd(3,"0")
matchAll() Walk through all regex matches and capture groups text.matchAll(/go/g)
Practical use
These helpers are useful for formatting labels, IDs, reports and repeated text patterns.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

20
String Method Pattern + Immutability
How to think about method calls
original string
↓
method call
↓
returned string
↓
save the result if you need it later
Important
Strings are primitive values. String methods return new string values; they do not mutate the original
primitive in place.
let text = " hello ";
let cleaned = text.trim();
let upper = cleaned.toUpperCase();
console.log(text);
console.log(upper);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

21
Number Checking
Global isNaN() vs the Number.is* family
Tool Question it answers
isNaN(value) Does conversion of this value produce NaN?
Number.isNaN(value) Is this value actually NaN?
Number.isFinite(value) Is this a finite number?
Number.isInteger(value) Is this an integer?
Number.isSafeInteger(value) Is this an integer in the safe range?
Why this matters
Validation should ask a precise question. The Number.is* methods avoid the broad coercion behavior of
global isNaN().
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

22
isNaN()
Global numeric validity check
Read it
Because 4 is a number, isNaN(4) is false and the else block runs.
Caution
The global isNaN() converts its argument before checking. That can be surprising for strings.
let value = 4;
console.log(isNaN(value));
if (isNaN(value)) {
  console.log("Invalid number");
} else {
  console.log("Valid number");
}
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

23
Number.isNaN(), isFinite(), isInteger(), isSafeInteger()
More explicit checks
Output
true
true
true
true
Rule of thumb
Prefer Number.is* methods when you want a specific numeric check without broad type coercion.
console.log(Number.isNaN(NaN));
console.log(Number.isFinite(12));
console.log(Number.isInteger(12));
console.log(Number.isSafeInteger(12));
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

24
Number Conversion Overview
The same input string can be interpreted in different ways
Function Simple rule
Number() The complete value must represent a valid number.
parseInt() Read an integer prefix.
parseFloat() Read a decimal-number prefix.
const value = "12.34abc";
Number(value);
parseInt(value, 10);
parseFloat(value);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

25
Number()
Whole-value conversion
Output
12.34
NaN
0
Simple rule
Use Number() when the whole input is supposed to be numeric. Validate the result when input may be
invalid.
console.log(Number("12.34"));
console.log(Number("12.34abc"));
console.log(Number(""));
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

26
parseInt()
Read an integer prefix
Output
12
5
42
Important
parseInt() stops when the next character cannot belong to the integer. The radix argument is
recommended.
console.log(parseInt("12.34abc", 10));
console.log(parseInt("101", 2));
console.log(parseInt("42px", 10));
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

27
parseFloat()
Read a decimal prefix
Output
12.34
42
3.14
Pair it with parseInt()
parseInt() is for an integer prefix. parseFloat() is for a decimal prefix.
console.log(parseFloat("12.34abc"));
console.log(parseFloat("42px"));
console.log(parseFloat("3.14"));
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

28
Number Conversion Comparison
Memorize the pattern
Input Number() parseInt() parseFloat()
"12.34abc" NaN 12 12.34
"42px" NaN 42 42
"12.34" 12.34 12 12.34
"" 0 NaN NaN
Memory map
Number() = whole value | parseInt() = integer prefix | parseFloat() = decimal prefix
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

29
DOM Basics - getElementById()
Select one element by its id
The DOM is the browser representation of the HTML document. JavaScript uses document
methods to find elements and read their properties.
Think in two steps
1) Find the element. 2) Read a property such as .value or .textContent.
Important
An id is intended to identify one element in a document.
const input = document.getElementById("name");
console.log(input.value);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

30
getElementsByTagName()
Collect elements by tag name
What you get
An HTMLCollection containing all matching paragraph elements. It can contain zero, one, or many items.
const paragraphs = document.getElementsByTagName("p");
console.log(paragraphs);
console.log(paragraphs[0].textContent);
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

31
Related DOM Selectors
Natural next steps from id and tag selectors
Method Purpose Example
querySelector() First element matching a CSS selector document.querySelector("p")
querySelectorAll() All matching elements as a NodeList document.querySelectorAll("p")
getElementsByClassName() Elements sharing a class name document.getElementsByClassName("item")
Easy rule
querySelector() gives the first match. querySelectorAll() gives all matches.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

32
Collections + Duplicate IDs
Why the HTML structure matters
Problem in the supplied HTML
The source uses id="para1" twice. IDs are meant to be unique. Use unique ids, or use a class for repeated
elements.
Collection thinking
Tag and class selectors can return collections. An id selector is intended to point to one specific element.
<div id="para1">1</div>
<div id="para1">2</div>
<p>Lorem ipsum dolor sit.</p>
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

33
getData() Walkthrough
Read the input and inspect paragraph elements
Step What happens
1 The user types in the input.
2 The button calls getData().
3 getElementById("name") finds the input.
4 .value reads the current text.
5 getElementsByTagName("p") returns the paragraph collection.
HTML connection
The supplied button uses onclick="getData()", so the function runs when the button is clicked.
function getData() {
  const res = document.getElementById("name").value;
  console.log("res", res);
  const para1 = document.getElementsByTagName("p");
  console.log("para1 =>", para1);
}

The browser interaction chain
HTML input
↓
id="name"
↓
.value
↓
JavaScript variable
↓
process or display result
button click
↓
onclick="getData()"
↓
getData()
↓
DOM selectors
↓
read data
Key idea
The DOM is the bridge between the HTML structure and JavaScript logic.

39
Mini Project - Input Validator
Combine the Session 4 ideas
User enters text
↓
trim() → empty check
↓
empty → show message
↓
else → uppercase + length + startsWith/endsWith
↓
numeric branch → Number() + Number.isNaN()
Requirement Concept
Read input getElementById() + .value
Clean input trim()
Text analysis length, includes(), startsWith(), endsWith()
Text transform toUpperCase(), replaceAll()
Numeric branch Number() + Number.isNaN()
Output console.log() or a DOM update
Why this is useful
It combines DOM reading, string cleanup, string methods and condition-based thinking in one small
browser task.
function validateInput() {
  const raw = document.getElementById("name").value;
  const value = raw.trim();
  if (value === "") {
    console.log("Please enter something");
    return;
  }
  console.log("Upper:", value.toUpperCase());
  console.log("Length:", value.length);
  console.log("Starts with J:", value.startsWith("J"));
}
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

40
Interview Revision
Fast answers
Question Answer
What does length return? String length as UTF-16 code units.
indexOf() vs lastIndexOf()? First matching index vs last matching index.
What does slice() do? Extracts a portion; end index is excluded.
replace() vs replaceAll()? First occurrence vs all occurrences for a string search.
What does isNaN() do? Checks whether a value becomes NaN after numeric conversion.
Number.isNaN()? Checks whether the value itself is NaN without broad coercion.
Number() vs parseInt()? Whole-value conversion vs integer-prefix parsing.
getElementById()? Selects the element with a matching id.
getElementsByTagName()? Returns matching elements as an HTMLCollection.
Why avoid duplicate ids? An id is intended to be unique in the document.
JavaScript - Session 4 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Session 4 Summary
What you should be able to do now
Skill Expected ability
String inspection Measure length and read characters
String searching Find text with indexes, includes or regex methods
String extraction Use slice and substring
String transformation Use case conversion and replacement methods
String formatting Trim, repeat and pad text
Number validation Use isNaN and Number.is* checks
Number conversion Choose Number, parseInt or parseFloat for the task
DOM selection Read elements by id, tag name and CSS selectors
DOM collections Work with HTMLCollection / NodeList results
Browser input Connect an HTML input and button to a JavaScript function
Next step
Use these methods inside conditions, loops and small DOM-driven applications.

<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">

<head>
    <meta charset="UTF-8">
    <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
    <title>Document</title>
    <style>
        #main {
            width: 400px;
            height: 400px;
            background-color: grey;
            margin: 50px auto;
        }

        #hey {
            background-color: rgb(179, 122, 122);
        }
    </style>
</head>

<body>
    <!-- <div id="main">
    </div> -->
    <button id="btn" onclick="change()">Click to change</button>
    <img src="https://encrypted-tbn0.gstatic.com/images?q=tbn:ANd9GcQ4OAugpFArNot9ly2idC95xS6nXIYRISzIJYtTdoJDPw&s=10"
        alt="" id="img" width="200px" height="200px">
    <button id="reverse" onclick="reverse()">Reverse</button>

    <script>
        // let sDiv = document.getElementById('main')
        // sDiv.innerHTML = "<div id=hey> Hey </div>"
        // sDiv.style.color = "cyan"
        // sDiv.style.borderLeft = "5px solid cyan"
        // sDiv.style.borderRight = "5px solid purple"
        // sDiv.style.borderBottom = "5px solid beige"
        // sDiv.style.borderTop = "5px solid lightblue"

        // let innerDiv = document.getElementById('hey')
        // innerDiv.style.backgroundColor = "purple"
        // innerDiv.style.fontSize = "35px"

        //getAttribute

        //setAttribute

        function change() {
            let image = document.getElementById('img')
            image.style.transition = "1s linear"
            image.setAttribute('src', 'https://media.istockphoto.com/id/1171712711/photo/supernova-explosion-in-space.jpg?s=612x612&w=0&k=20&c=7fwusg4bEZ47MWHKTudqAr6DZu3DhOfkowlPrl6d39E=')
        }

        function reverse() {
            let image = document.getElementById('img')
            image.style.transition = "1s linear"
            image.setAttribute('src', 'https://encrypted-tbn0.gstatic.com/images?q=tbn:ANd9GcQ4OAugpFArNot9ly2idC95xS6nXIYRISzIJYtTdoJDPw&s=10')
        }

        // Array Methods

        li = [11, 22, 33, 44, 55, 66]

        li.push(345, 333)
        console.log("Added two values using push()", li)

        li.pop()
        console.log("Deleted last value using pop()", li)

        li.shift()
        console.log("Removed first value using shift()", li)

        li.unshift()
        console.log("Get the first removed value using unshift()", li)

        li.unshift()
        console.log("To replace the first", li)


        // SPLICE 

        // add
        li.splice(1, 0, 888)
        console.log("Added using splice: ", li)

        // delete
        li.splice(1, 4)
        console.log("delete using splice: ", li)

        // replace
        li.splice(1, 1, 345)
        console.log("Replaced using splice: ", li)


        // JOIN method

        const str = li.join('_')
        console.log("Join using _ => ", str)


        // Reverse()

        const rev = li.reverse()
        console.log("Reverse => ", rev)

        // sort()

        const sortArray = ["1", '2', '3', '4', '5', '6']

        // Q) access the second last element from the array

        console.log(li[li.length - 2])



        // For each method use different array list
    </script>
</body>

</html>

04
Session 5 Roadmap
How DOM updates and arrays connect
JavaScript Session 5
|
+-- DOM interaction
|   +-- select element
|   +-- change style / HTML
|   +-- read or set attributes
|   +-- update image on click
|
+-- Array manipulation
    +-- add -> remove -> insert -> replace
    +-- join -> reverse -> sort
    +-- access by index -> process with callbacks
CORE IDEA
The DOM part changes browser state. The array part changes or analyzes collections of values. Both rely on
selecting data, performing an operation, and inspecting the result.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 4 of 50
Return to Index


05
DOM Element Selection
Start by selecting the element you want to change
Most DOM updates begin with selecting an element from the document. The source uses
getElementById() to find the image before changing its style and src attribute.
const image = document.getElementById("img");
console.log(image);
console.log(image.src);
MENTAL MODEL
document -> find element -> receive Element object -> read or modify its properties.
SOURCE CONNECTION
The supplied HTML gives the image the id="img", which is then selected from JavaScript.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 5 of 50
Return to Index


06
innerHTML
Insert or replace HTML inside an element
The commented source demonstrates innerHTML as a way to place HTML markup inside the
#main element.
const sDiv = document.getElementById("main");
sDiv.innerHTML = "<div id=hey>Hey</div>";
WHAT IT DOES
innerHTML parses the supplied string as HTML and replaces the element's current HTML contents with the
parsed markup.
sDiv.innerHTML = "<div id=hey>Hey</div>";
// Result inside #main:
// <div id="hey">Hey</div>
CAUTION
Use innerHTML carefully when inserting untrusted text because HTML parsing can create injection risks. For plain
text, textContent is safer.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 6 of 50
Return to Index


07
style Property
Change CSS directly from JavaScript
Every HTML element exposes a style object that lets JavaScript set inline CSS properties.
const sDiv = document.getElementById("main");
sDiv.style.color = "cyan";
sDiv.style.borderLeft = "5px solid cyan";
sDiv.style.borderRight = "5px solid purple";
sDiv.style.borderBottom = "5px solid beige";
sDiv.style.borderTop = "5px solid lightblue";
CSS PROPERTY NAMING
JavaScript style properties use camelCase: backgroundColor, fontSize, borderLeft, marginTop and so on.
SOURCE CONNECTION
The source also uses innerDiv.style.backgroundColor and innerDiv.style.fontSize on the nested #hey element.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

Return to Index


08
style.backgroundColor + fontSize
Update specific CSS properties
The source shows styling a nested element after inserting it into the DOM.
const innerDiv = document.getElementById("hey");
innerDiv.style.backgroundColor = "purple";
innerDiv.style.fontSize = "35px";
EXPECTED EFFECT
The #hey element receives a purple background and a larger 35px font size.
IMPORTANT
Direct style changes create inline styles. For reusable designs, CSS classes are usually easier to maintain;
classList is the natural next step.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

09
setAttribute()
Create or change an HTML attribute
setAttribute(name, value) assigns an attribute on an element. It is the method used by the
source to swap the image src.
const image = document.getElementById("img");
image.setAttribute("src", "new-image-url");
SOURCE CONNECTION
The change() and reverse() functions both call setAttribute("src", ...), which replaces the image resource.
RULE
Attribute values are strings. The method can set existing attributes or create an attribute that was not previously
present.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 9 of 50
Return to Index


10
getAttribute()
Read an attribute value
getAttribute(name) returns the current string value of an element attribute, or null when the
attribute is absent.
const image = document.getElementById("img");
console.log(image.getAttribute("src"));
console.log(image.getAttribute("alt"));
WHY IT MATTERS
Use getAttribute() when you specifically need the attribute value as written on the element.
SOURCE NOTE
The source lists getAttribute and setAttribute as concepts, but only setAttribute is executed. This page documents
the missing read operation.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

Return to Index


11
hasAttribute() + removeAttribute()
Check or remove an attribute
These are natural companion methods to getAttribute() and setAttribute().
const image = document.getElementById("img");
console.log(image.hasAttribute("src"));
image.removeAttribute("alt");
console.log(image.hasAttribute("alt"));
RESULT
hasAttribute() returns true or false. removeAttribute() removes the named attribute from the element.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

12
Image Attribute Switching
Change the image source dynamically
The session uses a button to replace the image source with another URL. The same idea can
be reused for galleries, theme images and status indicators.
function change() {
    const image = document.getElementById("img");
    image.style.transition = "1s linear";
    image.setAttribute("src", "new-image-url");
}
 
function reverse() {
    const image = document.getElementById("img");
    image.style.transition = "1s linear";
    image.setAttribute("src", "original-image-url");
}
FLOW
Click button -> run function -> select image -> set transition -> replace src -> browser loads the new image.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

onclick Event Handler
Connect a button click to JavaScript
The supplied HTML uses inline onclick attributes to call change() and reverse().
<button id="btn" onclick="change()">Click to change</button>
<img id="img" ...>
<button id="reverse" onclick="reverse()">Reverse</button>
SOURCE CONNECTION
onclick="change()" calls the change function when the user clicks the button. The reverse button does the same
for reverse().
EXTENSION
A later style of event handling is addEventListener("click", handler), which keeps JavaScript behavior separate
from HTML markup.

Array Fundamentals
Arrays as mutable ordered collections
The source creates a list of numbers and then applies multiple methods to it. Array indexes
start at 0, and most mutating methods change the original array.
const li = [11, 22, 33, 44, 55, 66];
console.log(li[0]); // 11
console.log(li.length); // 6
Category
Methods in Session 5
Add
push(), unshift()
Remove
pop(), shift()
Insert / replace
splice()
Convert / rearrange
join(), reverse(), sort()
Access / iteration
indexing, length, forEach()
SOURCE CORRECTION
The supplied code uses `li = [...]` without const/let/var. Use `const li = [...]` or `let li = [...]` so the variable is
declared explicitly.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

Return to Index


15
push()
Array methods
Adds one or more elements to the end of an array. It returns the new array length.
WHAT IT DOES
Use push() when new values should be appended to the end. It mutates the original array.
const li = [11,22,33,44,55,66];
console.log(li.push(345,333));
console.log(li);
EXPECTED RESULT
8 [11,22,33,44,55,66,345,333]
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 15 of 50
Return to Index


16
pop()
Array methods
Removes the last element from an array and returns the removed value.
WHAT IT DOES
pop() is useful when you need both the updated array and the removed item.
const li = [11,22,33,44,55,66];
console.log(li.pop());
console.log(li);
EXPECTED RESULT
66 [11,22,33,44,55]
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 16 of 50
Return to Index


17
shift()
Array methods
Removes the first element from an array and returns that removed value.
WHAT IT DOES
shift() is the front-removal partner of pop().
const li = [11,22,33,44,55,66];
const removed = li.shift();
console.log(removed);
console.log(li);
EXPECTED RESULT
11 [22,33,44,55,66]
SOURCE / COMMON PITFALL
The source comment says "Get the first removed value using unshift()". That is not correct: shift() returns the
removed first value; unshift() adds values to the front.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 17 of 50
Return to Index


18
unshift()
Array methods
Adds one or more elements to the beginning of an array and returns the new array length.
WHAT IT DOES
unshift() adds values. With no arguments, it makes no content change and simply returns the current length.
const li = [22,33,44];
console.log(li.unshift(11));
console.log(li);
EXPECTED RESULT
4 [11,22,33,44]
SOURCE / COMMON PITFALL
The supplied source calls li.unshift() with no values while describing replacement/getting removed values. That
description is incorrect.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 18 of 50
Return to Index


19
shift() vs unshift()
Front-of-array operations
Method
Action
Returns
shift()
Remove first element
Removed element
unshift(value)
Add at beginning
New array length
const li = [22, 33, 44];
const removed = li.shift();
li.unshift(11);
console.log(removed); // 22
console.log(li);      // [11,33,44]
MEMORY TRICK
shift = take away from the front. unshift = put onto the front.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 19 of 50
Return to Index


20
splice()
Insert, delete and replace in one method
splice(start, deleteCount, ...items) changes the original array by removing, inserting or
replacing elements.
li.splice(start, deleteCount, item1, item2, ...);
Goal
Pattern
Insert
splice(index, 0, value)
Delete
splice(index, count)
Replace
splice(index, count, value)
IMPORTANT
splice() mutates the original array and returns an array containing the removed elements.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 20 of 50
Return to Index


21
splice() - Add
Array methods
Insert a value without deleting existing elements by using deleteCount = 0.
WHAT IT DOES
The inserted item appears at index 1 and the existing items shift to the right.
const li = [22,33,44,55];
li.splice(1,0,888);
console.log(li);
EXPECTED RESULT
[22,888,33,44,55]
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 21 of 50
Return to Index


22
splice() - Delete
Array methods
Delete one or more elements by giving a start index and deleteCount.
WHAT IT DOES
deleteCount tells JavaScript how many elements to remove starting from the chosen index.
const li = [22,888,33,44,55];
const removed = li.splice(1,4);
console.log(removed);
console.log(li);
EXPECTED RESULT
[888,33,44,55] [22]
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

Return to Index


23
splice() - Replace
Array methods
Replace existing elements by deleting them and supplying replacement values in the same
call.
WHAT IT DOES
Replace = start at an index -> remove count -> insert replacement values.
const li = [22,66,345];
li.splice(1,1,345);
console.log(li);
EXPECTED RESULT
[22,345,345]
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

24
join()
Convert array values into one string
join(separator) combines array elements into a string. The separator is inserted between each
element.
const li = [22,345,345];
const str = li.join("_");
console.log(str);
EXPECTED RESULT
22_345_345
NOTE
join() does not mutate the array. It returns a new string value.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 24 of 50
Return to Index


25
reverse()
Reverse array order
reverse() reverses the elements of the array in place and returns the same array object.
const li = [22,345,345];
const rev = li.reverse();
console.log(rev);
console.log(li);
EXPECTED RESULT
[345,345,22] [345,345,22]
IMPORTANT
reverse() mutates the original array. If the original order must be preserved, copy first, for example `const copy =
[...li]`.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 25 of 50
Return to Index


26
sort()
Sort array elements
sort() sorts an array in place. By default, JavaScript compares values as strings, so numeric
arrays need a comparator for numeric ascending order.
const sortArray = [10, 2, 30, 4];
console.log(sortArray.sort());
console.log(sortArray.sort((a, b) => a - b));
EXPECTED RESULT
Default: [10, 2, 30, 4] can behave lexicographically. Numeric comparator: [2, 4, 10, 30]
SOURCE CONNECTION
The supplied source creates a string array named sortArray but does not call sort(). This page completes the
method concept and shows the important numeric-sort rule.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 26 of 50
Return to Index


27
Array Indexing + Second-Last Element
Access values without a method call
The source asks how to access the second-last element. Use length - 2 as the index because
the final index is length - 1.
const li = [345,345,22];
console.log(li[li.length - 2]);
EXPECTED RESULT
345
Index of last item      = li.length - 1
Index of second-last   = li.length - 2
REMEMBER
Indexing starts at 0, so length and last index are always different numbers.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 27 of 50
Return to Index

slice() + concat()
Useful non-mutating array companions
Method
Purpose
Mutates?
slice(start,end)
Copies a portion of an array
No
concat(values)
Combines arrays/values into a new array
No
const li = [11,22,33,44];
console.log(li.slice(1,3));
console.log(li.concat([55,66]));
WHY ADD THESE
They are natural non-mutating counterparts to splice() and are useful when you want to read or combine values
without changing the original array.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 28 of 50
Return to Index


29
includes(), indexOf(), lastIndexOf()
Find values in arrays
These methods answer whether a value exists and, when needed, where it occurs.
const li = [11,22,33,22];
console.log(li.includes(22));
console.log(li.indexOf(22));
console.log(li.lastIndexOf(22));
EXPECTED RESULT
true 1 3
PATTERN
includes() -> yes/no. indexOf() -> first index. lastIndexOf() -> last index.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 29 of 50
Return to Index


30
forEach()
Run code for every array element
forEach(callback) visits each element in order and runs the callback once for each existing
element. It is primarily used for side effects such as logging or updating the DOM.
const values = [10,20,30];
values.forEach((value, index) => {
    console.log(index, value);
});
CALLBACK DATA
The callback commonly receives (value, index, array).
IMPORTANT
forEach() does not build a new transformed array. Use map() when the goal is to create a new array of
transformed values.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 30 of 50
Return to Index


31
map()
Create a transformed array
map(callback) returns a new array containing the callback result for each element.
const values = [10,20,30];
const doubled = values.map(value => value * 2);
console.log(doubled);
EXPECTED RESULT
[20,40,60]
RULE
map() is for transformation: one input element -> one output element.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 31 of 50
Return to Index


32
filter()
Keep only elements that pass a condition
filter(callback) returns a new array containing only the elements for which the callback returns
true.
const values = [10,15,20,25];
const even = values.filter(value => value % 2 === 0);
console.log(even);
EXPECTED RESULT
[10,20]
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes
Page 32 of 50
Return to Index


33
find() + findIndex()
Find the first matching element
Method
Returns
find(predicate)
First matching element, or undefined
findIndex(predicate)
Index of first match, or -1
const values = [10,15,20,25];
console.log(values.find(v => v > 18));
console.log(values.findIndex(v => v > 18));
EXPECTED RESULT
20 2
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

34
some() + every()
Test array conditions
Method
Question
some(predicate)
Does at least one element pass?
every(predicate)
Do all elements pass?
const values = [2,4,6];
console.log(values.some(v => v > 5));
console.log(values.every(v => v % 2 === 0));
EXPECTED RESULT
true true
CONNECTION TO SESSION 2
These methods return Boolean results and fit naturally with the logical/comparison concepts from Session 2.

Answer Key 11-20
Suggested solutions
11. arr.push(10, 20, 30);
12. const removed = arr.pop();
13. const first = arr.shift();
14. arr.unshift(10, 20);
15. arr.splice(2, 0, value);
16. arr.splice(1, 2);

19. arr.reverse(); console.log(arr[arr.length - 2]);
20. arr.sort((a, b) => a - b);
MEMORY RULE
push/pop work at the end; shift/unshift work at the beginning; splice handles index-based edits; join returns a
string; reverse and sort mutate the array.

Mini Project - Array Manager
Practice the complete array method family
Create a small array manager that lets you add, remove, insert, replace, join and sort values.
const values = [11,22,33,44,55];
// add -> push()
// remove end -> pop()
// remove front -> shift()
// add front -> unshift()
// insert / delete / replace -> splice()
// string -> join()
// reverse -> reverse()
// numeric order -> sort((a,b) => a-b)
EXTENSION
Display the array in the DOM after every operation instead of only using console.log().

43
Interview Revision
Fast answers for Session 5
Question
Answer
What does push() return?
The new array length.
What does pop() return?
The removed last element.
shift() vs unshift()?
Remove first vs add at beginning.
What does splice() do?
Insert, delete and replace elements at a chosen index.
Does join() mutate the array?
No, it returns a string.
Does reverse() mutate?
Yes. It reverses the original array.
Why use a comparator with sort()?
Default sort is lexicographic; a-b gives numeric ascending order.
setAttribute()?
Sets or replaces an attribute value.
getAttribute()?
Reads an attribute value.
innerHTML?
Parses HTML and replaces an element's contents.
onclick?
Runs a handler when the element is clicked.
INTERVIEW TIP
Define the method first, then state whether it mutates the array or returns a new value. That distinction is often
more useful than memorizing examples.
JavaScript - Session 5 Notes

Return to Index

Quick Revision Sheet
Session 5 at a glance
DOM
getElementById -> select one element by id
innerHTML -> replace/insert HTML content
style.* -> set inline CSS properties
setAttribute -> set/change attribute
getAttribute -> read attribute
hasAttribute -> test attribute existence
removeAttribute -> remove attribute
onclick -> run function after click
 
ARRAYS
push -> add end
pop -> remove end
shift -> remove front
unshift -> add front
splice -> insert/delete/replace
join -> array to string
reverse -> reverse in place
sort -> sort in place
slice / concat -> non-mutating copies/combinations
forEach -> process each item
map -> transform
filter -> keep matches
find / findIndex -> first match
some / every -> Boolean tests
MEMORY MAP
DOM -> select -> read/change. Arrays -> add/remove/edit -> rearrange -> process.

Source Corrections & Clarifications
Important notes from the supplied code
Source item
Clarification
li = [..]
Declare the variable with const or let.
li.unshift()
With no arguments, it does not change the array; it returns the current length.
"Get the first removed value using
unshift()"
The removed first value comes from shift(), not unshift().
"To replace the first" + unshift()
unshift() does not replace; splice() or direct index assignment can replace.
sortArray created but not sorted
A sort() call was missing; numeric sorting needs (a,b) => a-b.
reverse()
Mutates the original array.
setAttribute(src)
Changes the image resource if the URL is valid.
WHY THIS PAGE EXISTS
The source contains useful experiments and comments, but several comments do not match the actual behavior
of the method. This page keeps the original intent while making the final study notes technically usable.

clicks, and works with arrays using common methods.

simple language.

Session 5 Roadmap
How the ideas connect

↓
select an element

HTML / CSS

change what it shows

Events

↓
Arrays
↓

Methods

Core idea
The DOM part changes browser state. The array part changes or analyzes collections of values. In both
cases, think: select → operate → inspect the result.
Visual roadmap
The supplied flowchart organizes the session into DOM interaction, array mutation, splice editing, conversion/
reversal/access, and practice. These notes follow that learning path while using fresh examples.

JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

DOM Element Selection
Start by selecting the element you want to change
Most browser updates begin with selecting an element. The supplied page gives the image
id="img", so JavaScript can find it with getElementById().
Mental model
document → find element → receive an Element object → read or modify its properties.
Remember
Choose a selector that matches the HTML. An id is intended to identify one element.
04
const image = document.getElementById("img");
console.log(image);
console.log(image.src);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

innerHTML
Insert or replace HTML inside an element
What happens
The supplied string is parsed as HTML, so a new child div is created inside #main.
Safer text
For plain text, textContent is usually better. Avoid inserting untrusted user input through innerHTML because
it parses HTML.
05
const box = document.getElementById("main");
box.innerHTML = "<div id="hey">Hey</div>";
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

style Property
Change CSS directly from JavaScript
CSS JavaScript style name
background-color backgroundColor
font-size fontSize
border-left borderLeft
margin-top marginTop
Memory trick
CSS names usually become camelCase when used through element.style.
06
const box = document.getElementById("main");
box.style.color = "cyan";
box.style.borderLeft = "5px solid cyan";
box.style.borderRight = "5px solid purple";
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

style.backgroundColor + fontSize
Update specific CSS properties
Read it
backgroundColor changes the background. fontSize changes the text size. Both are inline styles.
Better for larger UIs
When a design has reusable states, CSS classes and classList are usually easier to maintain than many inline
styles.
07
const title = document.getElementById("hey");
title.style.backgroundColor = "purple";
title.style.fontSize = "35px";
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

setAttribute()
Create or change an HTML attribute
Simple rule
setAttribute(name, value) writes the attribute. If the attribute exists, its value changes; if it does not exist, the
attribute is created.
Source connection
The supplied change() and reverse() functions use setAttribute("src", ...).
08
const image = document.getElementById("img");
image.setAttribute("src", "new-image.jpg");
image.setAttribute("alt", "A space image");
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

getAttribute()
Read an attribute value
Return value
You get the attribute value as a string. If that attribute is missing, the result is null.
Think
setAttribute = write. getAttribute = read.
09
const image = document.getElementById("img");
console.log(image.getAttribute("src"));
console.log(image.getAttribute("alt"));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

hasAttribute() + removeAttribute()
Check or remove an attribute
What happens
hasAttribute() returns true or false. removeAttribute() removes the named attribute.
Useful pattern
Check first when your UI logic depends on whether an attribute exists.
10
const image = document.getElementById("img");
console.log(image.hasAttribute("alt"));
image.removeAttribute("alt");
console.log(image.hasAttribute("alt"));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Image Attribute Switching
Change an image when the user clicks a button
click button
↓
run function
↓
select image
↓
change src
↓
browser loads new resource
Important
Changing src swaps the image resource. A CSS transition does not automatically animate a src swap itself.
11
function changeImage() {
  const image = document.getElementById("img");
  image.style.transition = "1s linear";
  image.setAttribute("src", "supernova.jpg");
}
function restoreImage() {
  const image = document.getElementById("img");
  image.setAttribute("src", "original.jpg");
}
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

onclick + addEventListener()
Connect user clicks to JavaScript
Source style
The supplied HTML uses inline onclick attributes. That is useful for learning the event flow.
Modern style
addEventListener("click", handler) keeps JavaScript behavior in JavaScript and allows multiple listeners.
Memory rule
onclick is the event-handler attribute name. With addEventListener(), use "click" without the on prefix.
12
<button id="btn" onclick="changeImage()">Change</button>
const btn = document.getElementById("btn");
btn.addEventListener("click", changeImage);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Array Fundamentals
Arrays are ordered, mutable collections
Group Methods in this session
Add / remove ends push, pop, shift, unshift
Edit by index splice
Convert / rearrange join, reverse, sort
Read / search length, indexing, includes, indexOf, lastIndexOf
Process forEach, map, filter, find, findIndex, some, every
Source correction
The supplied code writes li = [...] without let / const / var. Declare the variable explicitly.
13
const values = [11, 22, 33, 44, 55, 66];
console.log(values[0]);
console.log(values.length);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

push()
Add items at the end
Result
newLength is 5 and values becomes [11,22,33,44,55].
Return value
push() returns the new array length, not the array. It mutates the original array.
14
const values = [11, 22, 33];
const newLength = values.push(44, 55);
console.log(newLength);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

pop()
Remove the last item
Result
removed is 44. values becomes [11,22,33].
Return value
pop() returns the removed last element.
15
const values = [11, 22, 33, 44];
const removed = values.pop();
console.log(removed);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

shift()
Remove the first item
Result
first is 11. values becomes [22,33,44].
Source correction
The removed first value comes from shift(), not unshift().
16
const values = [11, 22, 33, 44];
const first = values.shift();
console.log(first);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

unshift()
Add items at the beginning
Result
newLength is 4. values becomes [11,22,33,44].
Important
Calling unshift() with no arguments does not add or replace anything. It simply returns the current length.
17
const values = [22, 33, 44];
const newLength = values.unshift(11);
console.log(newLength);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

shift() vs unshift()
The two front-of-array methods
Method Action Returns
shift() Remove first element Removed element
unshift(value) Add at beginning New array length
Memory trick
shift = take away from the front. unshift = put onto the front.
18
const values = [22, 33, 44];
const removed = values.shift();
values.unshift(11);
console.log(removed);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

splice()
The all-purpose array editor
Goal Pattern
Insert splice(index, 0, value)
Delete splice(index, count)
Replace splice(index, count, replacement)
Important
splice() mutates the original array and returns an array containing removed elements.
19
array.splice(start, deleteCount, item1, item2);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

splice() - Add
Insert without deleting
Result
[22, 888, 33, 44, 55]
How it works
Start at index 1. Delete zero items. Insert 888. Existing values move right.
20
const values = [22, 33, 44, 55];
values.splice(1, 0, 888);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

splice() - Delete
Remove a chosen number of items
Result
removed = [888,33,44,55] and values = [22].
Think
start + count = which items are removed.
21
const values = [22, 888, 33, 44, 55];
const removed = values.splice(1, 4);
console.log(removed);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

splice() - Replace
Delete and insert in one step
Result
[22, 999, 345]
Think
Start at index 1 → remove one item → put 999 in that position.
22
const values = [22, 66, 345];
values.splice(1, 1, 999);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

join()
Convert an array into one string
Result
22_345_345
Mutation?
No. join() returns a string and does not change the array.
23
const values = [22, 345, 345];
const text = values.join("_");
console.log(text);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

reverse()
Reverse the order in place
Result
Both result and values now show the reversed order because reverse() mutates the original array.
Safer copy
If the original order matters, copy first: const copy = [...values].
24
const values = [22, 345, 345];
const result = values.reverse();
console.log(result);
console.log(values);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

sort()
Sort arrays correctly
Why the comparator?
Default sort compares values as strings. For ascending numbers, return a - b.
Source clarification
The supplied file creates sortArray but never calls sort(). The important numeric rule is still worth learning.
25
const nums = [10, 2, 30, 4];
nums.sort();
console.log(nums);
nums.sort((a, b) => a - b);
console.log(nums);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Array Indexing + Second-Last
Use length arithmetic
length = 3
↓
last index = 3 - 1 = 2
↓
second-last = 3 - 2 = 1
↓
values[1] = 345
Memory rule
Last index = length - 1. Second-last index = length - 2.
26
const values = [345, 345, 22];
console.log(values[values.length - 2]);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

slice() + concat()
Non-mutating array companions
Method Purpose Mutates?
slice(start,end) Copy a portion of an array No
concat(values) Create a combined array No
Why learn these?
Use them when you want to read or combine values without changing the original array.
27
const values = [11,22,33,44];
console.log(values.slice(1, 3));
console.log(values.concat([55,66]));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

includes() / indexOf() / lastIndexOf()
Find values in an array
Method Meaning
includes() Does the value exist?
indexOf() First matching index
lastIndexOf() Last matching index
Output
true, 1, 3
28
const values = [11, 22, 33, 22];
console.log(values.includes(22));
console.log(values.indexOf(22));
console.log(values.lastIndexOf(22));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

forEach()
Do something for every element
Callback data
The callback commonly receives value, index and the full array.
Important
forEach() is for side effects. It does not build the transformed array for you. Use map() for transformation.
29
const values = [10, 20, 30];
values.forEach((value, index) => {
  console.log(index, value);
});
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

map()
Create a transformed array
Result
[20, 40, 60]
Rule
One input element → one output element. map() returns a new array.
30
const values = [10, 20, 30];
const doubled = values.map(value => value * 2);
console.log(doubled);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

filter()
Keep only values that pass a test
Result
[10, 20]
Rule
The callback should return true for items you want to keep.
31
const values = [10, 15, 20, 25];
const even = values.filter(value => value % 2 === 0);
console.log(even);
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

find() + findIndex()
Find the first match
Method Returns
find(predicate) First matching value or undefined
findIndex(predicate) Index of first match or -1
Output
20 and 2
32
const values = [10, 15, 20, 25];
console.log(values.find(v => v > 18));
console.log(values.findIndex(v => v > 18));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

some() + every()
Ask Boolean questions about an array
Method Question
some() Does at least one element pass?
every() Do all elements pass?
Connection to earlier sessions
These return Boolean results and work naturally with comparison and logical operators.
33
const values = [2, 4, 6];
console.log(values.some(v => v > 5));
console.log(values.every(v => v % 2 === 0));
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Modern Non-Mutating Array Options
Useful when you want immutable-style code
Simple idea
toSorted() and toReversed() return new arrays, so the original nums stays unchanged.
Why add this page?
The supplied session focuses on sort() and reverse(). Current JavaScript also provides non-mutating
alternatives for the same jobs.
34
const nums = [10, 2, 30, 4];
const sorted = nums.toSorted((a, b) => a - b);
const reversed = nums.toReversed();
console.log(nums);
console.log(sorted);
console.log(reversed);

Source Execution Walkthrough
Track the supplied array step by step
Step Operation Array after the operation
Start const li = [11,22,33,44,55,66] [11,22,33,44,55,66]
1 push(345,333) [11,22,33,44,55,66,345,333]
2 pop() [11,22,33,44,55,66,345]
3 shift() [22,33,44,55,66,345]
4 unshift() with no args unchanged
5 unshift() with no args again unchanged
6 splice(1,0,888) [22,888,33,44,55,66,345]
7 splice(1,4) [22,66,345]
8 splice(1,1,345) [22,345,345]
9 join("_") "22_345_345"
10 reverse() [345,345,22]
Key correction
The two unshift() calls do not add or replace anything because no values were supplied. The original source
comments around those lines are misleading.

DOM + Array Connection
A practical browser pattern
HTML list
↓
querySelectorAll
↓
DOM collection
↓
Array.from
↓
map()
↓
new array
Why it matters
DOM selection can produce collections. Converting them to arrays lets you use familiar array processing
methods.
36
const items = document.querySelectorAll("li");
const names = Array.from(items).map(item => item.textContent);
console.log(names);

Suggested solutions
Memory rule
push/pop work at the end. shift/unshift work at the beginning. splice handles index-based editing. join
returns a string. reverse and sort mutate the original array.

Mini Project - Array Manager
Practice the complete array family
Extension

42
const values = [11, 22, 33, 44, 55];
// add end -> push()
// remove end -> pop()
// remove front -> shift()
// add front -> unshift()
// insert / delete / replace -> splice()
// array -> string -> join()
// reverse order -> reverse()
// numeric order -> sort((a,b) => a-b)
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Interview Revision
Fast answers for Session 5
Question Answer
What does push() return? The new array length.
What does pop() return? The removed last element.
shift() vs unshift()? Remove first vs add at beginning.
What does splice() do? Insert, delete or replace values at an index.
Does join() mutate? No. It returns a string.
Does reverse() mutate? Yes. It reverses the original array.
Why use a comparator with sort()? Default sorting is lexicographic; a-b gives numeric ascending order.
setAttribute()? Sets or replaces an attribute.
getAttribute()? Reads an attribute value.
innerHTML? Parses HTML and replaces an element’s contents.
onclick? Runs a handler when the element is clicked.
forEach() vs map()? forEach performs work; map creates a new transformed array.
filter()? Creates a new array containing items that pass a test.
find() vs findIndex()? First matching value vs first matching index.
43
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Quick Revision Sheet
Session 5 at a glance
Memory map
DOM → select → read/change. Arrays → add/remove/edit → rearrange → process.
44
DOM
getElementById -> select one element by id
innerHTML -> insert / replace HTML
style.* -> set inline CSS properties
setAttribute -> set / change attribute
getAttribute -> read attribute
hasAttribute -> test attribute existence
removeAttribute -> remove attribute
onclick -> run handler after click
addEventListener -> modern event wiring
ARRAYS
push -> add end
pop -> remove end
shift -> remove front
unshift -> add front
splice -> insert / delete / replace
join -> array to string
reverse -> reverse in place
sort -> sort in place
slice / concat -> new arrays
forEach -> process each item
map -> transform
filter -> keep matches
find / findIndex -> first match
some / every -> Boolean tests

Session 5 Summary
What you should be able to do now
Skill Expected ability
DOM selection Select elements and work with element objects.
HTML content Insert or replace markup with innerHTML.
Inline styling Set CSS properties through element.style.
Attributes Read, set, check and remove attributes.
Events Connect clicks to JavaScript functions.
Array mutation Use push, pop, shift, unshift and splice.
Array conversion Use join to create a string.
Array rearrangement Reverse and numerically sort values.
Array access Use length and index arithmetic.
Array processing Use forEach, map, filter, find and condition tests.
Next step
Combine these tools with loops, reusable functions, event listeners and DOM rendering to build interactive
browser mini-projects.

Source Corrections & Clarifications
Important notes from the supplied code
Source item Clarification
li = [..] Declare the variable with const or let.
li.unshift() with no arguments It does not change the content; it returns the current length.
"Get the first removed value using unshift()" The removed first value comes from shift(), not unshift().
"To replace the first" + unshift() unshift adds at the beginning. It does not replace an existing item.
sortArray created but not sorted A sort call was missing; numeric sorting needs (a,b) => a-b.
reverse() Mutates the original array.
setAttribute(src) Changes the image resource when the URL is valid.
onclick Works for learning; addEventListener is cleaner for larger applications.
Why this page exists
The supplied material is useful practice, but a few comments do not match the actual behavior of the
method. These notes keep the original intent while making the examples technically usable.

Clean Source HTML - Part 2
The DOM interaction pattern
Flow
Click → event listener → select image → set src → browser updates the image.
48
const image = document.getElementById("img");
const changeBtn = document.getElementById("btn");
const reverseBtn = document.getElementById("reverse");
changeBtn.addEventListener("click", () => {
  image.setAttribute("src", "supernova.jpg");
});
reverseBtn.addEventListener("click", () => {
  image.setAttribute("src", "original.jpg");
});
JavaScript - Session 5 Handwritten Notes Return to Index

Clean Source HTML - Part 3
The array method pattern
Study note
For learning, use a fresh array for each practice problem. Chaining many mutating methods on one array is
good for tracing, but not ideal for beginners.
49
const values = [11, 22, 33, 44, 55, 66];
values.push(77);
const last = values.pop();
const first = values.shift();
values.unshift(10);
values.splice(1, 0, 88);
const text = values.join("_");
values.reverse();
values.sort((a, b) => a - b);
console.log(values, last, first, text);
